Pinned Jordan Decomposition of Characters and Depth-Zero Hecke Algebras Thanks: The second named author was partially supported by SERB Core Research Grant (CRG/2022/000415) and ANRF MATRICS Grant ANRF/ARGM/2025/000365/MTR.
Prashant Arote, Manish Mishra

Abstract
. We construct a pinned canonical Jordan decomposition of characters for finite reductive groups in situations where the dual centralizers may be disconnected. For a connected reductive group 𝐆 \mathbf{G} over a finite field, equipped with a pinning, and for a semisimple element s ∈ G ∗ s\in G^{*} , we construct a uniquely determined bijection 𝕁 s : ℰ ⁡ ( G , s ) → ∼ Uch ⁡ ( C 𝐆 ∗ ​ ( s ) F ∗ ) . \mathbb{J}_{s}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathcal{E}(G,s)\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname{Uch}\bigl(C_{\mathbf{G}^{*}}(s)^{F^{*}}\bigr). This refines Lusztig’s orbit-valued Jordan decomposition for groups with disconnected centre, and is characterized by compatibility with the Deligne–Lusztig character formula and with Harish–Chandra series. We then extend the construction to the class of possibly disconnected reductive groups with abelian component group whose rational components admit pinning-preserving representatives. For this class the correct unconditional statement is an enriched disconnected Jordan decomposition: the target is the connected unipotent Jordan datum together with the source Clifford class and the corresponding projective Clifford label. When the transported source Clifford classes agree with the ordinary Clifford classes on the dual-centralizer side, this enriched target specializes to the usual set of unipotent characters of the corresponding disconnected dual centralizer. The main technical input is a pinned-normalized construction of preferred extensions of cuspidal unipotent characters to their inertia groups. The extension is not forced by abstract Clifford theory alone: after the pinning fixes the relevant component action, Lusztig’s preferred-extension convention removes the remaining scalar ambiguity. The construction then uses Clifford theory, relative Weyl group comparison, Malle’s matching, and connected and disconnected forms of Howlett–Lehrer theory to extend the cuspidal Jordan decomposition functorially to all Harish–Chandra series. As an application, we give a pinned canonical form of the finite-field input in Ohara’s comparison of depth-zero Hecke algebra parameters with the unipotent case. More precisely, for a depth-zero Bernstein type ( K x 0 , ρ x 0 ) (K_{x_{0}},\rho_{x_{0}}) , the affine Hecke algebra factor in the AFMO–Morris presentation is canonically identified, after fixing the same pinning, with the affine Hecke algebra factor attached to Ohara’s corresponding unipotent type.



1991 Mathematics Subject Classification
20C33

1.
Introduction

The Jordan decomposition of characters is one of the central organizing
principles in the representation theory of finite reductive groups. Let

\mathbf{G}
be a connected reductive group over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
, with Frobenius
F
, and
write
G=\mathbf{G}^{F}
. If
(\mathbf{G}^{*},F^{*})
is dual to
(\mathbf{G},F)
, then
Deligne–Lusztig theory partitions
\operatorname{Irr}(G)
into Lusztig series

\operatorname{Irr}(G)=\bigsqcup_{(s)}\mathcal{E}(G,s),

where
s
runs over semisimple conjugacy classes in
G^{*}=\mathbf{G}^{*F^{*}}
.
The Jordan decomposition problem asks for a natural parametrization of

\mathcal{E}(G,s)
by unipotent characters of the centralizer

H=C_{\mathbf{G}^{*}}(s)^{F^{*}}.

Thus one seeks bijections

J_{s}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathcal{E}(G,s)\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname{Uch}(H),

compatible with the character formulae coming from Deligne–Lusztig induction.

When
Z(\mathbf{G})
is connected, the centralizer
C_{\mathbf{G}^{*}}(s)
is connected.
In this case Lusztig constructed the Jordan decomposition of characters

[
17
]
, and Digne–Michel proved a uniqueness theorem for a normalized
family of such bijections
[
7
]
. Their normalization includes
compatibility with central quotients, products, and certain induction
properties, and it gives a canonical way to remove the ambiguity present in
Lusztig’s original parametrization.

For groups with disconnected centre the situation is subtler. In this case

C_{\mathbf{G}^{*}}(s)
need not be connected. Lusztig proved in
[
20
]
that
one still has a natural orbit-valued Jordan decomposition

\mathcal{E}(G,s)\longrightarrow\operatorname{Uch}\bigl(C_{\mathbf{G}^{*}}(s)^{\circ F^{*}}\bigr)/\bigl(C_{\mathbf{G}^{*}}(s)^{F^{*}}/C_{\mathbf{G}^{*}}(s)^{\circ F^{*}}\bigr).

The fibres on the
G
-side are orbits under diagonal automorphisms, and the
orbits on the dual side are orbits of the component group of the centralizer.
This result is fundamental, but it does not by itself produce a bijection with
the irreducible unipotent characters of the disconnected finite group

C_{\mathbf{G}^{*}}(s)^{F^{*}}
. Passing from orbit data to actual irreducible
characters requires Clifford-theoretic choices, namely choices of extensions of
unipotent characters from the identity component to their stabilizers.

The canonical endoscopic Jordan decomposition of Lusztig and Yun

[
15
,
16
]
gives a different and more
geometric treatment of the same general problem. Their construction uses
monodromic Hecke categories and character sheaves, and relates a fixed
semisimple block to unipotent objects for a corresponding endoscopic group. The
present paper has a more representation-theoretic and application-driven aim:
we retain the dual-centralizer language used in depth-zero local Langlands
applications, fix a pinning to normalize the connected Jordan decomposition,
and record the necessary Clifford-theoretic data when parahoric finite
reductive quotients are disconnected. Thus the two constructions have
different targets and different normalizations, and the results below should be
viewed as a pinned finite-field package tailored to the depth-zero applications,
not as a substitute for the Lusztig–Yun endoscopic construction.

The purpose of this paper is to make these choices canonical in a precise
pinned-normalized sense. After a pinning has been fixed, the action of the
component group on the based root datum is fixed. The remaining ambiguity is
the familiar Clifford-theoretic scalar ambiguity in extending invariant
characters; it is removed by Lusztig’s preferred-extension convention, and by
Malle’s compatible matching on the cuspidal side. Thus the construction below
involves no further auxiliary choices. We do not mean that the abstract
disconnected group alone forces a unique extension before these normalizations
are specified. With this convention, we construct a pinned Jordan decomposition
of characters which refines Lusztig’s orbit-valued decomposition and is
compatible with Harish–Chandra induction.

Our first main result concerns connected reductive groups, but allows the
dual centralizer
C_{\mathbf{G}^{*}}(s)
to be disconnected.

The connected-group theorem.

Let
\mathbf{G}
be connected reductive over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
, equipped with a pinning

\mathcal{P}
, and let
s\in G^{*}
be semisimple. Put

\mathbf{H}=C_{\mathbf{G}^{*}}(s),\qquad H=\mathbf{H}^{F^{*}}.

Theorem
11.4
constructs a uniquely determined
bijection

\mathbb{J}_{s}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathcal{E}(G,s)\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname{Uch}(H),

depending only on the pinning, such that for every
F^{*}
-stable maximal torus

\mathbf{T}^{*}\subseteq\mathbf{G}^{*}
containing
s
one has

\bigl\langle R_{\mathbf{T}^{*}}^{\mathbf{G}}(s),\rho\bigr\rangle_{G}=\epsilon_{\mathbf{G}}\epsilon_{\mathbf{H}}\bigl\langle R_{\mathbf{T}^{*}}^{\mathbf{H}}(1),\mathbb{J}_{s}(\rho)\bigr\rangle_{H}.

Moreover, this bijection is compatible with Harish–Chandra series. If

(L,\tau)
is a cuspidal pair with
\tau\in\mathcal{E}(L,s)
, and if

H_{L}=C_{\mathbf{L}^{*}}(s)^{F^{*}},\qquad u_{\tau}=\mathbb{J}_{s}^{L}(\tau)\in\operatorname{Uch}(H_{L})_{\operatorname{cusp}},

then
\mathbb{J}_{s}
restricts to a canonical bijection

\operatorname{Irr}(G,(L,\tau))\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname{Irr}(H,(H_{L},u_{\tau})).

Thus the Jordan decomposition is not merely a set-theoretic parametrization of
Lusztig series; it is compatible with the Harish–Chandra decomposition inside
each Lusztig series.

Our second main result extends this construction to a class of disconnected
finite reductive groups, but in an enriched form. Let
\mathbf{G}
be a possibly
disconnected reductive group over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
, fix an
F
-stable pinning
\mathcal{P}
of

\mathbf{G}^{\circ}
, and assume that the finite component group is abelian and
satisfies the rational pinned-component condition of
Hypothesis
12.1
. For

s\in(\mathbf{G}^{*})^{\circ F^{*}}

semisimple, we define a disconnected Lusztig series
\mathcal{E}(G,s)
by restriction
to the identity component. If

H=C_{\mathbf{G}^{*}}(s)^{F^{*}},

then Theorem
13.4
gives a canonical enriched bijection

J^{\mathrm{enh}}_{G,s}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathcal{E}(G,s)\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname{Uch}^{\mathrm{enh}}_{G,s}(H).

On the cuspidal part, an irreducible character
\rho
lying above

\rho^{\circ}\in\mathcal{E}(G^{\circ},s)_{\operatorname{cusp}}
is sent to

\left[\mathbb{J}_{s}^{G^{\circ}}(\rho^{\circ}),[\alpha_{\rho^{\circ}}],E_{\rho}\right],

where
[\alpha_{\rho^{\circ}}]
and
E_{\rho}
are the source Clifford class and
source Clifford label. On a general Harish–Chandra series the construction
preserves the corresponding relative Howlett–Lehrer label. Thus the theorem
no longer requires an unproved equality between source and target Clifford
cocycles; that equality becomes precisely the extra condition under which the
enriched target reduces to the ordinary set
\operatorname{Uch}(H)
.

Although the main application carried out in this paper concerns depth-zero
Hecke algebras, the disconnected counterpart of Jordan decomposition also has a
more direct Langlands-theoretic application. In the second named author’s
construction of a pinned local Langlands correspondence for depth-zero
supercuspidal representations of a connected reductive group over a
non-archimedean local field
F_{\mathrm{loc}}

[
23
]
, the finite representation attached to a
depth-zero type at a vertex
x
lives naturally on the full parahoric quotient

G(F_{\mathrm{loc}})_{x}/G(F_{\mathrm{loc}})_{x,0+},

which is in general a possibly disconnected finite reductive group. The
pinned disconnected Jordan decomposition developed here supplies the finite
unipotent label on the dual-centralizer side. In that construction it is the
finite step which separates the finite representation in a depth-zero type from
the toral part of the parameter and makes the passage to a cuspidal enhanced
unramified parameter canonical. The inverse construction uses the same
finite-field bijection in the opposite direction. Thus the enriched
Clifford-theoretic bookkeeping in Theorem
13.4
is not
merely a technical refinement of Lusztig’s orbit-valued decomposition: it is the
finite mechanism which makes the depth-zero supercuspidal LLC reversible after a
pinned normalization has been fixed.

The third main result is an application to Hecke algebras attached to
depth-zero Bernstein blocks of
p
-adic groups. Let
F_{\mathrm{loc}}
be
a non-archimedean local field with residue field
\mathfrak{f}
, and let

G
be a connected reductive group over
F_{\mathrm{loc}}
. For a depth-zero

\mathfrak{s}
-type
(K,\rho)=(K_{x_{0}},\rho_{x_{0}})
, the Hecke algebra

\mathcal{H}_{\mathfrak{s}}(G)=\mathcal{H}(G(F),(K,\rho))
admits the AFMO–Morris
presentation as an affine Hecke algebra crossed with a finite twisted group
algebra; see
[
2
]
. Ohara’s construction
[
24
]
associates
to the same depth-zero datum a unipotent type
(K_{\theta,x_{0}},u_{x_{0}})
for a
related group
G_{\theta}
. Ohara proves that the affine Hecke algebra factor in

\mathcal{H}_{\mathfrak{s}}(G)
is isomorphic to the affine Hecke algebra factor
appearing in the Hecke algebra of this unipotent type. Theorem
15.4

records the pinned version of this affine-factor comparison. Equivalently, one
may rewrite
\mathcal{H}_{\mathfrak{s}}(G)
as its original source-side twisted group
algebra crossed with the corresponding unipotent affine Hecke factor. We do
not claim a general isomorphism from
\mathcal{H}_{\mathfrak{s}}(G)
to the full Hecke
algebra of the unipotent type, because the finite stabilizer group and its
cocycle in the crossed-product presentation need not agree with the unipotent
ones. The point of the present paper is that the finite-field Jordan
decomposition used in the comparison of the affine parameters is no longer an
auxiliary non-canonical choice: it is the pinned canonical bijection constructed
here.

We now describe the strategy of the proofs.

The construction of Theorem
11.4
begins with the
cuspidal case. Lusztig’s orbit-valued map gives, for each cuspidal

\rho\in\mathcal{E}(G,s)
, an orbit

O\subseteq\operatorname{Uch}(H^{\circ})_{\operatorname{cusp}},\qquad H^{\circ}=(C_{\mathbf{G}^{*}}(s)^{\circ})^{F^{*}}.

For
u^{\circ}\in O
, let
I_{H}(u^{\circ})
be its stabilizer in
H
. Since
the relevant component groups are abelian, Clifford theory says that the
irreducible characters of
H
lying above
O
are obtained from extensions
of
u^{\circ}
to
I_{H}(u^{\circ})
, twisted by characters of

I_{H}(u^{\circ})/H^{\circ}
, and then induced to
H
. The main difficulty is
to choose these extensions canonically.

This is done using Lusztig’s preferred-extension convention for Weyl-group
representations, together with the interpretation of unipotent characters via
generalized Springer theory and character sheaves. We first handle cyclic
component groups, then glue the resulting preferred extensions for abelian
component groups, and finally pass to products and repeated quasi-simple
factors by a wreath-product argument. This gives preferred extensions of
cuspidal unipotent characters to their inertia groups.

On the
G
-side, the fibre of Lusztig’s orbit-valued map over
O
is an
orbit under the diagonal automorphism group

A=G_{\operatorname{ad}}/G.

Lusztig’s Clifford-theoretic construction gives an orthogonality relation
between the stabilizer on the
G
-side and the stabilizer on the unipotent
side. The pinning supplies the missing normalization: using semisimple
characters and Malle’s results on automorphisms of cuspidal characters

[
22
]
, one attaches to each cuspidal
\rho
a canonical class in
the appropriate quotient of
A
. Under the orthogonality isomorphism this
class gives a character of the unipotent stabilizer, and hence selects a
specific irreducible character of
H
above the orbit
O
. This produces
the pinned cuspidal bijection

\mathcal{E}(G,s)_{\operatorname{cusp}}\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname{Uch}(H)_{\operatorname{cusp}}.

The passage from cuspidal characters to all of
\mathcal{E}(G,s)
uses
Harish–Chandra theory. For a cuspidal pair
(L,\tau)
with

\tau\in\mathcal{E}(L,s)
, the cuspidal construction gives a unipotent cuspidal
character

u_{\tau}\in\operatorname{Uch}(C_{\mathbf{L}^{*}}(s)^{F^{*}}).

A key point is that the relative Weyl groups on the two sides agree:

W_{\tau}\cong W_{u_{\tau}}.

This follows from the Weyl-equivariance of the pinned cuspidal correspondence.
Howlett–Lehrer theory identifies the constituents of

R_{\mathbf{L}}^{\mathbf{G}}(\tau)
with
\operatorname{Irr}(W_{\tau})
. On the dual centralizer side,
we use a disconnected version of Howlett–Lehrer theory, in stabilizer form,
to identify the constituents of

R_{H_{L}}^{H}(u_{\tau})
with
\operatorname{Irr}(W_{u_{\tau}})
. The isomorphism

W_{\tau}\cong W_{u_{\tau}}
then gives the desired bijection of Harish–Chandra
series. Taking the disjoint union over all cuspidal pairs gives
Theorem
11.4
.

The proof of Theorem
13.4
follows the same philosophy,
but with an additional Clifford-theoretic layer coming from the disconnected
group
G
itself. In the disconnected part of the paper we impose the
rational pinned-component condition: each rational component has a representative
whose conjugation action preserves the chosen pinning of
G^{\circ}
. Thus the
semidirect-product model determined by the pinning records the actual component
action on characters of the finite identity component, up to inner conjugacy by

G^{\circ}
. For a cuspidal constituent

\rho^{\circ}\in\mathcal{E}(G^{\circ},s)_{\operatorname{cusp}},

the connected-group theorem gives a cuspidal unipotent correspondent

u_{0}\in\operatorname{Uch}(C_{G^{\circ*}}(s)^{F^{*}})_{\operatorname{cusp}}.

The pinned equivariance of the connected correspondence, applied to these
pinning-preserving rational representatives, implies that the stabilizers of

\rho^{\circ}
and
u_{0}
in the component group agree. We do not assert that
the source and target Clifford-label cocycles agree. Instead, the source Clifford
class
[\alpha_{\rho^{\circ}}]
and the corresponding projective label
E_{\rho}

are retained as part of the enriched unipotent datum.

The full disconnected construction is then obtained by Harish–Chandra
induction. Disconnected Lusztig series are shown to be unions of
Harish–Chandra series. For a cuspidal pair
(L,\tau)
, the enriched cuspidal
datum records
\mathbb{J}_{s}^{L^{\circ}}(\tau^{\circ})
, the source Clifford class of

\tau^{\circ}
, and the Clifford label of
\tau
. The disconnected
Howlett–Lehrer/corner analysis then supplies the relative label of each
constituent of
R_{L}^{G}(\tau)
. Combining the enriched cuspidal datum with this
relative label gives the full enriched bijection of
Theorem
13.4
.

We close with a guide to the organization of the paper. The early sections
recall Deligne–Lusztig theory, Lusztig series, the Digne–Michel
normalization, Lusztig’s disconnected-centre construction, and the
Howlett–Lehrer parametrization of Harish–Chandra series. The technical heart
of the paper is the construction of preferred extensions of cuspidal unipotent
characters and the resulting cuspidal pinned Jordan decomposition. The
connected-group theorem is then obtained by combining this cuspidal
construction with relative Weyl group comparison and Howlett–Lehrer theory.
The later sections extend the construction to disconnected reductive groups
with abelian component group satisfying the rational pinned-component condition,
in the enriched sense explained above. Readers mainly interested in the
Hecke-algebra application to
p
-adic groups may read
Theorems
11.4
and
13.4
as the
finite-field input and then proceed directly to
§
15
. For the complementary
Langlands-theoretic application of the disconnected theorem to depth-zero
supercuspidal representations, see
[
23
]
.

2.
Notation and conventions

Let
\mathbb{F}_{q}
be a finite field with
q=p^{r}
elements, and fix a prime

\ell\neq p
. We fix, once and for all, an isomorphism

\mathbb{C}\cong\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}.

All characters will be viewed over either field through this fixed
isomorphism.

Unless otherwise stated,
\mathbf{G}
denotes a connected reductive algebraic group
defined over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
, and

F\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbf{G}\longrightarrow\mathbf{G}

denotes the Frobenius morphism defining its
\mathbb{F}_{q}
-rational structure. We
write

G=\mathbf{G}^{F}.

More generally, if
\mathbf{H}
is an
F
-stable algebraic subgroup of
\mathbf{G}
, we
write
H=\mathbf{H}^{F}
. On the dual side, rational points are always taken with
respect to the dual Frobenius
F^{*}
.

We write
Z(\mathbf{H})
,
C_{\mathbf{H}}(x)
, and
N_{\mathbf{H}}(\mathbf{K})
for the center of

\mathbf{H}
, the centralizer of an element
x\in\mathbf{H}
, and the normalizer of a
subgroup
\mathbf{K}\leq\mathbf{H}
, respectively. If
\mathbf{S}
is a torus in
\mathbf{H}
, then

W_{\mathbf{H}}(\mathbf{S})\coloneqq N_{\mathbf{H}}(\mathbf{S})/C_{\mathbf{H}}(\mathbf{S})

denotes the corresponding Weyl group. If
\mathbf{S}
is
F
-stable, we shall
also use the finite rational Weyl group

W_{H}(S)\coloneqq N_{H}(S)/C_{H}(S),\qquad S=\mathbf{S}^{F},

when this distinction is relevant.

For a finite group
X
, we write
\operatorname{Irr}(X)
for the set of irreducible
characters of
X
. If
X
is the group of rational points of a connected
reductive group, we write
\operatorname{Uch}(X)
for its set of unipotent characters. If

i\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}H\to K
is a homomorphism of finite groups, and if
i(H)
is clear from
context, we shall sometimes write
K/H
for
K/i(H)
. This notation will
only be used when no ambiguity can result.

We now fix notation for root data. Let
\mathbf{B}_{0}
be an
F
-stable Borel
subgroup of
\mathbf{G}
, and let
\mathbf{T}_{0}\subseteq\mathbf{B}_{0}
be an
F
-stable maximal
torus. We write

X(\mathbf{T}_{0})=\operatorname{Hom}(\mathbf{T}_{0},\mathbb{G}_{m}),\qquad X^{\vee}(\mathbf{T}_{0})=\operatorname{Hom}(\mathbb{G}_{m},\mathbf{T}_{0})

for the character and cocharacter groups. They are equipped with the usual
perfect pairing

\langle\ ,\ \rangle\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X(\mathbf{T}_{0})\times X^{\vee}(\mathbf{T}_{0})\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z},

defined by

\lambda(\mu(a))=a^{\langle\lambda,\mu\rangle}\qquad(a\in\mathbb{G}_{m}).

Let
\Phi=\Phi(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{T}_{0})
be the corresponding root system and

\Phi^{\vee}
the coroot system. The Borel subgroup
\mathbf{B}_{0}
determines a
positive system
\Phi^{+}\subseteq\Phi
, and we denote the associated set of
simple roots by
\Delta
. Thus the triple

(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{B}_{0},\mathbf{T}_{0})

determines the based root datum

\bigl(X(\mathbf{T}_{0}),\Phi,\Delta,X^{\vee}(\mathbf{T}_{0}),\Phi^{\vee},\Delta^{\vee}\bigr).

Let
(\mathbf{G}^{*},F^{*})
be dual to
(\mathbf{G},F)
. Thus there are
F
- and

F^{*}
-stable pairs
(\mathbf{B}_{0},\mathbf{T}_{0})
and
(\mathbf{B}_{0}^{*},\mathbf{T}_{0}^{*})
, together with
an isomorphism

\delta\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X(\mathbf{T}_{0})\xrightarrow{\sim}X^{\vee}(\mathbf{T}_{0}^{*})

which identifies the root datum of
\mathbf{G}
with the dual root datum of

\mathbf{G}^{*}
. In particular,

\delta(\Phi)=\Phi^{*\vee}.

If
\alpha\in\Phi
, we denote by
\alpha^{*}\in\Phi^{*}
the root determined by

\delta(\alpha)=\alpha^{*\vee}.

The isomorphism
\delta
is assumed to be compatible with Frobenius in the
sense that

\delta(\lambda\circ F|_{\mathbf{T}_{0}})=F^{*}|_{\mathbf{T}_{0}^{*}}\circ\delta(\lambda)\qquad(\lambda\in X(\mathbf{T}_{0})).

Equivalently, the induced isomorphism of root data commutes with the Frobenius
actions on the character and cocharacter lattices.

We write

W=W_{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbf{T}_{0}),\qquad W^{*}=W_{\mathbf{G}^{*}}(\mathbf{T}_{0}^{*}).

The duality above identifies
W
with
W^{*}
, by sending the simple
reflection associated with
\alpha
to the simple reflection associated with

\alpha^{*}
. This identification is compatible with the Frobenius actions,
and hence restricts to an isomorphism

W^{F}\xrightarrow{\sim}(W^{*})^{F^{*}}.

Finally, we fix our convention for pinnings. A pinning of
\mathbf{G}
will be
written

\mathcal{P}=(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{B},\mathbf{T},\{x_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in\Delta}),

where
\mathbf{B}
is a Borel subgroup,
\mathbf{T}\subseteq\mathbf{B}
is a maximal torus, and

x_{\alpha}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbb{G}_{a}\to\mathbf{X}_{\alpha}
is a choice of root homomorphism for
each simple root
\alpha
. When the pinning is
F
-stable, the dual group

\mathbf{G}^{*}
is equipped with the corresponding
F^{*}
-stable dual pinning

\mathcal{P}^{*}=(\mathbf{G}^{*},\mathbf{B}^{*},\mathbf{T}^{*},\{x_{\alpha^{*}}\}_{\alpha^{*}\in\Delta^{*}}).

If
s\in T^{*}=\mathbf{T}^{*F^{*}}
is semisimple, we put

\mathbf{H}\coloneqq C_{\mathbf{G}^{*}}(s),\qquad H\coloneqq\mathbf{H}^{F^{*}},\qquad\mathbf{H}^{\circ}\coloneqq C_{\mathbf{G}^{*}}(s)^{\circ},\qquad H^{\circ}\coloneqq(\mathbf{H}^{\circ})^{F^{*}}.

The pinning
\mathcal{P}^{*}
determines a natural pinning of the connected reductive
group
\mathbf{H}^{\circ}
, obtained from the root subgroups of
\mathbf{G}^{*}
whose roots
vanish on
s
.

We shall also use the following notation for pinned automorphisms. Define

\mathsf{A}^{*}\coloneqq\operatorname{Stab}_{\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbf{G}^{*},\mathcal{P}^{*})}(s).

Via duality of pinned root data,
\mathsf{A}^{*}
corresponds to a subgroup

\mathsf{A}\subseteq\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbf{G},\mathcal{P}).

When no confusion is possible, we use the same symbol for an algebraic
automorphism and for the induced automorphism of the corresponding finite group
of rational points.

We shall use the following terminology for algebraic groups. A connected
semisimple algebraic group
\mathbf{X}
over
\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
will
be called
quasi-simple
if its absolute root system is irreducible,
equivalently if its adjoint quotient

\mathbf{X}_{\mathrm{ad}}=\mathbf{X}/Z(\mathbf{X})

is a simple adjoint algebraic group. Thus a quasi-simple algebraic group need
not be simply connected or adjoint. When it is adjoint we shall usually say

simple adjoint
.

If
F
is a Frobenius endomorphism and
\mathbf{X}
is connected
semisimple, we shall say that
\mathbf{X}
is
F
-almost simple if
F

acts transitively on the set of absolute quasi-simple factors of
\mathbf{X}
.
Equivalently, up to isogeny,
\mathbf{X}
is obtained by restriction of
scalars from a quasi-simple group over a finite extension of the ground field.

These terms refer to algebraic groups. We shall not use the finite-group
theoretic meanings of “quasisimple” and “almost simple” unless this is
explicitly stated. In particular, when
X=\mathbf{X}^{F}
, the phrase
“
\mathbf{X}
is quasi-simple” is a statement about the algebraic group

\mathbf{X}
, not about the abstract finite group
X
.

We shall also use the following terminology for the disconnected groups that
arise as centralizers of semisimple elements.

Definition 2.1

(Semisimple-centralizer type)
.

A possibly disconnected reductive algebraic group
\mathbf{H}
is said to be
of
semisimple-centralizer type
if there exist a connected reductive
algebraic group
\mathbf{G}_{0}
and a semisimple element
s\in\mathbf{G}_{0}

such that

\mathbf{H}=C_{\mathbf{G}_{0}}(s).

If a Frobenius endomorphism
F
is present, we shall say that
(\mathbf{H},F)

is of
F
-stable semisimple-centralizer type
if
\mathbf{H}
is

F
-stable in such a realization.

Lemma 2.2

(Digne–Michel)
.

Let
\mathbf{H}=C_{\mathbf{G}_{0}}(s)
be of semisimple-centralizer type, with

\mathbf{G}_{0}
connected reductive and
s\in\mathbf{G}_{0}
semisimple. Then

1\longrightarrow\mathbf{H}^{\circ}\longrightarrow\mathbf{H}\longrightarrow\pi_{0}(\mathbf{H})\longrightarrow 1

splits. Equivalently, there is a finite subgroup
A\subset\mathbf{H}
,
mapping isomorphically onto
\pi_{0}(\mathbf{H})
, such that

\mathbf{H}=\mathbf{H}^{\circ}\rtimes A.

If, moreover,
(\mathbf{H},F)
is of
F
-stable semisimple-centralizer type
and
H=\mathbf{H}^{F}
,
H^{\circ}=(\mathbf{H}^{\circ})^{F}
, then the finite exact
sequence

1\longrightarrow H^{\circ}\longrightarrow H\longrightarrow(\pi_{0}(\mathbf{H}))^{F}\longrightarrow 1

also splits. Finally, after choosing a maximal torus of
\mathbf{H}^{\circ}

and a positive system for its root system, the component group is represented
at the Weyl-group level by the subgroup preserving this positive system.

Proof.

The algebraic splitting is
[
10
, Theorem 0.1]
, and
the splitting on rational points is
[
10
, Theorem 0.2]
.
The final assertion is the Weyl-group splitting in

[
10
, Proposition 1.1]
.
∎

Definition 2.3

(Pinned semidirect component model)
.

Let
\mathbf{H}
be a possibly disconnected reductive algebraic group with
Frobenius
F
, let
H=\mathbf{H}^{F}
, and let
H^{\circ}=(\mathbf{H}^{\circ})^{F}
.
Fix a pinning of
\mathbf{H}^{\circ}
. If
J\leq H
is a subgroup containing

H^{\circ}
, we shall say that
J
has a
pinned semidirect component
model
if there is a finite subgroup
A_{J}\leq J
, mapping isomorphically onto

J/H^{\circ}
, such that

J=H^{\circ}\rtimes A_{J},

and the conjugation action of
A_{J}
on
\mathbf{H}^{\circ}
, after passing to
outer automorphism classes, is represented by the corresponding pinned
automorphisms of
\mathbf{H}^{\circ}
. Thus the semidirect-product notation is
part of the specified model; it is not asserted for an arbitrary disconnected
reductive group.

Remark 2.4
.

If
(\mathbf{H},F)
is of
F
-stable semisimple-centralizer type, then
Lemma
2.2
supplies such a semidirect
component model for
H=\mathbf{H}^{F}
. Intersecting the Digne–Michel
complement with the inverse image of a subgroup of
H/H^{\circ}
supplies the
same kind of model for every intermediate subgroup
J
with

H^{\circ}\leq J\leq H
. This is the justification for the semidirect-product
notation used for centralizers of semisimple elements below.

3.
Preliminaries: Deligne–Lusztig Theory and Jordan Decomposition of Characters

In this section we recall the parts of Deligne–Lusztig theory and Jordan
decomposition of characters that will be used later. This summary is adapted
from
[
3
, §2.3]
. For further details we refer to

[
5
,
17
,
20
,
9
,
11
]
.

Let
\mathbf{G}
be a connected reductive group over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
, with Frobenius
morphism
F\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbf{G}\to\mathbf{G}
, and put
G=\mathbf{G}^{F}
. Let

(\mathbf{G}^{*},F^{*})
be dual to
(\mathbf{G},F)
, and put

G^{*}=\mathbf{G}^{*F^{*}}
.

Proposition 3.1
.

Let
\mathbf{T}
be an
F
-stable maximal torus of
\mathbf{G}
, and let

\mathbf{T}^{*}
be an
F^{*}
-stable maximal torus of
\mathbf{G}^{*}
dual to
\mathbf{T}
.
There is a natural bijection

\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}G\text{-conjugacy classes of pairs }(\mathbf{T},\theta),\\
\theta\in\operatorname{Irr}(T),\ T=\mathbf{T}^{F}\end{array}\right\}\longleftrightarrow\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}G^{*}\text{-conjugacy classes of pairs }(\mathbf{T}^{*},s),\\
s\in T^{*},\ T^{*}=\mathbf{T}^{*F^{*}}\end{array}\right\}.

For an
F
-stable maximal torus
\mathbf{T}
of
\mathbf{G}
and a character

\theta\in\operatorname{Irr}(T)
, Deligne and Lusztig associate a virtual character

R_{\mathbf{T}}^{\mathbf{G}}(\theta)
of
G
, constructed from
\ell
-adic cohomology
with compact support, where
\ell\neq p
. If
(\mathbf{T},\theta)
corresponds
to
(\mathbf{T}^{*},s)
under Proposition
3.1
, we also
write

R_{\mathbf{T}^{*}}^{\mathbf{G}}(s)\coloneqq R_{\mathbf{T}}^{\mathbf{G}}(\theta).

Remark 3.2
.

Suppose that
\mathbf{T}
is contained in an
F
-stable Borel subgroup

\mathbf{B}
of
\mathbf{G}
. Then
R_{\mathbf{T}}^{\mathbf{G}}(\theta)
is an actual character,
rather than merely a virtual character, and it agrees with ordinary
Harish–Chandra induction from
B=\mathbf{B}^{F}
:

R_{\mathbf{T}}^{\mathbf{G}}(\theta)=\operatorname{Ind}_{B}^{G}(\widetilde{\theta}),

where
\widetilde{\theta}
denotes the inflation of
\theta
to
B
.

Definition 3.3

(Lusztig series)
.

Let
s\in G^{*}
be semisimple. The Lusztig series, or rational series,
associated with
s
is

\mathcal{E}(G,s)\coloneqq\left\{\rho\in\operatorname{Irr}(G)\ \middle|\ \langle R_{\mathbf{T}^{*}}^{\mathbf{G}}(s),\rho\rangle\neq 0\text{ for some }F^{*}\text{-stable maximal torus }\mathbf{T}^{*}\subseteq\mathbf{G}^{*}\text{ with }s\in T^{*}\right\}.

Definition 3.4

(Unipotent character)
.

A character
\rho\in\operatorname{Irr}(G)
is called
unipotent
if

\langle R_{\mathbf{T}^{*}}^{\mathbf{G}}(1),\rho\rangle\neq 0

for some
F^{*}
-stable maximal torus
\mathbf{T}^{*}\subseteq\mathbf{G}^{*}
. The set of
unipotent characters of
G
is denoted by
\operatorname{Uch}(G)
. Thus

\operatorname{Uch}(G)=\mathcal{E}(G,1).

Theorem 3.5

(Partition into Lusztig series)
.

Every irreducible character
\rho\in\operatorname{Irr}(G)
occurs in some
Deligne–Lusztig virtual character. More precisely, there exist an

F
-stable maximal torus
\mathbf{T}\subseteq\mathbf{G}
and a character

\theta\in\operatorname{Irr}(T)
such that

\langle R_{\mathbf{T}}^{\mathbf{G}}(\theta),\rho\rangle\neq 0.

Moreover,

\operatorname{Irr}(G)=\bigsqcup_{(s)}\mathcal{E}(G,s),

where
(s)
runs over the semisimple conjugacy classes of
G^{*}
.

Definition 3.6

(Sign)
.

Let
\mathbf{G}
be a connected algebraic group defined over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
, with
Frobenius morphism
F
. Its sign is

\epsilon_{\mathbf{G}}\coloneqq(-1)^{\operatorname{rk}_{\mathbb{F}_{q}}(\mathbf{G})},

where
\operatorname{rk}_{\mathbb{F}_{q}}(\mathbf{G})
denotes the
F
-split rank of
\mathbf{G}
.

For connected reductive groups with connected center, Lusztig

[
17
]
introduced the Jordan decomposition of characters. It gives a
parametrization of the Lusztig series
\mathcal{E}(G,s)
by unipotent characters of
the finite group
C_{\mathbf{G}^{*}}(s)^{F^{*}}
. Lusztig’s construction does not, by
itself, determine a unique bijection with all the functorial properties needed
below. Digne and Michel resolved this ambiguity by proving the following
uniqueness theorem.

Theorem 3.7

(
[
7
, Theorem 7.1]
)
.

There exists a unique collection of bijections

J_{s}^{G}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathcal{E}(G,s)\longrightarrow\operatorname{Uch}(H),

where
\mathbf{G}
runs over connected reductive groups with connected center,

s\in G^{*}
is semisimple, and

\mathbf{H}\coloneqq C_{\mathbf{G}^{*}}(s),\qquad H\coloneqq\mathbf{H}^{F^{*}},

satisfying the following properties.

(1)

For every
F^{*}
-stable maximal torus
\mathbf{T}^{*}\leq\mathbf{H}
and every

\rho\in\mathcal{E}(G,s)
, one has

\langle R_{\mathbf{T}^{*}}^{\mathbf{G}}(s),\rho\rangle=\epsilon_{\mathbf{G}}\epsilon_{\mathbf{H}}\langle R_{\mathbf{T}^{*}}^{\mathbf{H}}(1_{T^{*}}),J_{s}^{G}(\rho)\rangle.

(2)

If
s=1
and
\rho\in\operatorname{Uch}(G)
, then:

(a)

the Frobenius eigenvalues
\omega_{\rho}
and

\omega_{J_{1}^{G}(\rho)}
are equal;

(b)

if
\rho
lies in the principal series, then

\rho
and
J_{1}^{G}(\rho)
correspond to the same character of the
Iwahori–Hecke algebra.

(3)

If
z\in Z(G^{*})
, then

J_{sz}^{G}(\rho\otimes\widehat{z})=J_{s}^{G}(\rho)

for every
\rho\in\mathcal{E}(G,s)
, where
\widehat{z}
is the linear character
of
G
corresponding to
z
.

(4)

Let
\mathbf{L}^{*}
be an
F^{*}
-stable Levi subgroup of
\mathbf{G}^{*}
such
that
\mathbf{H}\leq\mathbf{L}^{*}
, and let
\mathbf{L}\leq\mathbf{G}
be the dual Levi subgroup.
Then the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}(G,s)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Uch}(H)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}(L,s)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Uch}(H).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J_{s}^{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{L}^{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J_{s}^{L}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Id}}

(5)

Suppose that
\mathbf{G}
is of type
\mathsf{E}_{8}
, and that
\mathbf{H}
is of
type
\mathsf{E}_{7}\mathsf{A}_{1}
, respectively of type
\mathsf{E}_{6}\mathsf{A}_{2}
. Let

\mathbf{L}\leq\mathbf{G}
be a Levi subgroup of type
\mathsf{E}_{7}
, respectively

\mathsf{E}_{6}
, whose dual
\mathbf{L}^{*}
is contained in
\mathbf{H}
. Then the
following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{E}(G,s)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}\operatorname{Uch}(H)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{E}(L,s)_{c}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}\operatorname{Uch}(L^{*})_{c},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J_{s}^{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{\mathbf{L}}^{\mathbf{G}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J_{s}^{L}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{\mathbf{L}^{*}}^{\mathbf{H}}}

where the subscript
c
denotes the subgroup spanned by the cuspidal
part of the corresponding Lusztig series.

(6)

Let
\mathbf{T}_{1}\leq Z(\mathbf{G})
be an
F
-stable central torus, and let

\pi_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbf{G}\longrightarrow\mathbf{G}_{1}\coloneqq\mathbf{G}/\mathbf{T}_{1}

be the natural epimorphism. Let
s_{1}\in G_{1}^{*}
and suppose that

s=\pi_{1}^{*}(s_{1})
. Put

\mathbf{H}_{1}\coloneqq C_{\mathbf{G}_{1}^{*}}(s_{1}).

Then the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}(G,s)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Uch}(H)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}(G_{1},s_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Uch}(H_{1}),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J_{s}^{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J_{s_{1}}^{G_{1}}}

where the left vertical map is inflation along
G\to G_{1}
, and the right
vertical map is restriction along the natural embedding
H_{1}\hookrightarrow H
.

(7)

If
\mathbf{G}=\prod_{i}\mathbf{G}_{i}
is a direct product of
F
-stable
subgroups
\mathbf{G}_{i}
, and
s=\prod_{i}s_{i}
, then

J_{s}^{G}=\prod_{i}J_{s_{i}}^{G_{i}}.

Lusztig
[
20
]
extended the Jordan decomposition of characters to
connected reductive groups whose center need not be connected. The construction
uses a regular embedding into a connected reductive group with connected center.
We recall the relevant notation and auxiliary results, referring to

[
20
]
for the proofs.

Remark 3.8
.

Let

\pi\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbf{G}\longrightarrow\mathbf{G}_{\operatorname{ad}}

be the adjoint quotient. The finite group
G_{\operatorname{ad}}
acts on
G
by
conjugation through representatives in
\mathbf{G}_{\operatorname{ad}}
, and hence

G_{\operatorname{ad}}/\pi(G)
acts on
\operatorname{Irr}(G)
. This action extends linearly to
virtual characters. It preserves each Lusztig series
\mathcal{E}(G,s)
, since it
preserves the corresponding Deligne–Lusztig virtual characters

R_{\mathbf{T}^{*}}^{\mathbf{G}}(s)
. Thus
G_{\operatorname{ad}}/\pi(G)
acts naturally on

\mathcal{E}(G,s)
.

Definition 3.9

(Regular embedding)
.

Let
\mathbf{G}
be a connected reductive group defined over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
. A morphism

i\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbf{G}\longrightarrow\mathbf{G}^{\prime}

defined over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
is called a
regular embedding
if:

•

\mathbf{G}^{\prime}
is a connected reductive group over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
with connected
center;

•

i
identifies
\mathbf{G}
with a closed subgroup of
\mathbf{G}^{\prime}
;

•

i(\mathbf{G})
and
\mathbf{G}^{\prime}
have the same derived subgroup.

Let
i\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbf{G}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{G}^{\prime}
be a regular embedding. We write

G^{\prime}=\mathbf{G}^{\prime F}
, and identify
G
with its image in
G^{\prime}
. By

[
11
, Theorem 1.7.12]
, the dual morphism is a surjective
homomorphism

i^{*}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbf{G}^{\prime*}\longrightarrow\mathbf{G}^{*}

defined over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
. Its kernel

\mathbf{K}\coloneqq\ker(i^{*})

is an
F^{*}
-stable torus contained in
Z(\mathbf{G}^{\prime*})
. Put

K\coloneqq\mathbf{K}^{F^{*}}.

There is a canonical isomorphism

K\cong\operatorname{Hom}(G^{\prime}/G,\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}^{\times}).

Thus
K
acts on
\operatorname{Irr}(G^{\prime})
by tensoring with the corresponding linear
characters of
G^{\prime}/G
. Under this action, if
k\in K
, then tensoring by
the character corresponding to
k
sends

\mathcal{E}(G^{\prime},s^{\prime})\quad\text{onto}\quad\mathcal{E}(G^{\prime},ks^{\prime}).

For
s^{\prime}\in G^{\prime*}=\mathbf{G}^{\prime*F^{*}}
, define

K_{s^{\prime}}\coloneqq\{\,k\in K\mid ks^{\prime}\text{ is }G^{\prime*}\text{-conjugate to }s^{\prime}\,\}.

Equivalently,
K_{s^{\prime}}
is the stabilizer of the Lusztig series

\mathcal{E}(G^{\prime},s^{\prime})
under the above action of
K
.

Lemma 3.10
.

Let
s^{\prime}\in G^{\prime*}
, set

s=i^{*}(s^{\prime})\in G^{*},\qquad\mathbf{H}=C_{\mathbf{G}^{*}}(s),\qquad H=\mathbf{H}^{F^{*}},\qquad H^{\circ}=(\mathbf{H}^{\circ})^{F^{*}}.

Then there is a canonical isomorphism

H/H^{\circ}\cong K_{s^{\prime}}.

Consequently,
H/H^{\circ}
acts on
\mathcal{E}(G^{\prime},s^{\prime})
.

Proof.

For
x\in H
, choose a lift
\dot{x}\in\mathbf{G}^{\prime*}
with

i^{*}(\dot{x})=x
. Since
x
centralizes
s=i^{*}(s^{\prime})
, the element

s^{\prime-1}\dot{x}s^{\prime}\dot{x}^{-1}

belongs to
\mathbf{K}
. This construction induces a well-defined homomorphism

H/H^{\circ}\longrightarrow K,

whose image is precisely
K_{s^{\prime}}
. The resulting map is the required
isomorphism.
∎

Proposition 3.11

(cf.
[
11
, Proposition 2.3.15]
)
.

Let
i\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbf{G}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{G}^{\prime}
be a regular embedding, and let

s^{\prime}\in G^{\prime*}
. Put

s=i^{*}(s^{\prime}),\qquad\mathbf{H}=C_{\mathbf{G}^{*}}(s),\qquad\mathbf{H}^{\prime}=C_{\mathbf{G}^{\prime*}}(s^{\prime}).

Then
i^{*}
induces a surjective morphism

\mathbf{H}^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbf{H}^{\circ}

with kernel
\mathbf{K}
. Moreover, pullback along this morphism gives a canonical
bijection

\operatorname{Uch}(H^{\circ})\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname{Uch}(H^{\prime}),\qquad\eta\longmapsto\eta\circ i^{*}|_{H^{\prime}}.

Via the identification
H/H^{\circ}\cong K_{s^{\prime}}
, this bijection transports the
natural
H/H^{\circ}
-action on
\operatorname{Uch}(H^{\circ})
to the corresponding action
on
\operatorname{Uch}(H^{\prime})
.

The compatibility of the connected-center Jordan decomposition with central
twists implies the following equivariance statement.

Lemma 3.12

(
[
20
, Proposition 8.1]
)
.

The Jordan decomposition bijection for
G^{\prime}
,

J_{s^{\prime}}^{G^{\prime}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathcal{E}(G^{\prime},s^{\prime})\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname{Uch}(H^{\prime}),

is compatible with the
H/H^{\circ}
-action, where
H/H^{\circ}
is identified
with
K_{s^{\prime}}
as in Lemma
3.10
.

Remark 3.13
.

Let
i\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbf{G}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{G}^{\prime}
be a regular embedding. By

[
11
, Remark 1.7.6]
,
i
induces isomorphisms

\mathbf{G}_{\operatorname{ad}}\cong\mathbf{G}^{\prime}_{\operatorname{ad}},\qquad G_{\operatorname{ad}}\cong G^{\prime}_{\operatorname{ad}}.

Hence the conjugation action of
G^{\prime}
on
G
induces a natural surjective
homomorphism

G^{\prime}/G\twoheadrightarrow G_{\operatorname{ad}}/\pi(G).

In particular, the action of
G^{\prime}/G
on
\operatorname{Irr}(G)
factors through the
action of
G_{\operatorname{ad}}/\pi(G)
.

On the other hand, the canonical isomorphism

K\cong\operatorname{Hom}(G^{\prime}/G,\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}^{\times})

identifies

G^{\prime}/G\cong\operatorname{Hom}(K,\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}^{\times}).

Restricting characters from
K
to
K_{s^{\prime}}
, and using
Lemma
3.10
, gives a natural surjective homomorphism

\alpha\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}G^{\prime}/G\twoheadrightarrow\operatorname{Hom}(K_{s^{\prime}},\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}^{\times})\cong\operatorname{Hom}(H/H^{\circ},\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}^{\times}).

The preceding paragraph shows that
\alpha
factors through

G_{\operatorname{ad}}/\pi(G)
.

Theorem 3.14

(Multiplicity-freeness
[
20
, §10]
)
.

Let
i\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbf{G}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{G}^{\prime}
be a regular embedding. Then, for every

\rho^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Irr}(G^{\prime})
, the restriction
\rho^{\prime}|_{G}
is multiplicity-free.

We shall also use the following elementary Clifford-theoretic fact, in the
form used by Lusztig.

Remark 3.15

(
[
20
, §9]
)
.

Let
N\triangleleft M
be finite groups, and suppose that
M/N
is
abelian. The conjugation action of
M
on
N
induces an action of

M/N
on
\operatorname{Irr}(N)
. The dual group

(M/N)^{\vee}\coloneqq\operatorname{Hom}(M/N,\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}^{\times})

acts on
\operatorname{Irr}(M)
by tensoring with linear characters.

Assume that, for every
\rho^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Irr}(M)
, the restriction
\rho^{\prime}|_{N}
is
multiplicity-free. Then there is a unique bijection between orbit sets

\operatorname{Irr}(N)/(M/N)\longleftrightarrow\operatorname{Irr}(M)/(M/N)^{\vee}

with the following property. If
\mathcal{O}\subseteq\operatorname{Irr}(N)
corresponds to

\mathcal{O}^{\prime}\subseteq\operatorname{Irr}(M)
, and if

\rho_{\circ}\in\mathcal{O}
,
\rho^{\prime}_{\circ}\in\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
, then

\rho^{\prime}_{\circ}|_{N}=\sum_{\rho\in\mathcal{O}}\rho,\qquad\operatorname{Ind}_{N}^{M}(\rho_{\circ})=\sum_{\rho^{\prime}\in\mathcal{O}^{\prime}}\rho^{\prime}.

Moreover, the stabilizer of
\rho_{\circ}
in
M/N
and the stabilizer of

\rho^{\prime}_{\circ}
in
(M/N)^{\vee}
are orthogonal under the natural pairing

M/N\times(M/N)^{\vee}\longrightarrow\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}^{\times}.

With this notation in place, we can now state Lusztig’s disconnected-center
version of Jordan decomposition
[
20
, Proposition 5.1]
.

Theorem 3.16

(Jordan decomposition for disconnected centralizers)
.

Let
s\in G^{*}
be semisimple, and put

\mathbf{H}\coloneqq C_{\mathbf{G}^{*}}(s),\qquad H\coloneqq\mathbf{H}^{F^{*}},\qquad H^{\circ}\coloneqq(\mathbf{H}^{\circ})^{F^{*}}.

Fix a regular embedding
i\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbf{G}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{G}^{\prime}
, and choose

s^{\prime}\in G^{\prime*}
such that
i^{*}(s^{\prime})=s
. Then Lusztig constructs a
surjective map

J_{s}=J_{s}^{(G,G^{\prime})}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathcal{E}(G,s)\twoheadrightarrow\operatorname{Uch}(H^{\circ})/(H/H^{\circ})

with the following properties.

(1)

The fibers of
J_{s}
are precisely the

G_{\operatorname{ad}}/\pi(G)
-orbits on
\mathcal{E}(G,s)
.

(2)

Let
\mathcal{O}\subseteq\operatorname{Uch}(H^{\circ})
be an

H/H^{\circ}
-orbit, and let

\Gamma=\operatorname{Stab}_{H/H^{\circ}}(u)

be the stabilizer of any element
u\in\mathcal{O}
. Then

|J_{s}^{-1}(\mathcal{O})|=|\Gamma|.

(3)

If
\rho\in\mathcal{E}(G,s)
and
J_{s}(\rho)=\mathcal{O}
, then, for
every
F^{*}
-stable maximal torus
\mathbf{T}^{*}\subseteq\mathbf{G}^{*}
containing

s
, one has

\big\langle R_{\mathbf{T}^{*}}^{\mathbf{G}}(s),\rho\big\rangle_{G}=\epsilon_{\mathbf{G}}\epsilon_{\mathbf{H}^{\circ}}\sum_{u\in\mathcal{O}}\big\langle R_{\mathbf{T}^{*}}^{\mathbf{H}^{\circ}}(1_{T^{*}}),u\big\rangle_{H^{\circ}}.

Remark 3.17
.

We recall how the map
J_{s}
in Theorem
3.16
is
constructed. Let

K=\ker(i^{*}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbf{G}^{\prime*}\to\mathbf{G}^{*})

and choose
s^{\prime}\in G^{\prime*}
with
i^{*}(s^{\prime})=s
. By Clifford theory and the
multiplicity-freeness of restriction from
G^{\prime}
to
G
, there is a natural
bijection of orbit sets

\mathcal{E}(G,s)/(G_{\operatorname{ad}}/\pi(G))\;\cong\;\left(\bigcup_{k\in K}\mathcal{E}(G^{\prime},s^{\prime}k)\right)/K.

Since
K
acts by central twists, the right-hand side may be identified with

\mathcal{E}(G^{\prime},s^{\prime})/K_{s^{\prime}},

where

K_{s^{\prime}}=\{\,k\in K\mid s^{\prime}k\text{ is }G^{\prime*}\text{-conjugate to }s^{\prime}\,\}.

By Lemma
3.10
, we have

K_{s^{\prime}}\cong H/H^{\circ}.

The connected-center Jordan decomposition for
G^{\prime}
gives

J_{s^{\prime}}^{G^{\prime}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathcal{E}(G^{\prime},s^{\prime})\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname{Uch}(H^{\prime}),\qquad H^{\prime}=C_{\mathbf{G}^{\prime*}}(s^{\prime})^{F^{*}},

and Lemma
3.12
says that this bijection is

K_{s^{\prime}}
-equivariant. Hence it descends to a bijection

\mathcal{E}(G^{\prime},s^{\prime})/K_{s^{\prime}}\;\xrightarrow{\sim}\;\operatorname{Uch}(H^{\prime})/K_{s^{\prime}}.

Finally, Proposition
3.11
identifies
\operatorname{Uch}(H^{\prime})
with

\operatorname{Uch}(H^{\circ})
, compatibly with the action of

K_{s^{\prime}}\cong H/H^{\circ}
. Composing these maps gives

\mathcal{E}(G,s)/(G_{\operatorname{ad}}/\pi(G))\;\xrightarrow{\sim}\;\operatorname{Uch}(H^{\circ})/(H/H^{\circ}),

and
J_{s}
is the resulting orbit-valued map on
\mathcal{E}(G,s)
.

Theorem 3.18

(
[
11
, Theorem 3.2.22]
)
.

Let
s\in G^{*}
be semisimple, and put

\mathbf{H}=C_{\mathbf{G}^{*}}(s),\qquad H^{\circ}=(\mathbf{H}^{\circ})^{F^{*}}.

Let
\rho\in\mathcal{E}(G,s)
, and let

J_{s}(\rho)=\mathcal{O}\subseteq\operatorname{Uch}(H^{\circ})

be its image under Theorem
3.16
. Choose

u_{\rho}\in\mathcal{O}
. Then
\rho
is cuspidal if and only if the
following two conditions hold:

(1)

u_{\rho}
is cuspidal;

(2)

the maximal
\mathbb{F}_{q}
-split subtorus of
Z(\mathbf{H}^{\circ})^{\circ}
is
contained in
Z(\mathbf{G}^{*})^{\circ}
. Equivalently,

\operatorname{rank}_{\mathbb{F}_{q}}\bigl(Z(\mathbf{G}^{*})^{\circ}\bigr)=\operatorname{rank}_{\mathbb{F}_{q}}\bigl(Z(\mathbf{H}^{\circ})^{\circ}\bigr).

The first condition is independent of the choice of
u_{\rho}\in\mathcal{O}
,
since cuspidality is preserved under the action of
H/H^{\circ}
.

The following elementary consequence will be used later. In fact, the
conclusion follows directly from the description of Levi subgroups as
centralizers of split central tori; the cuspidality hypothesis is not needed.

Corollary 3.19
.

Let
\mathbf{L}^{*}
be an
F^{*}
-stable Levi factor of an
F^{*}
-stable parabolic
subgroup of
\mathbf{G}^{*}
, and suppose that
s\in L^{*}
. Then

C_{\mathbf{L}^{*}}(s)^{\circ}

is an
F^{*}
-stable Levi subgroup of

C_{\mathbf{G}^{*}}(s)^{\circ}.

Proof.

Let
A_{\mathbf{L}^{*}}
be the maximal
\mathbb{F}_{q}
-split torus in

Z(\mathbf{L}^{*})^{\circ}
. Since
\mathbf{L}^{*}
is an
F^{*}
-stable Levi subgroup, we
may write

\mathbf{L}^{*}=C_{\mathbf{G}^{*}}(A_{\mathbf{L}^{*}}).

Because
s\in L^{*}
, the torus
A_{\mathbf{L}^{*}}
centralizes
s
. Hence

A_{\mathbf{L}^{*}}\subseteq C_{\mathbf{G}^{*}}(s)^{\circ}
. Therefore

C_{\mathbf{L}^{*}}(s)^{\circ}=C_{C_{\mathbf{G}^{*}}(s)^{\circ}}(A_{\mathbf{L}^{*}}).

The centralizer of a torus in a connected reductive group is a Levi subgroup.
Thus
C_{\mathbf{L}^{*}}(s)^{\circ}
is a Levi subgroup of

C_{\mathbf{G}^{*}}(s)^{\circ}
. Since both
s
and
\mathbf{L}^{*}
are
F^{*}
-stable, this
Levi subgroup is
F^{*}
-stable.
∎

Lemma 3.20

(
[
9
, Lemma 11.2.1]
)
.

Let
\mathbf{G}
be a connected reductive group over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
.

(1)

If
Z(\mathbf{G})
is connected, then the center of every Levi subgroup
of
\mathbf{G}
is connected.

(2)

If
Z(\mathbf{G})
is connected, then the centralizer of every semisimple
element in
\mathbf{G}^{*}
is connected.

4.
Harish–Chandra Series, Endomorphism Algebras, and Relative Weyl Groups

In this section we recall the standard material on Harish–Chandra induction,
Harish–Chandra series, and the associated relative Weyl groups. We also fix
the notation used later for the Howlett–Lehrer parametrization of
Harish–Chandra series. The final result of the section is a uniqueness
criterion for certain Harish–Chandra series characters; it will be used later
in the construction of the Jordan decomposition.

We begin with the basic setup. Let
\mathbf{G}
be a connected reductive algebraic
group over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
, with Frobenius morphism
F\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbf{G}\to\mathbf{G}
, and put

G=\mathbf{G}^{F}
. Let
\mathbf{P}
be an
F
-stable parabolic subgroup of
\mathbf{G}
with
an
F
-stable Levi decomposition

\mathbf{P}=\mathbf{L}\mathbf{U},

where
\mathbf{L}
is a Levi subgroup and
\mathbf{U}
is the unipotent radical. We
write

R_{\mathbf{L}}^{\mathbf{G}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad{}^{*}R_{\mathbf{L}}^{\mathbf{G}}

for Harish–Chandra induction and Harish–Chandra restriction, respectively.

Definition 4.1

(Harish–Chandra series)
.

Let
\mathbf{L}
be an
F
-stable Levi subgroup of an
F
-stable parabolic
subgroup of
\mathbf{G}
, and let
\tau\in\operatorname{Irr}(L)
be cuspidal, where

L=\mathbf{L}^{F}
. The Harish–Chandra series associated with the cuspidal pair

(L,\tau)
is

\operatorname{Irr}(G,(L,\tau))\coloneqq\left\{\rho\in\operatorname{Irr}(G)\ \middle|\ \big\langle{}^{*}R_{\mathbf{L}}^{\mathbf{G}}(\rho),\tau\big\rangle_{L}\neq 0\right\}.

Equivalently,
\operatorname{Irr}(G,(L,\tau))
is the set of irreducible constituents of

R_{\mathbf{L}}^{\mathbf{G}}(\tau)
.

The Harish–Chandra series form a partition of
\operatorname{Irr}(G)
; see

[
11
, Corollary 3.1.17]
. More precisely, if

(L,\tau)
and
(M,\lambda)
are cuspidal pairs that are not

G
-conjugate, then

\operatorname{Irr}(G,(L,\tau))\cap\operatorname{Irr}(G,(M,\lambda))=\emptyset,

and every irreducible character of
G
belongs to a unique Harish–Chandra
series up to
G
-conjugacy of the cuspidal pair.

The following standard result relates Harish–Chandra series to Lusztig
series; see
[
9
, Corollary 3.3.21]
.

Proposition 4.2
.

Let
s\in G^{*}
be semisimple. Then the Lusztig series
\mathcal{E}(G,s)
is a
union of Harish–Chandra series:

\mathcal{E}(G,s)=\bigsqcup_{(L,\tau)\in\Sigma_{G}(s)}\operatorname{Irr}(G,(L,\tau)).

Here
\Sigma_{G}(s)
denotes the set of
G
-conjugacy classes of cuspidal
pairs
(L,\tau)
such that, for a dual Levi subgroup

\mathbf{L}^{*}\subseteq\mathbf{G}^{*}
with
s\in L^{*}
, one has

\tau\in\mathcal{E}(L,s).

We next recall the relative Weyl group attached to a cuspidal pair. Let

A_{\mathbf{L}}
be the maximal
\mathbb{F}_{q}
-split torus in
Z(\mathbf{L})
. Then

\mathbf{L}=C_{\mathbf{G}}(A_{\mathbf{L}}).

Define

W_{\mathbf{G}}(A_{\mathbf{L}})\coloneqq N_{G}(A_{\mathbf{L}})/L.

This group acts on
L
, and hence on
\operatorname{Irr}(L)
, through representatives in

N_{G}(A_{\mathbf{L}})
. For a cuspidal character
\tau\in\operatorname{Irr}(L)
, set

W_{\tau}(\mathbf{G})\coloneqq\{\,w\in W_{\mathbf{G}}(A_{\mathbf{L}})\mid{}^{w}\tau\simeq\tau\,\}.

Equivalently,
W_{\tau}(\mathbf{G})
is the stabilizer of the character

\chi_{\tau}
of
\tau
. When the ambient group
\mathbf{G}
is clear from
context, we write simply
W_{\tau}
.

The structure of Harish–Chandra series is governed by Howlett–Lehrer theory.
In its most useful form for us, this theory describes the endomorphism algebra
of
R_{\mathbf{L}}^{\mathbf{G}}(\tau)
in terms of an Iwahori–Hecke algebra attached to
the relative Weyl group
W_{\tau}
. The possible twisting cocycle that appears
in the general construction is trivial in the finite reductive group setting;
this follows from Lusztig’s work in the connected-center case and from Geck’s
reduction to that case. We refer to
[
13
]
and

[
11
, §3.2]
for details.

Theorem 4.3

(Howlett–Lehrer)
.

Let
\mathbf{L}
be the Levi factor of an
F
-stable parabolic subgroup of

\mathbf{G}
, and let
\tau\in\operatorname{Irr}(L)
be cuspidal. Then

\operatorname{End}_{G}\!\bigl(R_{\mathbf{L}}^{\mathbf{G}}(\tau)\bigr)^{\mathrm{op}}

is naturally described by the Howlett–Lehrer Hecke algebra attached to

W_{\tau}(\mathbf{G})
. In particular, by the Tits deformation theorem, the simple
modules of this endomorphism algebra are naturally parametrized by

\operatorname{Irr}(W_{\tau}(\mathbf{G}))
. Consequently, there is a bijection

I_{L,\tau}^{G}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\operatorname{Irr}(W_{\tau}(\mathbf{G}))\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname{Irr}(G,(L,\tau)),\qquad\phi\longmapsto\rho_{\phi}.

We shall use this parametrization together with its compatibility with
Harish–Chandra induction. The following form is the one needed later; see

[
11
, Theorem 3.2.7]
.

Theorem 4.4

(Howlett–Lehrer comparison theorem)
.

Let
(L,\tau)
be a cuspidal pair of
G
. For every
F
-stable Levi
subgroup
\mathbf{M}
of an
F
-stable parabolic subgroup of
\mathbf{G}
, with

\mathbf{L}\leq\mathbf{M}\leq\mathbf{G},

there is a bijection

I_{L,\tau}^{M}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\operatorname{Irr}(W_{\tau}(\mathbf{M}))\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname{Irr}(M,(L,\tau)),\qquad\phi\longmapsto\rho_{\phi},

and these bijections are compatible with induction in the following sense:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Irr}(W_{\tau}(\mathbf{G}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}\operatorname{Irr}(G,(L,\tau))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Irr}(W_{\tau}(\mathbf{M}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}\operatorname{Irr}(M,(L,\tau))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I_{L,\tau}^{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Ind}_{W_{\tau}(\mathbf{M})}^{W_{\tau}(\mathbf{G})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I_{L,\tau}^{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{\mathbf{M}}^{\mathbf{G}}}

Here the horizontal maps are extended linearly to Grothendieck groups on the
right-hand side.

Equivalently, if
\phi\in\operatorname{Irr}(W_{\tau}(\mathbf{M}))
, then

R_{\mathbf{M}}^{\mathbf{G}}(\rho_{\phi})=\sum_{\psi\in\operatorname{Irr}(W_{\tau}(\mathbf{G}))}\big\langle\operatorname{Ind}_{W_{\tau}(\mathbf{M})}^{W_{\tau}(\mathbf{G})}(\phi),\psi\big\rangle\rho_{\psi}.

Thus the decomposition of Harish–Chandra induced characters is controlled by
ordinary character induction for the corresponding relative Weyl groups.

We now record the promised uniqueness criterion. The following result and its
proof were provided to us by Jay Taylor.

Proposition 4.5

(Jay Taylor)
.

Let
(L,\tau)
be a cuspidal pair in
G
.
Suppose
\phi,\phi^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Irr}(W_{\tau}(\mathbf{G}))
satisfy the following:

(1)

{}^{*}R_{\mathbf{M}}^{\mathbf{G}}(\rho_{\phi})={}^{*}R_{\mathbf{M}}^{\mathbf{G}}(\rho_{\phi^{\prime}})
for any proper
F
-stable Levi subgroup
\mathbf{L}\leqslant\mathbf{M}<\mathbf{G}
, of an
F
-stable parabolic subgroup of
\mathbf{G}
,

(2)

\rho_{\phi}(1)=\rho_{\phi^{\prime}}(1)
.

Then
\rho_{\phi}=\rho_{\phi^{\prime}}
except possibly when
W_{\tau}(\mathbf{G})
is irreducible of type
\mathsf{E}_{7}
or
\mathsf{E}_{8}
and
\{\phi,\phi^{\prime}\}
is an exceptional family of characters of dimension
512
or
4096
as in
[
12
, 6.3.6]
.

Proof.

Put

\mathcal{W}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=W_{\tau}(\mathbf{G}).

If
\mathcal{W}
is trivial, then there is nothing to prove. We therefore assume that

\mathcal{W}\neq 1
.

We first translate the hypothesis on Harish–Chandra restrictions into a
statement about restrictions of characters of
\mathcal{W}
. Let
\mathbf{M}
be an

F
-stable Levi subgroup with
\mathbf{L}\leq\mathbf{M}\leq\mathbf{G}
, and put

\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{M}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=W_{\tau}(\mathbf{M}).

We extend the Howlett–Lehrer bijection

I_{L,\tau}^{M}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\operatorname{Irr}(\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{M}})\longrightarrow\operatorname{Irr}(M,(L,\tau))

linearly to virtual characters. By the comparison theorem and Frobenius
reciprocity, the projection of
{}^{*}R_{\mathbf{M}}^{\mathbf{G}}(\rho_{\psi})
to the
Harish–Chandra series
\operatorname{Irr}(M,(L,\tau))
is

(4.1)

I_{L,\tau}^{M}\!\left(\operatorname{Res}_{\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{M}}}^{\mathcal{W}}\psi\right),\qquad\psi\in\operatorname{Irr}(\mathcal{W}).

Indeed, for
\eta\in\operatorname{Irr}(\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{M}})
, the comparison theorem gives

R_{\mathbf{M}}^{\mathbf{G}}\bigl(I_{L,\tau}^{M}(\eta)\bigr)=I_{L,\tau}^{G}\!\left(\operatorname{Ind}_{\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{M}}}^{\mathcal{W}}\eta\right),

and hence

\left\langle{}^{*}R_{\mathbf{M}}^{\mathbf{G}}(\rho_{\psi}),I_{L,\tau}^{M}(\eta)\right\rangle_{M}=\left\langle\psi,\operatorname{Ind}_{\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{M}}}^{\mathcal{W}}\eta\right\rangle_{\mathcal{W}}=\left\langle\operatorname{Res}_{\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{M}}}^{\mathcal{W}}\psi,\eta\right\rangle_{\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{M}}}.

Thus hypothesis (1) implies

(4.2)

\operatorname{Res}_{\mathcal{W}^{\prime}}^{\mathcal{W}}\phi=\operatorname{Res}_{\mathcal{W}^{\prime}}^{\mathcal{W}}\phi^{\prime}

for every proper parabolic subgroup
\mathcal{W}^{\prime}<\mathcal{W}
. In particular, taking

\mathbf{M}=\mathbf{L}
, so that
\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{L}}=1
, gives

(4.3)

\phi(1)=\phi^{\prime}(1).

We shall also use the degree formula for the constituents of

R_{\mathbf{L}}^{\mathbf{G}}(\tau)
. Let
c_{\psi}
denote the Schur element attached to
the irreducible character
\psi
of the Howlett–Lehrer Hecke algebra. By
Lusztig’s degree formula, see
[
17
, Cor. 8.7]
and

[
12
, §8.1.8]
,
there is a nonzero constant
C_{\mathbf{G},\mathbf{L},\tau}
, independent of
\psi
, such
that

(4.4)

\rho_{\psi}(1)=C_{\mathbf{G},\mathbf{L},\tau}\frac{\psi(1)}{c_{\psi}}.

Combining hypothesis (2) with (
4.3
) and
(
4.4
), we obtain

(4.5)

c_{\phi}=c_{\phi^{\prime}}.

We now reduce to the case where
\mathcal{W}
is irreducible. Write

\mathcal{W}=\mathcal{W}_{1}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{W}_{r}

as a product of its irreducible Coxeter components, and write

\phi=\phi_{1}\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes\phi_{r},\qquad\phi^{\prime}=\phi^{\prime}_{1}\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes\phi^{\prime}_{r}.

If
r>1
, then each factor
\mathcal{W}_{i}
is a proper parabolic subgroup of
\mathcal{W}
,
arising from an intermediate Levi subgroup in the Howlett–Lehrer
correspondence. Hence (
4.2
) gives

\frac{\phi(1)}{\phi_{i}(1)}\,\phi_{i}=\operatorname{Res}_{\mathcal{W}_{i}}^{\mathcal{W}}\phi=\operatorname{Res}_{\mathcal{W}_{i}}^{\mathcal{W}}\phi^{\prime}=\frac{\phi^{\prime}(1)}{\phi^{\prime}_{i}(1)}\,\phi^{\prime}_{i}.

Since
\phi_{i}
and
\phi^{\prime}_{i}
are irreducible characters of
\mathcal{W}_{i}
, this
implies
\phi_{i}=\phi^{\prime}_{i}
. This holds for every
i
, and therefore

\phi=\phi^{\prime}
. We may therefore assume from now on that
\mathcal{W}
is irreducible.

Suppose first that
\mathcal{W}
is of type
\mathsf{A}_{n}
, with
n\geq 1
. If neither

\phi
nor
\phi^{\prime}
is the trivial character
\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{W}}
, then
(
4.2
) implies
\phi=\phi^{\prime}
by

[
12
, Cor. 5.4.8]
.
We may therefore assume that one of the two characters is
\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{W}}
.
By (
4.3
), both characters have degree one, so

\{\phi,\phi^{\prime}\}\subseteq\{\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{W}},\varepsilon\},

where
\varepsilon
is the sign character. If
n\geq 2
, then restriction to
a rank-one parabolic subgroup distinguishes
\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{W}}
from

\varepsilon
, and hence
\phi=\phi^{\prime}
. If
n=1
, then the corresponding
Hecke algebra has parameter
q^{k}
for some
k\geq 1
, and the two Schur
elements are

c_{\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{W}}}=\Phi_{2}(q^{k}),\qquad c_{\varepsilon}=q^{-k}\Phi_{2}(q^{k}).

These are distinct. Therefore (
4.5
) again forces

\phi=\phi^{\prime}
.

If
\mathcal{W}
is of type
\mathsf{B}_{n}
,
\mathsf{C}_{n}
, or
\mathsf{D}_{n}
, in the
usual irreducible ranges, then (
4.2
)
already implies
\phi=\phi^{\prime}
by

[
12
, Thm. 6.2.9]
.
It remains to consider the exceptional crystallographic types. Let
\mathcal{W}
be
of type
\mathsf{G}_{2}
. By the classification of the possible failures of
separation by proper parabolic restrictions, the only possible distinct pair
satisfying (
4.2
) is

\{\phi,\phi^{\prime}\}=\{\phi_{2,1},\phi_{2,2}\},

where
\phi_{2,1}
and
\phi_{2,2}
are the two irreducible characters of
degree
2
, distinguished by their
b
-invariants. According to

[
17
, Table II]
and
[
17
, Thm. 8.6]
, the possible parameters for
the corresponding Hecke algebra are of the form

(q,q^{2k-1}),\qquad k\in\{1,2,5\}.

By
[
12
, Thm. 8.3.4]
,
the Schur elements are

c_{\phi_{2,b}}=2q^{-2k+1}\,\Phi_{3}(q^{k+b-2})\,\Phi_{6}(q^{k-b+1}),\qquad b\in\{1,2\}.

Thus it suffices to compare the two products

\Phi_{3}(q^{k-1})\Phi_{6}(q^{k})\quad\text{and}\quad\Phi_{3}(q^{k})\Phi_{6}(q^{k-1}).

For
k=1,2,5
these factor as follows:

\begin{array}[]{c|c|c}k&\Phi_{3}(q^{k-1})\Phi_{6}(q^{k})&\Phi_{3}(q^{k})\Phi_{6}(q^{k-1})\\
\hline\cr 1&3\Phi_{6}(q)&\Phi_{3}(q)\\
2&\Phi_{3}(q)\Phi_{12}(q)&\Phi_{3}(q)\Phi_{6}(q)^{2}\\
5&\Phi_{3}(q)\Phi_{6}(q)^{2}\Phi_{12}(q)\Phi_{30}(q)&\Phi_{3}(q)\Phi_{15}(q)\Phi_{24}(q).\end{array}

For
q=2
the two entries in each row are unequal by direct evaluation. For

q\neq 2
, Zsigmondy’s theorem gives a primitive prime divisor of

\Phi_{6}(q)
,
\Phi_{12}(q)
, and
\Phi_{30}(q)
respectively in the three
rows; such a prime cannot divide any of the cyclotomic factors appearing on
the other side. Hence

c_{\phi_{2,1}}\neq c_{\phi_{2,2}},

contradicting (
4.5
). Therefore
\phi=\phi^{\prime}
in
type
\mathsf{G}_{2}
as well.

Finally suppose that
\mathcal{W}
is of type

\mathsf{F}_{4},\quad\mathsf{E}_{6},\quad\mathsf{E}_{7},\quad\text{or}\quad\mathsf{E}_{8}.

By
[
12
, 6.3.6]
,
proper parabolic restrictions determine irreducible characters in these types
except for the exceptional pairs occurring in types
\mathsf{E}_{7}
and

\mathsf{E}_{8}
. These exceptional pairs have degrees
512
and
4096
,
respectively. Thus, outside precisely the exceptional cases stated in the
proposition, (
4.2
) forces

\phi=\phi^{\prime}
, and hence
\rho_{\phi}=\rho_{\phi^{\prime}}
.
∎

Let
\mathbf{T}
be an
F
-stable maximal torus contained in
\mathbf{L}
, and let
\theta\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}T\to\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}^{\times}
be a character. Let
\mathbf{L}^{*}
be an
F^{*}
-stable Levi subgroup of
\mathbf{G}^{*}
corresponding to the Levi subgroup
\mathbf{L}
of
\mathbf{G}
. Note that
\mathbf{L}^{*}
is determined up to
G^{*}
-conjugacy.
Let
(\mathbf{T}^{*},s)
be a pair corresponding to
(\mathbf{T},\theta)
as in Proposition
3.1
, with
\mathbf{T}^{*}\subset\mathbf{L}^{*}
.

Lemma 4.6
.

Let
[(\mathbf{T},\theta)]
denote the
L
-conjugacy class of
(\mathbf{T},\theta)
, and let
[(\mathbf{T}^{*},s)]
denote the
L^{*}
-conjugacy class of
(\mathbf{T}^{*},s)
. Then the isomorphism

\delta\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}W_{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbf{T}_{0})\longrightarrow W_{\mathbf{G}^{*}}(\mathbf{T}_{0}^{*}),

induces an isomorphism

\operatorname{Stab}_{W_{\mathbf{G}}(A_{\mathbf{L}})}\left([(\mathbf{T},\theta)]\right)\cong\operatorname{Stab}_{W_{\mathbf{G}^{*}}(A_{\mathbf{L}^{*}})}\left([(\mathbf{T}^{*},s)]\right).

Proof.

By
[
13
, Proposition 2.1]
, we have

W_{\mathbf{G}}(A_{\mathbf{L}})\cong\frac{N_{W_{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbf{T}_{0})^{F}}(W_{\mathbf{L}}(\mathbf{T}_{0})^{F})}{W_{\mathbf{L}}(\mathbf{T}_{0})^{F}}.

Thus,
W_{\mathbf{G}}(A_{\mathbf{L}})\cong W_{\mathbf{G}^{*}}(A_{\mathbf{L}^{*}})
under the isomorphism
\delta
, from which the result follows.
∎

Lemma 4.7
.

Let
s\in T^{*}
be a semisimple element of
L^{*}
and let
\tau\in\mathcal{E}(L,s)
be a cuspidal representation.
Let
\mathbf{H}=C_{\mathbf{G}^{*}}(s)
be the centraliser of
s
in
\mathbf{G}^{*}
, and let
\mathbf{H}_{\mathbf{L}}=C_{\mathbf{L}^{*}}(s)
be the centraliser of
s
in
\mathbf{L}^{*}
.
Then

\operatorname{Stab}_{W_{\mathbf{G}^{*}}(A_{\mathbf{L}^{*}})}\left([(\mathbf{T}^{*},s)]\right)\cong\operatorname{Stab}_{W_{\mathbf{H}}(A_{\mathbf{L}^{*}})}\left([(\mathbf{T}^{*},1)]\right),

where

W_{\mathbf{H}}(A_{\mathbf{L}^{*}})=\frac{N_{H}(A_{\mathbf{L}^{*}})}{H_{\mathbf{L}}}.

Proof.

Since
A_{\mathbf{L}^{*}}
is the maximal
\mathbb{F}_{q}
-split subtorus of
Z(\mathbf{L}^{*})^{\circ}
, Theorem
3.18
implies that
A_{\mathbf{L}^{*}}
is also the maximal
\mathbb{F}_{q}
-split subtorus of
Z((\mathbf{H}_{L})^{\circ})
. Moreover, we have
C_{\mathbf{G}^{*}}(A_{\mathbf{L}^{*}})=\mathbf{L}^{*}
and
C_{\mathbf{H}}(A_{\mathbf{L}^{*}})=\mathbf{H}_{\mathbf{L}}
. The result follows from these observations and the definition of the relevant stabilisers.
∎

5.
Jordan decomposition, relative Weyl groups, and endomorphism algebras

In this section we compare the action of relative Weyl groups on cuspidal
characters with the corresponding action on the Jordan-decomposition side.
We first treat the case where the Levi subgroup has connected centre, and
then reduce the general case to this one by means of a regular embedding.
The final consequence is that the endomorphism algebra of Harish–Chandra
induction depends only on the relevant fibre of the disconnected Jordan
decomposition.

5.1.
The connected-centre case

Let
\mathbf{G}
be a connected reductive group defined over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
, and let

\mathbf{L}
be an
F
–stable Levi factor of an
F
–stable parabolic subgroup of

\mathbf{G}
. Throughout this subsection we assume that
Z(\mathbf{L})
is connected.
Let
A_{\mathbf{L}}
denote the maximal
\mathbb{F}_{q}
–split torus in
Z(\mathbf{L})
.

Let

\delta\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}W_{\mathbf{G}}(A_{\mathbf{L}})\xrightarrow{\sim}W_{\mathbf{G}^{*}}(A_{\mathbf{L}^{*}})

be the duality isomorphism. For
w\in W_{\mathbf{G}}(A_{\mathbf{L}})
, write

w^{*}=\delta(w)
. We choose representatives inducing
F
–equivariant
automorphisms

\sigma_{w}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbf{L}\longrightarrow\mathbf{L},\qquad\sigma_{w^{*}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbf{L}^{*}\longrightarrow\mathbf{L}^{*},

dual to one another. The induced action on characters of
L
is given by

\rho\longmapsto\rho\circ\sigma_{w}^{-1}.

Let
s\in L^{*}
be semisimple and put

s^{w}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\sigma_{w^{*}}(s),\qquad\mathbf{H}_{L,s}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=C_{\mathbf{L}^{*}}(s),\qquad\mathbf{H}_{L,s^{w}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=C_{\mathbf{L}^{*}}(s^{w}).

Then
\sigma_{w^{*}}
identifies
\mathbf{H}_{L,s}
with
\mathbf{H}_{L,s^{w}}
. By the
functoriality of Deligne–Lusztig induction under dual automorphisms, the map

f_{w}^{s}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathcal{E}(L,s)\longrightarrow\mathcal{E}(L,s^{w}),\qquad\rho\longmapsto\rho\circ\sigma_{w}^{-1},

is a bijection. Similarly,
\sigma_{w^{*}}
induces a bijection

f_{w^{*}}^{s}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\operatorname{Uch}(H_{L,s})\longrightarrow\operatorname{Uch}(H_{L,s^{w}}),\qquad u\longmapsto u\circ\sigma_{w^{*}}^{-1}.

Let

J_{s}^{L}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathcal{E}(L,s)\longrightarrow\operatorname{Uch}(H_{L,s})

denote the Digne–Michel Jordan decomposition for
\mathbf{L}
, as normalized in
Theorem
3.7
. Transporting the Jordan
decomposition at
s^{w}
back to the Lusztig series of
s
gives another
bijection

\widetilde{J}_{s,w}^{L}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=(f_{w^{*}}^{s})^{-1}\circ J_{s^{w}}^{L}\circ f_{w}^{s}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathcal{E}(L,s)\longrightarrow\operatorname{Uch}(H_{L,s}).

The following proposition says that the Digne–Michel normalization is
invariant under this transport.

Proposition 5.1
.

Let
\mathbf{G}
be a connected reductive group defined over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
, and let

\mathbf{L}
be an
F
–stable Levi factor of an
F
–stable parabolic subgroup of

\mathbf{G}
such that
Z(\mathbf{L})
is connected. Let

w\in W_{\mathbf{G}}(A_{\mathbf{L}})
, and let

w^{*}\in W_{\mathbf{G}^{*}}(A_{\mathbf{L}^{*}})
be the corresponding element under the duality
isomorphism. Choose representatives inducing
F
–equivariant automorphisms

\sigma_{w}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbf{L}\longrightarrow\mathbf{L},\qquad\sigma_{w^{*}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbf{L}^{*}\longrightarrow\mathbf{L}^{*}.

For a semisimple element
s\in L^{*}
, put

s^{w}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\sigma_{w^{*}}(s),\qquad\mathbf{H}_{s}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=C_{\mathbf{L}^{*}}(s),\qquad\mathbf{H}_{s^{w}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=C_{\mathbf{L}^{*}}(s^{w}).

Then
\sigma_{w^{*}}
identifies
\mathbf{H}_{s}
with
\mathbf{H}_{s^{w}}
, and for every

\tau\in\mathcal{E}(L,s)
one has

J_{s^{w}}^{L}\bigl(\tau\circ\sigma_{w}^{-1}\bigr)=J_{s}^{L}(\tau)\circ\sigma_{w^{*}}^{-1}.

Equivalently, if

f_{w}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathcal{E}(L,s)\longrightarrow\mathcal{E}(L,s^{w}),\qquad\tau\longmapsto\tau\circ\sigma_{w}^{-1},

and

f_{w^{*}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\operatorname{Uch}(H_{s})\longrightarrow\operatorname{Uch}(H_{s^{w}}),\qquad u\longmapsto u\circ\sigma_{w^{*}}^{-1},

then

f_{w^{*}}\circ J_{s}^{L}=J_{s^{w}}^{L}\circ f_{w},

or, equivalently,

J_{s}^{L}=f_{w^{*}}^{-1}\circ J_{s^{w}}^{L}\circ f_{w}.

Proof.

Changing the chosen representatives of
w
and
w^{*}
changes

\sigma_{w}
and
\sigma_{w^{*}}
only by inner automorphisms of
\mathbf{L}
and

\mathbf{L}^{*}
, respectively. Hence the induced maps on irreducible characters and
on unipotent characters are independent of these choices, after the usual
identification of characters under inner conjugacy.

The automorphisms
\sigma_{w}
and
\sigma_{w^{*}}
are dual to one another.
Therefore Deligne–Lusztig induction is transported by these automorphisms:
for every
F^{*}
–stable maximal torus
\mathbf{T}^{*}\leq\mathbf{H}_{s}
,

R_{\sigma_{w^{*}}(\mathbf{T}^{*})}^{\mathbf{L}}(s^{w})=R_{\mathbf{T}^{*}}^{\mathbf{L}}(s)\circ\sigma_{w}^{-1},

and similarly

R_{\sigma_{w^{*}}(\mathbf{T}^{*})}^{\mathbf{H}_{s^{w}}}(1)=R_{\mathbf{T}^{*}}^{\mathbf{H}_{s}}(1)\circ\sigma_{w^{*}}^{-1}.

It follows at once that
f_{w}
maps
\mathcal{E}(L,s)
bijectively onto

\mathcal{E}(L,s^{w})
, and that
f_{w^{*}}
maps
\operatorname{Uch}(H_{s})
bijectively onto

\operatorname{Uch}(H_{s^{w}})
.

Define

\widetilde{J}_{s}^{L}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=f_{w^{*}}^{-1}\circ J_{s^{w}}^{L}\circ f_{w}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathcal{E}(L,s)\longrightarrow\operatorname{Uch}(H_{s}).

We claim that
\widetilde{J}_{s}^{L}
satisfies the same Digne–Michel
normalizing conditions as
J_{s}^{L}
. For the first condition, let

\rho\in\mathcal{E}(L,s)
and let
\mathbf{T}^{*}\leq\mathbf{H}_{s}
be an
F^{*}
–stable
maximal torus. Using the preceding functoriality and then the defining
property of
J_{s^{w}}^{L}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\bigl\langle R_{\mathbf{T}^{*}}^{\mathbf{L}}(s),\rho\bigr\rangle_{L}

\displaystyle=\bigl\langle R_{\sigma_{w^{*}}(\mathbf{T}^{*})}^{\mathbf{L}}(s^{w}),\rho\circ\sigma_{w}^{-1}\bigr\rangle_{L}

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{\mathbf{L}}\epsilon_{\mathbf{H}_{s^{w}}}\bigl\langle R_{\sigma_{w^{*}}(\mathbf{T}^{*})}^{\mathbf{H}_{s^{w}}}(1),J_{s^{w}}^{L}(\rho\circ\sigma_{w}^{-1})\bigr\rangle_{H_{s^{w}}}

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{\mathbf{L}}\epsilon_{\mathbf{H}_{s}}\bigl\langle R_{\mathbf{T}^{*}}^{\mathbf{H}_{s}}(1),\widetilde{J}_{s}^{L}(\rho)\bigr\rangle_{H_{s}}.

Here
\epsilon_{\mathbf{H}_{s}}=\epsilon_{\mathbf{H}_{s^{w}}}
, since

\sigma_{w^{*}}
identifies
\mathbf{H}_{s}
with
\mathbf{H}_{s^{w}}
.

The remaining Digne–Michel conditions are preserved by the same transport
of structure. The condition for
s=1
is preserved because the relevant
Deligne–Lusztig varieties, Frobenius actions, and Iwahori–Hecke algebras
are identified by
\sigma_{w}
and
\sigma_{w^{*}}
. The compatibility with
central characters follows from the fact that, if
z\in Z(L^{*})
, then the
linear character
\widehat{z}\circ\sigma_{w}^{-1}
corresponds to

\widehat{\sigma_{w^{*}}(z)}
. Compatibility with Harish–Chandra induction
is transported by the identities

f_{w}\circ R_{\mathbf{M}}^{\mathbf{L}}=R_{\sigma_{w}(\mathbf{M})}^{\mathbf{L}}\circ f_{w}

and the corresponding identity on the dual side. The exceptional

\mathsf{E}_{8}
conditions, the compatibility with central quotients, and the
direct-product condition are likewise invariant under the same

F
–equivariant isomorphisms.

Thus the family obtained from the Digne–Michel Jordan decomposition by
transport through
(\sigma_{w},\sigma_{w^{*}})
again satisfies the
Digne–Michel characterizing properties. By the uniqueness assertion in
Theorem
3.7
, it must coincide with the
original family. Hence

\widetilde{J}_{s}^{L}=J_{s}^{L},

which is precisely

J_{s}^{L}=f_{w^{*}}^{-1}\circ J_{s^{w}}^{L}\circ f_{w}.

Equivalently,

J_{s^{w}}^{L}\bigl(\tau\circ\sigma_{w}^{-1}\bigr)=J_{s}^{L}(\tau)\circ\sigma_{w^{*}}^{-1}

for all
\tau\in\mathcal{E}(L,s)
.
∎

Proposition 5.2
.

Let
\mathbf{G}
be a connected reductive group over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
, and let

\mathbf{L}
be an
F
–stable Levi factor of an
F
–stable parabolic subgroup of

\mathbf{G}
such that
Z(\mathbf{L})
is connected. Let
s\in L^{*}
be semisimple, and let

\tau\in\mathcal{E}(L,s)

be a cuspidal irreducible character of
L
. Put

\mathbf{H}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=C_{\mathbf{G}^{*}}(s),\qquad\mathbf{H}_{\mathbf{L}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=C_{\mathbf{L}^{*}}(s),

and set

u\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=J_{s}^{L}(\tau)\in\operatorname{Uch}(H_{\mathbf{L}}).

Define

W_{\tau}(\mathbf{G})\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\left\{w\in W_{\mathbf{G}}(A_{\mathbf{L}})\;\middle|\;\tau\circ\sigma_{w}^{-1}=\tau\right\},

and

W_{u}(\mathbf{H})\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\left\{w^{*}\in W_{\mathbf{H}}(A_{\mathbf{L}^{*}})\;\middle|\;u\circ\sigma_{w^{*}}^{-1}=u\right\},

where

W_{\mathbf{H}}(A_{\mathbf{L}^{*}})=N_{H}(A_{\mathbf{L}^{*}})/H_{\mathbf{L}}.

Then the duality isomorphism

\delta\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}W_{\mathbf{G}}(A_{\mathbf{L}})\xrightarrow{\sim}W_{\mathbf{G}^{*}}(A_{\mathbf{L}^{*}})

restricts, after the standard identification of the stabilizer of the

L^{*}
–class of
s
with
W_{\mathbf{H}}(A_{\mathbf{L}^{*}})
, to a canonical isomorphism

W_{\tau}(\mathbf{G})\xrightarrow{\;\sim\;}W_{u}(\mathbf{H}).

Equivalently,

W_{\tau}(\mathbf{G})\cong W_{J_{s}^{L}(\tau)}(\mathbf{H}).

Proof.

We first recall the identification of the target relative Weyl group. The
inclusion

N_{H}(A_{\mathbf{L}^{*}})\subset N_{G^{*}}(A_{\mathbf{L}^{*}})

induces an injective homomorphism

W_{\mathbf{H}}(A_{\mathbf{L}^{*}})=N_{H}(A_{\mathbf{L}^{*}})/H_{\mathbf{L}}\longrightarrow W_{\mathbf{G}^{*}}(A_{\mathbf{L}^{*}})=N_{G^{*}}(A_{\mathbf{L}^{*}})/L^{*}.

Its image is precisely the stabilizer of the
L^{*}
–conjugacy class of
s
.
Indeed, if
n^{*}\in N_{G^{*}}(A_{\mathbf{L}^{*}})
represents an element stabilizing the

L^{*}
–class of
s
, then there is an element
\ell^{*}\in L^{*}
such that

\ell^{*}n^{*}s(n^{*})^{-1}(\ell^{*})^{-1}=s.

Thus
\ell^{*}n^{*}\in N_{H}(A_{\mathbf{L}^{*}})
, and its class modulo
H_{\mathbf{L}}
depends
only on the class of
n^{*}
modulo
L^{*}
.

Let
w\in W_{\tau}(\mathbf{G})
. Then

\tau\circ\sigma_{w}^{-1}=\tau.

If
w^{*}=\delta(w)
, then the usual compatibility of Lusztig series with dual
automorphisms gives

\tau\circ\sigma_{w}^{-1}\in\mathcal{E}\bigl(L,\sigma_{w^{*}}(s)\bigr).

Since the Lusztig series of
L
are disjoint and

\tau\in\mathcal{E}(L,s)
, the elements
s
and
\sigma_{w^{*}}(s)
are

L^{*}
–conjugate. Hence
w^{*}
lies in the stabilizer of the
L^{*}
–class of

s
, and therefore determines an element of

W_{\mathbf{H}}(A_{\mathbf{L}^{*}})
. We denote this element by
\delta_{s}(w)
.

Choose a representative of
\delta_{s}(w)
in
N_{H}(A_{\mathbf{L}^{*}})
. Then it
centralizes
s
, and hence its action preserves
\mathbf{H}_{\mathbf{L}}=C_{\mathbf{L}^{*}}(s)
.
By Proposition
5.1
,
applied in the form

J_{s}^{L}(\rho\circ\sigma_{w}^{-1})=J_{s}^{L}(\rho)\circ\sigma_{\delta_{s}(w)}^{-1},\qquad\rho\in\mathcal{E}(L,s),

we obtain

u\circ\sigma_{\delta_{s}(w)}^{-1}=J_{s}^{L}(\tau)\circ\sigma_{\delta_{s}(w)}^{-1}=J_{s}^{L}(\tau\circ\sigma_{w}^{-1})=J_{s}^{L}(\tau)=u.

Thus
\delta_{s}(w)\in W_{u}(\mathbf{H})
.

Conversely, let
w^{*}\in W_{u}(\mathbf{H})
, and let

w\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\delta^{-1}(w^{*})

under the inclusion

W_{\mathbf{H}}(A_{\mathbf{L}^{*}})\hookrightarrow W_{\mathbf{G}^{*}}(A_{\mathbf{L}^{*}}).

Since
w^{*}
is represented by an element of
N_{H}(A_{\mathbf{L}^{*}})
, it centralizes

s
. Hence
\sigma_{w^{*}}(s)=s
, and therefore

\tau\circ\sigma_{w}^{-1}
again lies in
\mathcal{E}(L,s)
. Using the same
equivariance of the Jordan decomposition, we get

J_{s}^{L}(\tau\circ\sigma_{w}^{-1})=J_{s}^{L}(\tau)\circ\sigma_{w^{*}}^{-1}=u\circ\sigma_{w^{*}}^{-1}=u=J_{s}^{L}(\tau).

Since
J_{s}^{L}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathcal{E}(L,s)\to\operatorname{Uch}(H_{\mathbf{L}})
is a bijection, it follows that

\tau\circ\sigma_{w}^{-1}=\tau.

Thus
w\in W_{\tau}(\mathbf{G})
.

The two constructions are inverse to each other, and hence
\delta
induces
the desired canonical isomorphism

W_{\tau}(\mathbf{G})\cong W_{u}(\mathbf{H})=W_{J_{s}^{L}(\tau)}(\mathbf{H}).

∎

Theorem 5.3
.

Let
\mathbf{G}
be a connected reductive group over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
with a connected center.
Let
\mathbf{L}
be an
F
-stable Levi factor of an
F
-stable parabolic subgroup of
G
and
let
\tau\in\mathcal{E}(L,s)
be a cuspidal irreducible representation of
L^{F}
.
Then there is a canonical isomorphism,

\operatorname{End}_{G}(R_{\mathbf{L}}^{\mathbf{G}}(\tau))\cong\operatorname{End}_{H}(R_{\mathbf{H}_{L}}^{\mathbf{H}}(J_{s}^{L}(\tau))),

where
J_{s}^{L}
is the unique Jordan decomposition as in Theorem
3.7
.

Proof.

The Theorem follows from Proposition
5.2
and Theorem
4.3
.
∎

5.2.
General case

We now remove the hypothesis that the centre of the Levi subgroup is connected.
Let
\mathbf{L}
be an
F
–stable Levi subgroup of
\mathbf{G}
. Choose an
F
–stable
torus
\mathbf{S}
containing
Z(\mathbf{L})
, and form

\mathbf{G}^{\prime}=(\mathbf{G}\times\mathbf{S})/\{(z,z^{-1})\mid z\in Z(\mathbf{G})\}.

Let
i\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbf{G}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{G}^{\prime}
be the regular embedding induced by

g\mapsto(g,1)
, and let
\mathbf{S}^{\prime}
be the image of
\{1\}\times\mathbf{S}
in

\mathbf{G}^{\prime}
. Then
Z(\mathbf{G}^{\prime})=\mathbf{S}^{\prime}
is connected. Put

\mathbf{L}^{\prime}=\mathbf{L}\cdot\mathbf{S}^{\prime}.

Then
\mathbf{L}^{\prime}
is an
F
–stable Levi subgroup of
\mathbf{G}^{\prime}
, and the restriction

i_{L}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbf{L}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{L}^{\prime}

is a regular embedding. We write, as usual,

L=\mathbf{L}^{F},\qquad L^{\prime}=\mathbf{L}^{\prime F}.

Let
