i_{L}^{*}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbf{L}^{\prime*}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbf{L}^{*}

be the dual epimorphism, and let

\mathbf{K}=\ker(i_{L}^{*}).

For
s\in L^{*}
choose
s^{\prime}\in L^{\prime*}
such that
i_{L}^{*}(s^{\prime})=s
. Put

\mathbf{H}_{L}=C_{\mathbf{L}^{*}}(s),\qquad\mathbf{H}_{L^{\prime}}=C_{\mathbf{L}^{\prime*}}(s^{\prime}).

By Lusztig’s construction for a regular embedding, we have a surjective map

J_{s}^{L}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathcal{E}(L,s)\longrightarrow\operatorname{Uch}(H_{L}^{\circ})/(H_{L}/H_{L}^{\circ}).

Its fibres are the diagonal-automorphism orbits on
\mathcal{E}(L,s)
; with
the above regular embedding, these may be described as the
L^{\prime}/L
–orbits.
For
\tau\in\mathcal{E}(L,s)
, we denote its
L^{\prime}/L
–orbit by

[\tau]_{L^{\prime}}.

We shall use the following equivariance property of the disconnected Jordan
map.

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
w\in W_{\mathbf{G}}(A_{\mathbf{L}})
, and let

w^{*}=\delta(w)\in W_{\mathbf{G}^{*}}(A_{\mathbf{L}^{*}})

be its dual element. Suppose that
w^{*}
stabilizes the
L^{*}
–conjugacy
class of
s
. Under the standard identification of this stabilizer with

W_{\mathbf{H}}(A_{\mathbf{L}^{*}})
, where
\mathbf{H}=C_{\mathbf{G}^{*}}(s)
, let the corresponding element
be denoted by
w_{s}^{*}
. Then, for every
\rho\in\mathcal{E}(L,s)
,

J_{s}^{L}(\rho\circ\sigma_{w}^{-1})=w_{s}^{*}\cdot J_{s}^{L}(\rho)

as elements of

\operatorname{Uch}(H_{L}^{\circ})/(H_{L}/H_{L}^{\circ}).

Proof.

The assertion is a formal consequence of Lusztig’s construction of

J_{s}^{L}
by means of the regular embedding
i_{L}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbf{L}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{L}^{\prime}
,
together with the equivariance of the connected-centre Jordan decomposition.

Indeed, the element
w
also acts on
\mathbf{L}^{\prime}
, since
\mathbf{L}^{\prime}=\mathbf{L}\cdot\mathbf{S}^{\prime}
and

\mathbf{S}^{\prime}
is central in
\mathbf{G}^{\prime}
. Let
\widetilde{w}^{*}
be the corresponding
dual element acting on
\mathbf{L}^{\prime*}
. Since
i_{L}^{*}(s^{\prime})=s
and
w_{s}^{*}
centralizes

s
, the element
\widetilde{w}^{*}
need not fix
s^{\prime}
itself; rather,

\widetilde{w}^{*}(s^{\prime})=s^{\prime}k_{w}

for a uniquely determined element
k_{w}\in K
. Thus
\widetilde{w}^{*}
permutes
the union

\bigcup_{k\in K}\mathcal{E}(L^{\prime},s^{\prime}k),

and this is precisely the ambiguity accounted for in Lusztig’s construction
of the disconnected Jordan map.

Each step in the construction of
J_{s}^{L}
is natural with respect to the
automorphisms induced by
w
and
\widetilde{w}^{*}
: the passage to

L^{\prime}/L
–orbits, the correspondence between
L^{\prime}/L
–orbits in

\mathcal{E}(L,s)
and
K
–orbits in the union

\bigcup_{k\in K}\mathcal{E}(L^{\prime},s^{\prime}k)
, the passage from
K
to
K_{s^{\prime}}
, and
the identification

K_{s^{\prime}}\cong H_{L}/H_{L}^{\circ}.

The only non-formal part is the connected-centre Jordan decomposition

J_{s^{\prime}}^{L^{\prime}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathcal{E}(L^{\prime},s^{\prime})\longrightarrow\operatorname{Uch}(H_{L^{\prime}}),

and its equivariance is exactly Proposition

5.1
, applied to the
Levi subgroup
\mathbf{L}^{\prime}
of
\mathbf{G}^{\prime}
.

Therefore the disconnected map
J_{s}^{L}
is equivariant for the induced Weyl
actions, giving

J_{s}^{L}(\rho\circ\sigma_{w}^{-1})=w_{s}^{*}\cdot J_{s}^{L}(\rho).

∎

Theorem 5.5
.

Let
\mathbf{G}
be a connected reductive group over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
, and let

\mathbf{L}
be an
F
–stable Levi factor of an
F
–stable parabolic subgroup of

\mathbf{G}
. Let
s\in L^{*}
be semisimple, and let

\tau\in\mathcal{E}(L,s)

be cuspidal. Put

\mathbf{H}=C_{\mathbf{G}^{*}}(s),\qquad\mathbf{H}_{L}=C_{\mathbf{L}^{*}}(s),

and let

\bar{u}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=J_{s}^{L}(\tau)\in\operatorname{Uch}(H_{L}^{\circ})/(H_{L}/H_{L}^{\circ}).

Define

\operatorname{Stab}_{W_{\mathbf{G}}(A_{\mathbf{L}})}([\tau]_{L^{\prime}})=\left\{w\in W_{\mathbf{G}}(A_{\mathbf{L}})\;\middle|\;[\tau\circ\sigma_{w}^{-1}]_{L^{\prime}}=[\tau]_{L^{\prime}}\right\},

and

W_{\bar{u}}(\mathbf{H})=\left\{w^{*}\in W_{\mathbf{H}}(A_{\mathbf{L}^{*}})\;\middle|\;w^{*}\cdot\bar{u}=\bar{u}\right\}.

Then the duality isomorphism

\delta\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}W_{\mathbf{G}}(A_{\mathbf{L}})\xrightarrow{\sim}W_{\mathbf{G}^{*}}(A_{\mathbf{L}^{*}})

restricts to a canonical isomorphism

\operatorname{Stab}_{W_{\mathbf{G}}(A_{\mathbf{L}})}([\tau]_{L^{\prime}})\;\xrightarrow{\;\sim\;}\;W_{\bar{u}}(\mathbf{H}).

Equivalently,

\operatorname{Stab}_{W_{\mathbf{G}}(A_{\mathbf{L}})}([\tau]_{L^{\prime}})\cong W_{J_{s}^{L}(\tau)}(\mathbf{H}),

where the right-hand side is the stabilizer of the

H_{L}/H_{L}^{\circ}
–orbit
J_{s}^{L}(\tau)
.

Proof.

We first recall the relevant identification on the dual side. The natural
map

W_{\mathbf{H}}(A_{\mathbf{L}^{*}})=N_{H}(A_{\mathbf{L}^{*}})/H_{L}\longrightarrow W_{\mathbf{G}^{*}}(A_{\mathbf{L}^{*}})

is injective, and its image is the stabilizer of the
L^{*}
–conjugacy class
of
s
. Indeed, if
n^{*}\in N_{G^{*}}(A_{\mathbf{L}^{*}})
represents an element
stabilizing the
L^{*}
–class of
s
, then for some
\ell^{*}\in L^{*}
one has

\ell^{*}n^{*}s(n^{*})^{-1}(\ell^{*})^{-1}=s.

Thus
\ell^{*}n^{*}\in N_{H}(A_{\mathbf{L}^{*}})
, and its class modulo
H_{L}
depends only
on the class of
n^{*}
modulo
L^{*}
.

Let

w\in\operatorname{Stab}_{W_{\mathbf{G}}(A_{\mathbf{L}})}([\tau]_{L^{\prime}}).

Then

[\tau\circ\sigma_{w}^{-1}]_{L^{\prime}}=[\tau]_{L^{\prime}}.

In particular,
\tau\circ\sigma_{w}^{-1}
belongs to the same disconnected
Lusztig series
\mathcal{E}(L,s)
. On the other hand, the usual compatibility
of Lusztig series with dual automorphisms gives

\tau\circ\sigma_{w}^{-1}\in\mathcal{E}(L,\delta(w)(s)).

Since Lusztig series are disjoint,
\delta(w)(s)
is
L^{*}
–conjugate to

s
. Hence
\delta(w)
lies in the stabilizer of the
L^{*}
–class of
s
,
and therefore determines an element

\delta_{s}(w)\in W_{\mathbf{H}}(A_{\mathbf{L}^{*}}).

By Lemma
5.4
,

J_{s}^{L}(\tau\circ\sigma_{w}^{-1})=\delta_{s}(w)\cdot J_{s}^{L}(\tau).

But
J_{s}^{L}
is constant on
L^{\prime}/L
–orbits, and

[\tau\circ\sigma_{w}^{-1}]_{L^{\prime}}=[\tau]_{L^{\prime}}.

Therefore

\delta_{s}(w)\cdot J_{s}^{L}(\tau)=J_{s}^{L}(\tau).

Thus

\delta_{s}(w)\in W_{\bar{u}}(\mathbf{H}).

Conversely, let

w^{*}\in W_{\bar{u}}(\mathbf{H}),

and let
w\in W_{\mathbf{G}}(A_{\mathbf{L}})
be the inverse image of
w^{*}
under the
duality isomorphism, using the inclusion

W_{\mathbf{H}}(A_{\mathbf{L}^{*}})\hookrightarrow W_{\mathbf{G}^{*}}(A_{\mathbf{L}^{*}}).

Since
w^{*}
is represented by an element of
N_{H}(A_{\mathbf{L}^{*}})
, it stabilizes

s
. Hence

\tau\circ\sigma_{w}^{-1}\in\mathcal{E}(L,s).

Again by Lemma
5.4
,

J_{s}^{L}(\tau\circ\sigma_{w}^{-1})=w^{*}\cdot J_{s}^{L}(\tau).

Since
w^{*}
fixes
\bar{u}=J_{s}^{L}(\tau)
, this gives

J_{s}^{L}(\tau\circ\sigma_{w}^{-1})=J_{s}^{L}(\tau).

The fibres of
J_{s}^{L}
are precisely the diagonal-automorphism orbits, which
in the present regular embedding are the
L^{\prime}/L
–orbits. Therefore

[\tau\circ\sigma_{w}^{-1}]_{L^{\prime}}=[\tau]_{L^{\prime}}.

Thus

w\in\operatorname{Stab}_{W_{\mathbf{G}}(A_{\mathbf{L}})}([\tau]_{L^{\prime}}).

The two constructions are inverse to one another. Hence duality induces the
desired canonical isomorphism

\operatorname{Stab}_{W_{\mathbf{G}}(A_{\mathbf{L}})}([\tau]_{L^{\prime}})\cong W_{J_{s}^{L}(\tau)}(\mathbf{H}).

∎

The preceding theorem also shows that the endomorphism algebra of the
Harish–Chandra induction depends only on the fibre of the disconnected Jordan
decomposition, up to non-canonical algebra isomorphism.

Corollary 5.6
.

Let
\mathbf{G}
be a connected reductive group over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
, and let

\mathbf{L}
be an
F
–stable Levi factor of an
F
–stable parabolic subgroup of

\mathbf{G}
. Let
s\in L^{*}
be semisimple, and let

\tau,\tau^{\prime}\in\mathcal{E}(L,s)

be irreducible cuspidal characters. Suppose that

J_{s}^{L}(\tau)=J_{s}^{L}(\tau^{\prime})

in

\operatorname{Uch}(H_{L}^{\circ})/(H_{L}/H_{L}^{\circ}),\qquad H_{L}=C_{\mathbf{L}^{*}}(s).

Then

\operatorname{End}_{G}\bigl(R_{\mathbf{L}}^{\mathbf{G}}(\tau)\bigr)\cong\operatorname{End}_{G}\bigl(R_{\mathbf{L}}^{\mathbf{G}}(\tau^{\prime})\bigr).

Proof.

Choose a regular embedding

i\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbf{G}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{G}^{\prime}

with connected centre, and put
\mathbf{L}^{\prime}=\mathbf{L}Z(\mathbf{G}^{\prime})
. By Lusztig’s construction of
the disconnected Jordan decomposition, the fibres of

J_{s}^{L}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathcal{E}(L,s)\longrightarrow\operatorname{Uch}(H_{L}^{\circ})/(H_{L}/H_{L}^{\circ})

are precisely the diagonal-automorphism orbits, equivalently the
L^{\prime}/L
–orbits
on
\mathcal{E}(L,s)
. Hence the equality

J_{s}^{L}(\tau)=J_{s}^{L}(\tau^{\prime})

implies that there exists
x\in L^{\prime}
such that

\tau^{\prime}\simeq\tau\circ\sigma_{x}^{-1},

where
\sigma_{x}
denotes the automorphism of
L
induced by conjugation by
x
.

Let
\mathbf{P}
be an
F
–stable parabolic subgroup of
\mathbf{G}
with Levi factor
\mathbf{L}
.
Since
\mathbf{L}^{\prime}
is a Levi factor of the corresponding parabolic subgroup of
\mathbf{G}^{\prime}

and
\mathbf{G}
is normalized by
\mathbf{G}^{\prime}
, the automorphism
\sigma_{x}
preserves the pair

(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{L})
and transports Harish–Chandra induction. Thus

R_{\mathbf{L}}^{\mathbf{G}}(\tau^{\prime})\simeq R_{\mathbf{L}}^{\mathbf{G}}(\tau\circ\sigma_{x}^{-1})\simeq R_{\mathbf{L}}^{\mathbf{G}}(\tau)\circ\sigma_{x}^{-1}.

Twisting by the automorphism
\sigma_{x}
is an equivalence of the category of

G
–modules, and it preserves endomorphism algebras. Therefore

\operatorname{End}_{G}\bigl(R_{\mathbf{L}}^{\mathbf{G}}(\tau^{\prime})\bigr)\cong\operatorname{End}_{G}\bigl(R_{\mathbf{L}}^{\mathbf{G}}(\tau)\circ\sigma_{x}^{-1}\bigr)\cong\operatorname{End}_{G}\bigl(R_{\mathbf{L}}^{\mathbf{G}}(\tau)\bigr).

This proves the claim.
∎

6.
Harish–Chandra compatibility in the connected-centre case

In this section we record the Harish–Chandra compatibility of the
Digne–Michel Jordan decomposition in the connected-centre case.

Let
\mathbf{G}
be a connected reductive group over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
with connected centre, and
let
s\in G^{*}
be semisimple. We fix, once and for all, the unique
Jordan decomposition of Digne–Michel,

J_{s}^{G}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathcal{E}(G,s)\longrightarrow\operatorname{Uch}(H),\qquad H=C_{\mathbf{G}^{*}}(s)^{F^{*}},

as in Theorem
3.7
. If
\mathbf{L}
is an

F
-stable Levi factor of an
F
-stable parabolic subgroup of
\mathbf{G}
, then

Z(\mathbf{L})
is again connected. Hence the same theorem applies to
\mathbf{L}
and gives

J_{s}^{L}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathcal{E}(L,s)\longrightarrow\operatorname{Uch}(H_{L}),\qquad H_{L}=C_{\mathbf{L}^{*}}(s)^{F^{*}}.

Let
\tau\in\mathcal{E}(L,s)
be cuspidal and put

u_{\tau}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=J_{s}^{L}(\tau).

By Theorem
5.3
, there is a canonical
isomorphism of relative endomorphism algebras

\operatorname{End}_{G}\!\bigl(R_{\mathbf{L}}^{\mathbf{G}}(\tau)\bigr)\cong\operatorname{End}_{H}\!\bigl(R_{\mathbf{H}_{L}}^{\mathbf{H}}(u_{\tau})\bigr).

Consequently, this isomorphism induces a canonical bijection between the
corresponding Harish–Chandra series,

\mathbb{J}_{L,\tau}^{G}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\operatorname{Irr}(G,(L,\tau))\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname{Irr}(H,(H_{L},u_{\tau})).

Equivalently,

\mathbb{J}_{L,\tau}^{G}

is the bijection obtained by transporting the simple modules of the relative
endomorphism algebra on the
G
-side to those of the corresponding relative
endomorphism algebra on the centralizer side.
Using Proposition
4.2
, the Lusztig series

\mathcal{E}(G,s)
is the disjoint union of the Harish–Chandra series

\operatorname{Irr}(G,(L,\tau))
with
\tau\in\mathcal{E}(L,s)
cuspidal, taken up to

G
-conjugacy of cuspidal pairs. We therefore define

\mathbb{J}_{G,s}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathcal{E}(G,s)\longrightarrow\operatorname{Uch}(H)

by requiring

\mathbb{J}_{G,s}|_{\operatorname{Irr}(G,(L,\tau))}=\mathbb{J}_{L,\tau}^{G}.

From now on,
u_{\tau}=J_{s}^{L}(\tau)
where
J_{s}^{L}
is the unique Jordan decomposition as in Theorem
3.7
and
\mathbb{J}_{G,s}
is constructed as above using this collection of bijections at the cupidal levels.

Lemma 6.1

(The Harish–Chandra series map is a Jordan decomposition)
.

The map

\mathbb{J}_{G,s}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathcal{E}(G,s)\longrightarrow\operatorname{Uch}(H)

constructed above is a Jordan decomposition of
\mathcal{E}(G,s)
.

More precisely, for every
\rho\in\mathcal{E}(G,s)
one has

\mathbb{J}_{G,s}(\rho)=J_{s}^{G}(\rho)

unless the two characters lie in one of the exceptional pairs occurring in
Proposition
4.5
. In those exceptional cases the two
possible characters have the same degree and the same multiplicities in the
relevant Deligne–Lusztig virtual characters. Hence the possible interchange
inside such a pair does not affect the defining character-theoretic properties
of Jordan decomposition.

Proof.

We argue by induction on the semisimple rank of
\mathbf{G}
. The assertion is clear
for tori. By the usual reduction to simple adjoint factors, it is enough to
consider the case where
\mathbf{G}/Z(\mathbf{G})
is simple.

Let

\rho\in\operatorname{Irr}(G,(L,\tau))\subseteq\mathcal{E}(G,s),

and put

u_{\tau}=J_{s}^{L}(\tau),\qquad\eta=J_{s}^{G}(\rho),\qquad\eta^{\prime}=\mathbb{J}_{G,s}(\rho).

By the Harish–Chandra compatibility of the Digne–Michel Jordan decomposition

[
11
, Theorem 4.7.5]
, if

\rho\in\operatorname{Irr}(G,(L,\tau))\subseteq\mathcal{E}(G,s),

then

J_{s}^{G}(\rho)\in\operatorname{Irr}(H,(H_{L},J_{s}^{L}(\tau))).

Moreover, for every
F
-stable Levi subgroup
\mathbf{M}
with

\mathbf{L}\leq\mathbf{M}\leq\mathbf{G}
, one has

{}^{*}R_{\mathbf{H}_{M}}^{\mathbf{H}}\bigl(J_{s}^{G}(\rho)\bigr)=J_{s}^{M}\!\left({}^{*}R_{\mathbf{M}}^{\mathbf{G}}(\rho)\right).

By the induction hypothesis applied to
\mathbf{M}
, the right-hand side agrees with

\mathbb{J}_{M,s}\!\left({}^{*}R_{\mathbf{M}}^{\mathbf{G}}(\rho)\right),

up to the exceptional ambiguity described in
Proposition
4.5
. On the other hand, the construction of

\mathbb{J}_{G,s}
and the comparison theorem give

\mathbb{J}_{M,s}\!\left({}^{*}R_{\mathbf{M}}^{\mathbf{G}}(\rho)\right)={}^{*}R_{\mathbf{H}_{M}}^{\mathbf{H}}\bigl(\mathbb{J}_{G,s}(\rho)\bigr).

By the induction hypothesis applied to
\mathbf{M}
, the maps
J_{s}^{M}
and
\mathbb{J}_{M,s}

agree on the constituents of
{}^{*}R_{\mathbf{M}}^{\mathbf{G}}(\rho)
, except possibly for the
exceptional pairs of Proposition
4.5
. In the exceptional
cases, the two possible constituents have the same proper Harish–Chandra
restrictions and the same Deligne–Lusztig multiplicities, so the ambiguity is
invisible to the character-theoretic tests used below. Thus
J_{s}^{G}(\rho)
and

\mathbb{J}_{G,s}(\rho)
have the same Harish–Chandra restrictions to all proper
Levi subgroups of
\mathbf{H}
, up to precisely the exceptional ambiguity already
listed in Proposition
4.5
.

It remains to compare degrees. The degree formula for Jordan decomposition
[
11
, Theorem 3.2.18]
and
[
11
, Corollary 2.6.6]
,
together with the degree formula for the Howlett–Lehrer parametrization and
the parameter-preserving endomorphism algebra comparison in
Theorem
5.3
, gives

J_{s}^{G}(\rho)(1)=\mathbb{J}_{G,s}(\rho)(1).

Applying Proposition
4.5
to the connected reductive
group
\mathbf{H}
and the cuspidal pair
(H_{L},u_{\tau})
, we obtain

J_{s}^{G}(\rho)=\mathbb{J}_{G,s}(\rho)

unless the two characters lie in one of the exceptional pairs described in
that proposition.

In the remaining cases, the two possible characters have the same degree

512
in type
\mathsf{E}_{7}
and
4096
in type
\mathsf{E}_{8}
, and the two
members of each pair have the same multiplicities in the relevant
Deligne–Lusztig virtual characters. Therefore replacing one by the other
does not change the defining scalar-product identities for Jordan
decomposition. Hence
\mathbb{J}_{G,s}
is a Jordan decomposition.
∎

By Lemma
6.1
, the map

\mathbb{J}_{G,s}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathcal{E}(G,s)\longrightarrow\operatorname{Uch}(H)

constructed above from the Harish–Chandra series bijections is a Jordan
decomposition. We shall use this Jordan decomposition in the following
compatibility statement.

Proposition 6.2
.

Let
\mathbf{G}
be a connected reductive group over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
with a connected centre.
Let
s\in G^{*}
be any semisimple element and
\mathbf{L}^{*}\leq\mathbf{G}^{*}
be an
F^{*}
-stable Levi factor of an
F^{*}
-stable parabolic subgroup of
\mathbf{G}^{*}
containing
s
with dual
\mathbf{L}
.
Let
\mathbb{J}_{G,s}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathcal{E}(G,s)\rightarrow\operatorname{Uch}(H)
be the Jordan decomposition as above. Then the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{E}(G,s)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}\operatorname{Uch}(H)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{E}(M,s)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}\operatorname{Uch}(H_{M}),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{J}_{G,s}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{J}_{M,s}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{\mathbf{M}}^{\mathbf{G}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{\mathbf{H}_{M}}^{\mathbf{H}}}

where
\mathbf{L}^{*}\leq\mathbf{M}^{*}\leq\mathbf{G}^{*}
are
F^{*}
-stable Levi subgroups of
F^{*}
-stable parabolic subgroups of
\mathbf{G}^{*}
.

Proof.

The proposition follows from Theorem
4.4
and the definition of bijections
\mathbb{J}_{G,s}
and
\mathbb{J}_{M,s}
.
∎

7.
Preferred extensions of cuspidal unipotent characters

7.1.
Preferred extensions in the quasisimple case

Let
\mathbf{H}^{\circ}
be a connected reductive group over
\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}

equipped with a Frobenius endomorphism
F
, and put
H^{\circ}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathbf{H}^{\circ F}
.
Let
\mathbf{H}
be an
F
–stable (possibly disconnected) algebraic group with identity component

\mathbf{H}^{\circ}
such that
\pi_{0}(\mathbf{H})\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathbf{H}/\mathbf{H}^{\circ}
is abelian, and set

H\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathbf{H}^{F}
.
Let
u^{\circ}\in\operatorname{Irr}(H^{\circ})
be a cuspidal unipotent character, and write

I\ \mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\ I_{H}(u^{\circ})\ =\ \{\,h\in H\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\ {}^{h}u^{\circ}\simeq u^{\circ}\,\}.

We shall use the word
canonical
in this section in the following
pinned-normalized sense. For a fixed pinning, the component action on the based
root datum is fixed. If an invariant cuspidal unipotent character admits
several extensions to its inertia group, those extensions differ by the usual
characters of the component quotient. Lusztig’s preferred-extension
normalization, recalled below, selects one of them without any further auxiliary
choice. Thus the extensions constructed here are canonical relative to the
fixed pinning and to Lusztig’s preferred-extension convention; this is the sense
in which they are used later in the Jordan decomposition.

The semidirect-product notation in this section is used only when a pinned
semidirect component model, in the sense of
Definition
2.3
, has been specified. In
the centralizer situations needed later, this model is supplied by
Lemma
2.2
; no assertion is being made
that an arbitrary disconnected reductive group splits over its identity
component.

Lemma 7.1

(Existence of preferred extensions on cyclic subgroups)
.

Let
\mathbf{H}^{\circ}
be a quasisimple adjoint algebraic group with Frobenius
F
and put

H^{\circ}=\mathbf{H}^{\circ F}
. Let
\mathbf{H}
be an
F
–stable (possibly disconnected) algebraic
group with identity component
\mathbf{H}^{\circ}
and set
H=\mathbf{H}^{F}
.
Let
u^{\circ}\in\operatorname{Irr}(H^{\circ})
be a cuspidal unipotent character, and let

I\ \mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\ I_{H}(u^{\circ})=\{\,h\in H\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\ {}^{h}u^{\circ}\simeq u^{\circ}\,\}.

Fix a cyclic subgroup
C\leq I/H^{\circ}
, and let
H_{C}\leq H
be its inverse image.
Assume that
H_{C}
has a pinned semidirect component model

H_{C}=H^{\circ}\rtimes A_{C},\qquad A_{C}\cong C,

in the sense of Definition
2.3
.

Then there exists an extension
u_{C}\in\operatorname{Irr}(H_{C})
of
u^{\circ}
to
H_{C}
which is
canonical once one fixes Lusztig’s preferred extension convention for Weyl-group representations.

Proof.

Choose an element
\sigma\in A_{C}
whose image generates
C
. By the pinned
semidirect component model,

H_{C}=H^{\circ}\rtimes\langle\sigma\rangle.

This is the only point in the proof where a semidirect-product decomposition is
used; for semisimple centralizers it is supplied by
Lemma
2.2
. Since
C\leq I/H^{\circ}
, the
character
u^{\circ}
is
H_{C}
–stable, hence extends to
H_{C}
(cyclic-quotient
extension criterion, e.g.
[
14
, Cor. 11.22]
); different extensions correspond to multiplying the
intertwiner
T_{\sigma}
by a
|C|
-th root of unity.

Lusztig’s preferred extension on the Weyl-group side.

Let
W
be the Weyl group of
\mathbf{H}^{\circ}
.
The automorphism
\sigma
induces an automorphism (still denoted)
\sigma
of the based root datum,
hence of
W
, and we form the semidirect product
C_{c}W\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=W\rtimes\langle\sigma\rangle
where

c=|\sigma|
.
Lusztig shows that if an irreducible
W
–module
E
is extendable to
C_{c}W
, then it has
c

extensions, and he
singles out one
such extension and calls it the
preferred extension
,
defined case-by-case and functorially with respect to products.
[
18
, §17.2]

More generally, for an abelian group acting on
W
, Lusztig constructs canonical intertwiners

\sigma\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}E\to E
that realize the preferred extensions and build an extension of
E
to the relevant
semidirect product.
[
18
, §17.3]

Transfer from the Weyl-group side.

Let
c=|\sigma|
.
Fix an irreducible representation
(\rho,V)
of
H^{\circ}
affording
u^{\circ}
.
Since
u^{\circ}
is
H_{C}
–stable, there exists an intertwiner

T_{\sigma}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}V\to V
with

T_{\sigma}\,\rho(h)\,T_{\sigma}^{-1}\;=\;\rho(\sigma(h))\qquad(h\in H^{\circ}),

unique up to scalar by Schur’s lemma. Choosing
T_{\sigma}
with
T_{\sigma}^{c}=1

is equivalent to choosing an extension of
u^{\circ}
to
H_{C}
, and different
extensions correspond to multiplying
T_{\sigma}
by a
c
-th root of unity.

On the geometric side,
u^{\circ}
lies in a (generalized) Springer block
I

and corresponds to a
\sigma
–stable Weyl-group character
\varphi

(of a suitable relative Weyl group
W
, in particular
\varphi
is extendable
to
W\rtimes\langle\sigma\rangle
). By
[
6
, §2]
(following Lusztig’s
construction of characteristic functions from
F
–stable pairs, cf.

[
17
, 24.1–24.2]
), an extension
\tilde{\varphi}
of
\varphi
to

W\rtimes\langle\sigma\rangle
canonically determines the corresponding

\sigma
–equivariant structure on the local system in the block, hence fixes
the scalar by which
\sigma
acts on the resulting function/representation.
Equivalently, it fixes the scalar ambiguity in the intertwiner
T_{\sigma}

above, and therefore picks out a unique extension of
u^{\circ}
to
H_{C}
.

We now take
\tilde{\varphi}
to be Lusztig’s preferred extension

[
18
, §17.2–§17.3]
. The resulting intertwiner
T_{\sigma}
(hence the
corresponding extension) is then canonical once the preferred-extension
convention is fixed; denote this extension by
u_{C}\in\operatorname{Irr}(H_{C})
.

∎

Lemma 7.2

(Gluing preferred extensions in the non-cyclic abelian case)
.

Let
u^{\circ}\in\operatorname{Irr}(H^{\circ})
be
I
-invariant, and put

\Omega\ \mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\ I/H^{\circ}.

Assume that
\Omega
is non-cyclic abelian and that a decomposition

\Omega\ \cong\ \Gamma\times\Phi

has been fixed with
\Gamma
and
\Phi
cyclic. Let

I_{\Gamma},\ I_{\Phi}\ \leq\ I

be the inverse images of
\Gamma
and
\Phi
under the quotient map

I\twoheadrightarrow\Omega
.

Assume that:

(1)

there is an extension
u_{\Gamma}\in\operatorname{Irr}(I_{\Gamma})
of
u^{\circ}
which is

\Phi
-invariant (equivalently,
I
-invariant, since
I/I_{\Gamma}\simeq\Phi
);

(2)

there is an extension
u_{\Phi}\in\operatorname{Irr}(I_{\Phi})
of
u^{\circ}
.

Then there exists a unique extension
\widetilde{u}\in\operatorname{Irr}(I)
of
u^{\circ}
such that

\widetilde{u}|_{I_{\Gamma}}=u_{\Gamma},\qquad\widetilde{u}|_{I_{\Phi}}=u_{\Phi}.

In particular, if
u_{\Gamma}
and
u_{\Phi}
are canonically determined, then so is

\widetilde{u}
.

Proof.

Since
I_{\Gamma}\triangleleft I
and
I/I_{\Gamma}\simeq\Phi
is cyclic, the

\Phi
-invariance of
u_{\Gamma}
implies, by the cyclic-quotient extension criterion
(e.g.
[
14
, Cor. 11.22]
), that
u_{\Gamma}
extends to some

\widetilde{u}_{0}\in\operatorname{Irr}(I)
.

We claim that
\widetilde{u}_{0}|_{I_{\Phi}}
is irreducible. Indeed, let

\rho\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}I\to\operatorname{GL}(V)
afford
\widetilde{u}_{0}
. Since
\widetilde{u}_{0}
extends

u^{\circ}
, the restriction
\rho|_{H^{\circ}}
is irreducible. Any
I_{\Phi}
-stable
subspace of
V
is in particular
H^{\circ}
-stable, hence must be
0
or
V
.
Thus
\rho|_{I_{\Phi}}
is irreducible, so
\widetilde{u}_{0}|_{I_{\Phi}}\in\operatorname{Irr}(I_{\Phi})
,
and it extends
u^{\circ}
.

Now
I_{\Phi}/H^{\circ}\simeq\Phi
is abelian, and both

\widetilde{u}_{0}|_{I_{\Phi}}
and
u_{\Phi}
lie above
u^{\circ}
. By Clifford theory,
there is therefore a unique linear character
\lambda_{\Phi}\in\operatorname{Irr}(\Phi)
such that

\widetilde{u}_{0}|_{I_{\Phi}}\;=\;u_{\Phi}\otimes\widehat{\lambda_{\Phi}},

where
\widehat{\lambda_{\Phi}}
denotes the inflation of
\lambda_{\Phi}
to
I_{\Phi}
.

Let
\lambda\in\operatorname{Irr}(\Omega)
be the inflation of
\lambda_{\Phi}
along the projection

\Omega=\Gamma\times\Phi\twoheadrightarrow\Phi
, and let
\widehat{\lambda}
be its
inflation to
I
. Set

\widetilde{u}\ \mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\ \widetilde{u}_{0}\otimes\widehat{\lambda}^{-1}.

Because
\widehat{\lambda}
is trivial on
I_{\Gamma}
, we have

\widetilde{u}|_{I_{\Gamma}}=\widetilde{u}_{0}|_{I_{\Gamma}}=u_{\Gamma}.

Because
\widehat{\lambda}|_{I_{\Phi}}=\widehat{\lambda_{\Phi}}
, we obtain

\widetilde{u}|_{I_{\Phi}}=\bigl(\widetilde{u}_{0}|_{I_{\Phi}}\bigr)\otimes\widehat{\lambda_{\Phi}}^{-1}=u_{\Phi}.

Thus
\widetilde{u}
is the required extension of
u^{\circ}
.

For uniqueness, let
\widetilde{u}_{1},\widetilde{u}_{2}\in\operatorname{Irr}(I)
be two extensions of

u^{\circ}
with the same restrictions to
I_{\Gamma}
and
I_{\Phi}
. Since

\Omega=I/H^{\circ}
is abelian and both characters lie above
u^{\circ}
, Clifford theory
gives

\widetilde{u}_{2}\ =\ \widetilde{u}_{1}\otimes\widehat{\mu}

for some
\mu\in\operatorname{Irr}(\Omega)
. Restricting to
I_{\Gamma}
shows that

\mu|_{\Gamma}=1
, and restricting to
I_{\Phi}
shows that
\mu|_{\Phi}=1
.
Since
\Omega=\Gamma\times\Phi
, it follows that
\mu=1
, hence

\widetilde{u}_{1}=\widetilde{u}_{2}
.

Therefore the extension
\widetilde{u}
is unique. The final assertion on canonicity is
immediate.
∎

Lemma 7.3

(
\Phi
-stable preferred extension along the graph factor)
.

Assume that
\mathbf{H}^{\circ}
is simple adjoint. Assume that

\Omega\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=H/H^{\circ}
is non-cyclic abelian with decomposition

\Omega\ \cong\ \Gamma\times\Phi

where
\Gamma
and
\Phi
are cyclic. Assume moreover that this decomposition is realized by a
pinned semidirect component model

H=H^{\circ}\rtimes(\Gamma\times\Phi).

Set
K\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=H^{\circ}\rtimes\Gamma\lhd H
. Then there exists a canonically preferred extension
\tilde{u}_{\Gamma}\in\operatorname{Irr}(K)
of
u^{\circ}
which is
\Phi
–invariant.

Proof.

For a quasisimple adjoint
\mathbf{H}^{\circ}
, the only Lie types in which the relevant
outer action can contribute a non-cyclic abelian inertia quotient are the three families
appearing in Malle’s reduction in
[
21
, Prop. 2.2]
, namely:

A_{n-1}\ (n\geq 3),\qquad D_{n}\ (n\geq 4),\qquad E_{6}.

In type
A_{n-1}
(equivalently
L_{n}(q)
with
n\geq 3
) there are
no
cuspidal unipotent
characters, so there is nothing to prove.

Thus we may assume
\mathbf{H}^{\circ}
is of type
D_{n}
or
E_{6}
.
By the pinned semidirect component model in the statement, the decomposition

\Omega\cong\Gamma\times\Phi
is represented inside
H
and we may write

H\ =\ H^{\circ}\rtimes(\Gamma\times\Phi),

where
\Gamma
is generated by a (quasi-central) graph automorphism of order
r\in\{2,3\}
,
and
\Phi
is cyclic and commutes with
\Gamma
(“field part” in the sense of Malle).
Set
K\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=H^{\circ}\rtimes\Gamma\lhd H
.

The argument is identical to the one used by Malle in the proof of
[
21
, Prop. 2.3]

in the present cuspidal-unipotent situation: in each of the cases

D_{4}
(i.e.
O_{8}^{+}(q)
),
E_{6}(q)
, and
D_{n}(q)
with
n=s^{2}
(
s
even),
he produces a
\Phi
–stable virtual character
R
of
K
(a suitable generalized
Deligne–Lusztig character for the disconnected group)
whose decomposition into irreducibles separates the different extensions of
u^{\circ}

to
K
by
pairwise distinct multiplicities
. More precisely:

•

In type
D_{4}
, one takes
R=R^{G\sigma}_{T\sigma}(1)
in the disconnected group

G\rtimes\langle\sigma\rangle
; its decomposition contains the
r
extensions of the cuspidal
unipotent character with
different multiplicities
, and
T^{F}
is stabilized by field
automorphisms, hence
R
is
\Phi
–stable
[
21
, Prop. 2.3]
.

•

In type
E_{6}
, the proof of
[
21
, Prop. 2.3]
similarly uses an explicit
decomposition of a suitable generalized Deligne–Lusztig character in
G\rtimes\langle\gamma\rangle

showing that the extensions occur with different multiplicities.

•

In type
D_{n}
(
n=s^{2}
with
s
even), Malle takes a
\gamma
– and
F
–stable Levi
L
,
extends a suitable unipotent character
\rho_{1}
to
\widetilde{L}=L\rtimes\langle\gamma\rangle
,
and applies
[
8
, Cor. 2.4]
to obtain a
\Phi
–stable virtual character

R=R^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{L}}(\widetilde{\rho}_{1})
in which the two extensions of
u^{\circ}

to
K
occur with distinct multiplicities
[
21
, Prop. 2.3]
.

Now write the resulting
\Phi
–stable virtual character as

R\;=\;\sum_{\eta\in\operatorname{Irr}(K)}m_{\eta}\,\eta.

Since
R
is
\Phi
–stable, the multiplicity
m_{\eta}
is constant on
\Phi
–orbits.
Since the irreducible constituents
\eta
lying above
u^{\circ}
occur with
pairwise distinct

multiplicities (as stated in
[
21
, Prop. 2.3]
), there is a unique such constituent with
maximal
m_{\eta}
; denote it by
\tilde{u}_{\Gamma}
. Its
\Phi
–orbit must be trivial (otherwise an
orbit would contain two constituents with the same multiplicity), hence
\tilde{u}_{\Gamma}
is

\Phi
–invariant. This is the canonical choice used below.

∎

Lemma 7.4

(Cuspidal unipotent invariance on quasi-simple factors)
.

Let
\mathbf{X}
be a connected quasi-simple algebraic group over

\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
, equipped with a Frobenius endomorphism
F
, and
put
X=\mathbf{X}^{F}
. If
\chi\in\operatorname{Uch}(X)
is cuspidal unipotent, then
\chi
is invariant under all automorphisms of
X
.
In particular, for any subgroup
A\leq\operatorname{Aut}(X)
one has
I_{A}(\chi)=A
.

Proof.

As recalled by Malle, in the classical types Lusztig’s parametrisation of unipotent characters by
symbols implies that a classical group has
at most one
cuspidal unipotent character (see
Table 1 in
[
22
, §2.1]
); consequently this cuspidal unipotent character is fixed
by
all
automorphisms of
G
.
For groups of exceptional type, see the proof in
[
22
, Thm. 3.2]
) and
[
21
, Thm. 2.5]
.
Together these references yield that any cuspidal unipotent character of
X=\mathbf{X}^{F}
is

\operatorname{Aut}(X)
–invariant.

∎

We recall a standard fact from the literature in the form of a Lemma.

Lemma 7.5
.

Let
\mathbf{G}
be a connected reductive group defined over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
with Frobenius
F
.
If
\chi\in\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{G}^{F},(1))
is a unipotent character (in particular, a cuspidal unipotent
character), then
Z(\mathbf{G})^{F}\subseteq\ker(\chi)
. Equivalently, the central character of
\chi

is trivial.

Proof.

Let
\pi\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbf{G}\to\mathbf{G}_{\operatorname{ad}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathbf{G}/Z(\mathbf{G})
be the adjoint quotient. Then
\pi

is defined over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
, has central kernel
Z(\mathbf{G})
, and is surjective, hence

\pi(\mathbf{G})
contains the derived group
(\mathbf{G}_{\operatorname{ad}})^{\prime}
.
Therefore
[
9
, Prop. 13.20]
applies to
\pi
and shows that every unipotent character of

\mathbf{G}^{F}
is of the form
\bar{\chi}\circ\pi^{F}
with
\bar{\chi}
a unipotent character of

\mathbf{G}_{\operatorname{ad}}^{F}
.
In particular,
\chi
is inflated from
\mathbf{G}_{\operatorname{ad}}^{F}
, so
\ker(\pi^{F})=Z(\mathbf{G})^{F}

acts trivially; hence
Z(\mathbf{G})^{F}\subseteq\ker(\chi)
, i.e. the central character is trivial.
∎

We now combine the preceding lemmas in the form needed later. For the construction of the
canonical Jordan decomposition, it is enough to know that a cuspidal unipotent character admits a
canonically determined extension across an abelian component group.

Proposition 7.6

(Canonical extension in the abelian component case)
.

Let
\mathbf{H}^{\circ}
be a quasisimple algebraic group over
\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}

equipped with a Frobenius endomorphism
F
, and set
H^{\circ}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathbf{H}^{\circ F}
.
Let
\mathbf{H}
be an
F
–stable (possibly disconnected) algebraic group with identity component

\mathbf{H}^{\circ}
, and put
H\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathbf{H}^{F}
. Assume that the component group

\pi_{0}(\mathbf{H})=\mathbf{H}/\mathbf{H}^{\circ}

is abelian, so that
H/H^{\circ}
is abelian as well. Assume moreover that
H
has a pinned semidirect
component model in the sense of Definition
2.3
. By
Remark
2.4
, this extra hypothesis is automatic for the
semisimple centralizers to which the proposition is applied below. Let

u^{\circ}\in\operatorname{Irr}(H^{\circ})

be a cuspidal unipotent character. Then there exists a canonically determined extension

\widetilde{u}\in\operatorname{Irr}(H)

of
u^{\circ}
to
H
.

We shall refer to this canonically determined extension as the
preferred extension
of

u^{\circ}
to
H
.

Proof.

Let

\pi\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbf{H}\longrightarrow\overline{\mathbf{H}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathbf{H}/Z(\mathbf{H}^{\circ})

be the quotient by the center of
\mathbf{H}^{\circ}
. Then

\overline{\mathbf{H}}^{\,\circ}=\mathbf{H}^{\circ}/Z(\mathbf{H}^{\circ})

is adjoint, and

\overline{\mathbf{H}}/\overline{\mathbf{H}}^{\,\circ}\;\cong\;\mathbf{H}/\mathbf{H}^{\circ},

so the component group is unchanged. The chosen pinned semidirect component model for
H

maps to a pinned semidirect component model for
\overline{H}
with the same component quotient.
By Lemma
7.5
,
the cuspidal unipotent character
u^{\circ}
is trivial on
Z(\mathbf{H}^{\circ})^{F}
, hence is the
inflation of a cuspidal unipotent character
\overline{u}^{\circ}\in\operatorname{Irr}(\overline{H}^{\circ})
, where

\overline{H}^{\circ}=\overline{\mathbf{H}}^{\,\circ F}
. Any extension of
\overline{u}^{\circ}
to

\overline{H}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\overline{\mathbf{H}}^{\,F}
inflates to an extension of
u^{\circ}
to
H
.
Therefore, for the existence and canonicity statement, we may replace
\mathbf{H}
by

\overline{\mathbf{H}}
and hence assume from the outset that
\mathbf{H}^{\circ}
is simple adjoint.

Set

\Omega\ \mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\ H/H^{\circ}.

By Lemma
7.4
, the character
u^{\circ}
is fixed by all
automorphisms of
H^{\circ}
, hence

I_{H}(u^{\circ})=H.

If
\Omega
is cyclic, then applying
Lemma
7.1
with
C=\Omega
yields a canonical
extension

\widetilde{u}\in\operatorname{Irr}(H)

of
u^{\circ}
. This settles the cyclic case.

Assume now that
\Omega
is non-cyclic abelian. By Malle’s reduction
(
[
21
, Prop. 2.2]
), the only simple adjoint types for which such a non-cyclic abelian
component quotient can occur in the present setting are

A_{n-1}\ (n\geq 3),\qquad D_{n}\ (n\geq 4),\qquad E_{6}.

In type
A_{n-1}
there are no cuspidal unipotent characters, so this case does not arise.

Thus
\mathbf{H}^{\circ}
is of type
D_{n}
or
E_{6}
. Again by the same reduction, after fixing
commuting representatives of the component action, we may choose a decomposition

\Omega\ \cong\ \Gamma\times\Phi

with
\Gamma
and
\Phi
cyclic, where
\Gamma
is the graph part and
\Phi
the cyclic
field part. Let

I_{\Gamma},\ I_{\Phi}\leq H

denote the inverse images of
\Gamma
and
\Phi
under the quotient map
H\twoheadrightarrow\Omega
.

By Lemma
7.3
, there exists a canonically preferred
extension

u_{\Gamma}\in\operatorname{Irr}(I_{\Gamma})

of
u^{\circ}
which is
\Phi
-invariant. By
Lemma
7.1
, applied to the cyclic subgroup

\Phi\leq\Omega
, there exists a canonical extension

u_{\Phi}\in\operatorname{Irr}(I_{\Phi})

of
u^{\circ}
.

Now Lemma
7.2
applies and yields a unique extension

\widetilde{u}\in\operatorname{Irr}(H)

of
u^{\circ}
such that

\widetilde{u}|_{I_{\Gamma}}=u_{\Gamma},\qquad\widetilde{u}|_{I_{\Phi}}=u_{\Phi}.

Since
u_{\Gamma}
and
u_{\Phi}
are canonical, the character
\widetilde{u}
is canonical as
well.

Hence in all cases
u^{\circ}
admits a preferred extension to
H
.
∎

7.2.
Pinned representatives for component actions

Fix a pinning
\mathcal{P}
of
G
and hence a pinning of
G^{*}
and of
H^{\circ}=C_{G^{*}}(s)^{\circ}
.
Write
\mathcal{P}_{H^{\circ}}
for this pinning. Let
\operatorname{Aut}(H^{\circ},\mathcal{P}_{H^{\circ}})
denote automorphisms of
the algebraic group
H^{\circ}
preserving the pinning; it is canonically identified with the
automorphism group of the based root datum of
H^{\circ}
.

Lemma 7.7

(Pinned representatives of the component action)
.

Let
H=C_{G^{*}}(s)
and
\Omega\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=H/H^{\circ}
. Conjugation by
H
induces a homomorphism

\Omega\to\operatorname{Out}(H^{\circ})
. The pinning
\mathcal{P}_{H^{\circ}}
provides a canonical section

\operatorname{Out}(H^{\circ})\to\operatorname{Aut}(H^{\circ},\mathcal{P}_{H^{\circ}})
. Composing, we obtain a canonical homomorphism

\Omega\ \longrightarrow\ \operatorname{Aut}(H^{\circ},\mathcal{P}_{H^{\circ}}),\qquad\omega\ \longmapsto\ \sigma_{\omega},

whose image consists of pinned automorphisms of
H^{\circ}
representing the outer action class of
\omega
.

Proof.

Self evident.
∎

7.3.
Permutation factors and wreath-product extensions

Lemma 7.8

(Canonical wreath-product extension)
.

Let
K
be a finite group and
\theta\in\operatorname{Irr}(K)
. For
m\geq 1
, put
K^{m}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=K\times\cdots\times K
and

\theta^{\boxtimes m}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\theta\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes\theta\in\operatorname{Irr}(K^{m})
. Let
\mathfrak{S}_{m}
act on
K^{m}

by permuting factors and form the semidirect product
K\wr\mathfrak{S}_{m}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=K^{m}\rtimes\mathfrak{S}_{m}
.

Then
\theta^{\boxtimes m}
has a
canonical
extension
\widetilde{\theta}^{(m)}\in\operatorname{Irr}(K\wr\mathfrak{S}_{m})

characterized as follows: if
(\rho,V)
affords
\theta
, then
K^{m}
acts on
V^{\otimes m}
by the tensor
product representation and
\mathfrak{S}_{m}
acts by permuting tensor factors:

\sigma\cdot(v_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{m})\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=v_{\sigma^{-1}(1)}\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{\sigma^{-1}(m)}.

This yields a representation of
K\wr\mathfrak{S}_{m}
on
V^{\otimes m}
whose character is
\widetilde{\theta}^{(m)}
.
Moreover,

\operatorname{Res}^{K\wr\mathfrak{S}_{m}}_{K^{m}}\widetilde{\theta}^{(m)}=\theta^{\boxtimes m}\qquad\text{and hence}\qquad\bigl\langle\operatorname{Res}^{K\wr\mathfrak{S}_{m}}_{K^{m}}\widetilde{\theta}^{(m)},\ \theta^{\boxtimes m}\bigr\rangle_{K^{m}}=1.

Proof.

Let
(\rho,V)
afford
\theta
. With the actions described in the statement,

V^{\otimes m}
becomes a representation of
K\wr\mathfrak{S}_{m}
, because the
permutation action of
\mathfrak{S}_{m}
on tensor factors satisfies the semidirect
product relation

\sigma(k_{1},\dots,k_{m})\sigma^{-1}=(k_{\sigma^{-1}(1)},\dots,k_{\sigma^{-1}(m)}).

Its restriction to
K^{m}
is exactly the external tensor product

\theta^{\boxtimes m}
.

Since
\theta\in\operatorname{Irr}(K)
, the character
\theta^{\boxtimes m}
is irreducible on

K^{m}
. As
K^{m}\triangleleft K\wr\mathfrak{S}_{m}
, any nonzero

(K\wr\mathfrak{S}_{m})
-submodule of
V^{\otimes m}
is in particular a nonzero

K^{m}
-submodule, hence must be all of
V^{\otimes m}
. Therefore the above
representation is irreducible, and its character

\widetilde{\theta}^{(m)}\in\operatorname{Irr}(K\wr\mathfrak{S}_{m})
extends

\theta^{\boxtimes m}
.

The equality

\operatorname{Res}^{K\wr\mathfrak{S}_{m}}_{K^{m}}\widetilde{\theta}^{(m)}=\theta^{\boxtimes m}

is built into the construction, so the inner product statement follows
immediately. Finally, the construction is basis-free and functorial in
(\rho,V)
,
so the resulting character depends only on
\theta
; hence the extension is canonical.
∎

7.4.
The general reductive case

Proposition 7.9

(Preferred characters arising out of a cuspidal unipotent of the identity component)
.

Let
\mathbf{H}^{\circ}
be a connected reductive group over
\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
equipped with a
Frobenius endomorphism
F
, and put
H^{\circ}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathbf{H}^{\circ F}
.
Let
\mathbf{H}
be an
F
–stable (possibly disconnected) algebraic group with identity component

\mathbf{H}^{\circ}
and set
H\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathbf{H}^{F}
.
Assume that
\pi_{0}(\mathbf{H})\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathbf{H}/\mathbf{H}^{\circ}
is abelian (hence
H/H^{\circ}
is abelian),
and assume that
H
has a pinned semidirect component model in the sense of
Definition
2.3
. This holds for
F
-stable
semisimple centralizers by Remark
2.4
.
Let
u^{\circ}\in\operatorname{Irr}(H^{\circ})
be a cuspidal unipotent character and let

I\ \mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\ I_{H}(u^{\circ})\ =\ \{\,h\in H\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\ {}^{h}u^{\circ}\simeq u^{\circ}\,\}.

Then
u^{\circ}
admits a preferred extension to
I
.
In particular, if
u^{\circ}
is
H
–invariant (equivalently
I=H
), then
u^{\circ}
admits a preferred
extension to
H
.

Proof.

The pinned semidirect component model for
H
restricts to
I
, by intersecting the chosen
complement with the inverse image of
I/H^{\circ}
. Hence, replacing
H
by
I
and using this
restricted model, we may and do assume that
u^{\circ}
is
H
–invariant, so that

\Omega\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=H/H^{\circ}
is abelian and
u^{\circ}
is
\Omega
–stable.

Since
u^{\circ}
is unipotent cuspidal, it factors through the adjoint quotient, so we may assume

\mathbf{H}^{\circ}
is semisimple of adjoint type. Decompose

H^{\circ}\ \cong\ \prod_{a\in\mathcal{A}}X_{a}

as a direct product of finite groups of Lie type
X_{a}=\mathbf{X}_{a}^{F}
coming from connected almost
simple adjoint algebraic groups
\mathbf{X}_{a}
, possibly with repetitions.
Then every unipotent character of
H^{\circ}
is an external tensor product, and cuspidality forces
each factor to be cuspidal; thus we may write

u^{\circ}\ =\ \boxtimes_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\chi_{a},\qquad\chi_{a}\in\operatorname{Uch}(X_{a})\ \text{cuspidal unipotent.}

For each isomorphism type
X
occurring among the
X_{a}
, and each cuspidal unipotent character

\chi\in\operatorname{Uch}(X)
, let
m_{X,\chi}
be the multiplicity of the pair
(X,\chi)
among the factors

\{(X_{a},\chi_{a})\}_{a\in\mathcal{A}}
. Reordering factors gives a canonical refinement

(7.1)

H^{\circ}\ \cong\ \prod_{(X,\chi)}X^{m_{X,\chi}},\qquad u^{\circ}\ =\ \boxtimes_{(X,\chi)}\chi^{\boxtimes m_{X,\chi}},

where
(X,\chi)
runs over the finite set of pairs consisting of an absolute quasi-simple factor
X
and a
cuspidal unipotent character
\chi
of
X
which appears in
u^{\circ}
.

Conjugation by
H
induces an action of
\Omega
on the set of almost simple direct factors of

H^{\circ}
. Since
\Omega
stabilizes
u^{\circ}
, it cannot permute a factor carrying
(X,\chi)
into
a factor carrying
(X,\chi^{\prime})
with
\chi^{\prime}\neq\chi
, because that would send
u^{\circ}
to a distinct
tensor product character. Hence the action of
\Omega
preserves each block
X^{m_{X,\chi}}
in
(
7.1
), and even preserves the partition of the
m_{X,\chi}
copies by the label

\chi
. Consequently, for each pair
(X,\chi)
the action of
\Omega
on the block

X^{m_{X,\chi}}
factors through an abelian subgroup

\Omega_{X,\chi}\ \leq\ \operatorname{Aut}(X)\wr\mathfrak{S}_{m_{X,\chi}},

whose permutation image lies in the Young subgroup permuting only the
m_{X,\chi}
identical

\chi
–factors.

Moreover, by Lemma
7.4
,
\chi
is invariant under
all

automorphisms of
X
. In particular, the character
\chi^{\boxtimes m_{X,\chi}}
is stabilized by

\Omega_{X,\chi}
.

Fix a pair
(X,\chi)
and put
m\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=m_{X,\chi}
.
Let
A_{X,\chi}
be the (abelian) projection of
\Omega_{X,\chi}
to
\operatorname{Aut}(X)
(i.e. the “outer”
part acting on a single factor), and let
P_{X,\chi}
be the (abelian) permutation image in

\mathfrak{S}_{m}
.
Then the extension
\widetilde{\chi}
of
\chi
across the automorphism part
A_{X,\chi}
follows from Proposition
7.6
, i.e., the disconnected group
\mathbf{X}\rtimes A_{X,\chi}
(whose component group is
abelian) yields a preferred extension

\widetilde{\chi}\ \in\ \operatorname{Irr}(X\rtimes A_{X,\chi})\qquad\text{with}\qquad\operatorname{Res}^{X\rtimes A_{X,\chi}}_{X}\widetilde{\chi}=\chi.

Consider the wreath product

(X\rtimes A_{X,\chi})\wr\mathfrak{S}_{m}\ \mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\ (X\rtimes A_{X,\chi})^{m}\rtimes\mathfrak{S}_{m}.

By Lemma
7.8
(applied with
K=X\rtimes A_{X,\chi}
and
\theta=\widetilde{\chi}
),
the character
\widetilde{\chi}^{\boxtimes m}\in\operatorname{Irr}\bigl((X\rtimes A_{X,\chi})^{m}\bigr)
has a
canonical extension to
(X\rtimes A_{X,\chi})\wr\mathfrak{S}_{m}
.
Restrict this canonical extension to the subgroup

X^{m}\rtimes\Omega_{X,\chi}\ \leq\ (X\rtimes A_{X,\chi})\wr\mathfrak{S}_{m}

(realizing
\Omega_{X,\chi}
via its given action on the
m
copies). Denote the resulting
character by
\widetilde{u}_{X,\chi}\in\operatorname{Irr}(X^{m}\rtimes\Omega_{X,\chi})
.

Irreducibility after restriction.

Note that
X^{m}\triangleleft X^{m}\rtimes\Omega_{X,\chi}
and
\operatorname{Res}^{\,X^{m}\rtimes\Omega_{X,\chi}}_{X^{m}}\tilde{u}_{X,\chi}=\chi^{\boxtimes m}\in\operatorname{Irr}(X^{m})
. Thus any nonzero
(X^{m}\rtimes\Omega_{X,\chi})
–submodule is a nonzero
X^{m}
–submodule, so
\tilde{u}_{X,\chi}
is irreducible.
By construction,

\operatorname{Res}^{X^{m}\rtimes\Omega_{X,\chi}}_{X^{m}}\widetilde{u}_{X,\chi}=\chi^{\boxtimes m},

and
\widetilde{u}_{X,\chi}
is canonical once the preferred-extension and wreath-extension
conventions are fixed.

Now, form the external tensor product over all pairs
(X,\chi)
appearing in (
7.1
):

\widetilde{u}\ \mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\ \boxtimes_{(X,\chi)}\widetilde{u}_{X,\chi}.

Then
\widetilde{u}
is the desired canonical extension of
u^{\circ}
to
H
.

∎

Theorem 7.10

(Pinned canonical extension of cuspidal unipotents)
.

Let
G
be a connected reductive group over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
, let
s\in G^{*}
be semisimple, and put
H\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=C_{G^{*}}(s)
with identity component
H^{\circ}
. Fix a pinning
\mathcal{P}
of
G
, and let
\mathcal{P}_{H}
be the induced pinning of
H^{\circ}
. Let
u_{\rho}^{\circ}\in\operatorname{Uch}(H^{\circ})
be a cuspidal unipotent character, and set

I\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\operatorname{Stab}_{H}(u_{\rho}^{\circ}).

Then the pinning
\mathcal{P}
, together with Lusztig’s preferred-extension
normalization fixed above, determines a distinguished extension

\dot{u}_{\rho}\in\operatorname{Irr}(I_{H}(u^{\circ}))

of
u^{\circ}
. We will call this extension the
\mathcal{P}
-preferred extension.
Consequently,

u_{\rho}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\operatorname{Ind}_{I}^{H}(\dot{u}_{\rho})

is a canonically determined irreducible character of
H
lying above the
H
-orbit of

u_{\rho}^{\circ}
.

Proof.

The algebraic group
\mathbf{H}=C_{\mathbf{G}^{*}}(s)
is an
F^{*}
-stable semisimple centralizer.
Hence Lemma
2.2
and
Remark
2.4
give pinned semidirect component models for

H
and for the inertia subgroup
I
. By Lemma
3.10
, the quotient

H/H^{\circ}
is abelian. Hence

I/H^{\circ}\leq H/H^{\circ}

is abelian as well. Applying Proposition
7.9

to
H
and the character
u_{\rho}^{\circ}
, we obtain a canonical preferred
extension

\dot{u}_{\rho}\in\operatorname{Irr}(I)

of
u_{\rho}^{\circ}
.

Now set

u_{\rho}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\operatorname{Ind}_{I}^{H}(\dot{u}_{\rho}).

By Clifford theory,
u_{\rho}
is irreducible, and its restriction to
H^{\circ}
is the sum of the

H
-conjugates of
u_{\rho}^{\circ}
. Thus
u_{\rho}
lies above the
H
-orbit of
u_{\rho}^{\circ}
.
Since
\dot{u}_{\rho}
is canonical, so is
u_{\rho}
.
∎

8.
The cuspidal part of the pinned Jordan decomposition

The preceding section supplied the extension-theoretic input needed on the
unipotent side: after fixing the pinning, cuspidal unipotent characters admit
canonical preferred extensions to the relevant stabilisers. We now use this
input to isolate the cuspidal part of the pinned Jordan decomposition.

8.1.
Canonical Jordan decomposition of cuspidal characters

Theorem 8.1

(Pinned Malle matching for simple groups)
.

Let
\mathbf{G}
be simple and simply connected, equipped with a Frobenius
endomorphism
F
, and put
G=\mathbf{G}^{F}
. Fix an
F
-stable pinning

\mathcal{P}
of
\mathbf{G}
, and let
\psi_{\mathcal{P}}
be the corresponding
non-degenerate character of the unipotent radical of the fixed Borel subgroup.
Let

A\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=G_{\operatorname{ad}}/G

denote the group of diagonal automorphisms of
G
. Let

s\in G^{*F}
, and let

\mathcal{S}_{s}\subset\mathcal{E}(G,s)

be the
A
-orbit of semisimple characters in
\mathcal{E}(G,s)
. Denote by

\rho_{s,\psi_{\mathcal{P}}}\in\mathcal{S}_{s}

the Whittaker-normalized semisimple character determined by
\psi_{\mathcal{P}}
.

Let

F_{O}\subset\mathcal{E}(G,s)_{\operatorname{cusp}}

be an
A
-orbit of cuspidal characters. Then the pinning
\mathcal{P}
determines
a distinguished element

\rho_{O,\mathcal{P}}\in F_{O}

such that

\operatorname{Aut}(G)_{\rho_{O,\mathcal{P}}}=\operatorname{Aut}(G)_{\rho_{s,\psi_{\mathcal{P}}}}.

Consequently there is a unique
A
-equivariant bijection

m_{\mathcal{P},O}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}F_{O}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{S}_{s}

satisfying

m_{\mathcal{P},O}(\rho_{O,\mathcal{P}})=\rho_{s,\psi_{\mathcal{P}}}.

Moreover, for every
\rho\in F_{O}
, one has

\operatorname{Aut}(G)_{\rho}=\operatorname{Aut}(G)_{m_{\mathcal{P},O}(\rho)}.

We call
m_{\mathcal{P},O}
the
\mathcal{P}
-preferred Malle matching on the orbit
F_{O}
.

Proof.

Write the action of
a\in A
on characters as

\eta^{a}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\eta\circ\operatorname{ad}(a).

Malle’s theorem gives, for each cuspidal character

\rho\in\mathcal{E}(G,s)
, a semisimple character

\chi\in\mathcal{E}(G,s)
satisfying

\operatorname{Aut}(G)_{\chi}=\operatorname{Aut}(G)_{\rho}.

In particular, the
A
-orbit
F_{O}
is isomorphic, as an
A
-set, to the

A
-orbit
\mathcal{S}_{s}
of semisimple characters. The point is to choose
the isomorphism in a way normalized by the pinning.

We first treat type
A
. In this case the diagonal automorphism group may
have order larger than two, so the stabilizer condition alone does not choose
a distinguished point. Instead one uses the Whittaker normalization. By the
type
A
equivariant character correspondence of Cabanes–Späth, as used in
Malle’s proof, each diagonal orbit of cuspidal characters in
\mathcal{E}(G,s)

contains a unique
\psi_{\mathcal{P}}
-generic constituent. We define

\rho_{O,\mathcal{P}}

to be this constituent. The semisimple orbit
\mathcal{S}_{s}
similarly
contains the unique
\psi_{\mathcal{P}}
-generic semisimple character

\rho_{s,\psi_{\mathcal{P}}}
. The type
A
equivariance theorem gives

\operatorname{Aut}(G)_{\rho_{O,\mathcal{P}}}=\operatorname{Aut}(G)_{\rho_{s,\psi_{\mathcal{P}}}}.

We now assume that
\mathbf{G}
is not of type
A
. Let

S_{\rho}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\operatorname{Aut}(G)_{\rho},\qquad A_{\rho}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=S_{\rho}\cap A,

and let
B_{\rho}
be the image of
S_{\rho}
in the graph-field part of

\operatorname{Out}(G)
. If
\chi\in\mathcal{S}_{s}
is one semisimple character satisfying

\operatorname{Aut}(G)_{\chi}=S_{\rho},

then the other diagonal translates of
\chi
with the same stabilizer

S_{\rho}
are parametrized by

(A/A_{\rho})^{B_{\rho}}.

For the non-type
A
groups occurring in Malle’s reduction, this group is
either trivial or of order two.

If

(A/A_{\rho})^{B_{\rho}}=1,

then there is no residual choice: the semisimple character in

\mathcal{S}_{s}
whose full automorphism stabilizer equals
S_{\rho}
is unique.
Equivalently, there is a unique element of
F_{O}
whose stabilizer equals

\operatorname{Aut}(G)_{\rho_{s,\psi_{\mathcal{P}}}}
. We define this element to be

\rho_{O,\mathcal{P}}
.

It remains to discuss the residual twofold cases. These are precisely the
cases in Malle’s proof where the stabilizer condition leaves two diagonal
translates undistinguished. Malle’s proof treats them by explicit Brauer-tree
chains, by Deligne–Lusztig induction chains with multiplicity-one
constituents, and by the dihedral actions occurring in type
D
. The pinning
removes the remaining sign ambiguity as follows.

In the Brauer-tree cases, Malle connects the cuspidal character to the
semisimple character by a labelled chain of non-exceptional vertices on
Brauer trees. We root this chain at the pinned semisimple endpoint

\rho_{s,\psi_{\mathcal{P}}}
. Since the labels of hooks, cohooks, and the relevant
simple factors are fixed by the pinned root datum, this rooted chain has a
unique cuspidal endpoint in
F_{O}
. We define this endpoint to be

\rho_{O,\mathcal{P}}
. Malle’s Brauer-tree argument then gives equality of the
full automorphism stabilizers.

In the Deligne–Lusztig induction cases, choose the Levi subgroup
L
to be
the standard Levi determined by the pinned root datum. The character on
L

appearing in Malle’s induction chain is chosen with the corresponding pinned
Whittaker normalization. The relevant constituents of

R_{L}^{G}(\psi)

occur with multiplicity one. Hence, among the two possible diagonal translates
in the residual orbit, the pinned induction datum selects a unique constituent.
This constituent is defined to be
\rho_{O,\mathcal{P}}
, and Malle’s
Deligne–Lusztig argument gives

\operatorname{Aut}(G)_{\rho_{O,\mathcal{P}}}=\operatorname{Aut}(G)_{\rho_{s,\psi_{\mathcal{P}}}}.

Finally, in the type
D
cases where the residual ambiguity is spinorial, the
non-trivial ambiguity is the interchange of the two half-spin labels, or its
graph-field analogue. The pinning labels the two terminal vertices of the

D_{n}
-diagram. We choose the constituent corresponding to the preferred
terminal vertex determined by
\mathcal{P}
. Malle’s case analysis shows that the
cuspidal and semisimple diagonal orbits carry the same dihedral action of the
relevant graph-field automorphisms. Therefore this pinned choice again has
the same full automorphism stabilizer as the pinned semisimple character.

Thus in every case we have constructed a distinguished element

\rho_{O,\mathcal{P}}\in F_{O}

with

\operatorname{Aut}(G)_{\rho_{O,\mathcal{P}}}=\operatorname{Aut}(G)_{\rho_{s,\psi_{\mathcal{P}}}}.

In particular,

\operatorname{Stab}_{A}(\rho_{O,\mathcal{P}})=\operatorname{Stab}_{A}(\rho_{s,\psi_{\mathcal{P}}}).

There is therefore a unique
A
-equivariant bijection

m_{\mathcal{P},O}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}F_{O}\longrightarrow\mathcal{S}_{s}

sending
\rho_{O,\mathcal{P}}
to
\rho_{s,\psi_{\mathcal{P}}}
, namely

m_{\mathcal{P},O}\bigl(\rho_{O,\mathcal{P}}^{a}\bigr)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\rho_{s,\psi_{\mathcal{P}}}^{a}\qquad(a\in A).

This formula is well-defined because the two basepoints have the same

A
-stabilizer. It is visibly
A
-equivariant and is the only

A
-equivariant map with the prescribed value at
\rho_{O,\mathcal{P}}
.

For any
a\in A
, conjugating the equality

\operatorname{Aut}(G)_{\rho_{O,\mathcal{P}}}=\operatorname{Aut}(G)_{\rho_{s,\psi_{\mathcal{P}}}}

by
a
gives

\operatorname{Aut}(G)_{\rho_{O,\mathcal{P}}^{a}}=\operatorname{Aut}(G)_{\rho_{s,\psi_{\mathcal{P}}}^{a}}.

Hence

\operatorname{Aut}(G)_{\rho}=\operatorname{Aut}(G)_{m_{\mathcal{P},O}(\rho)}

for all
\rho\in F_{O}
, as claimed.
∎

Lemma 8.2
.

Fix a pinning
\mathcal{P}
of
(\mathbf{G},F)
. Then there exists a canonically defined bijection

\mathbb{J}_{s,\mathcal{P}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathcal{E}(G,s)_{\operatorname{cusp}}\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }\operatorname{Uch}(H)_{\operatorname{cusp}}

satisfying

\langle R_{\mathbf{T}^{*}}^{\mathbf{G}}(s),\rho\rangle_{G}=\epsilon_{\mathbf{G}}\epsilon_{\mathbf{H}}\langle R_{\mathbf{T}^{*}}^{\mathbf{H}}(1),\mathbb{J}_{s}(\rho)\rangle_{H}.

We call
\mathbb{J}_{s,\mathcal{P}}
the preferred bijection attached to
\mathcal{P}
.

Proof.

Let

\bar{J}_{s}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathcal{E}(G,s)_{\operatorname{cusp}}\longrightarrow\operatorname{Uch}(H^{\circ})_{\operatorname{cusp}}/\!\sim(H/H^{\circ})

be Lusztig’s disconnected Jordan decomposition restricted to the cuspidal part. This is independent of the choice of
J_{s}

For an
H/H^{\circ}
-orbit

O\subset\operatorname{Uch}(H^{\circ})_{\operatorname{cusp}},

put

F_{O}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\bar{J}_{s}^{-1}(O)\subset\mathcal{E}(G,s)_{\operatorname{cusp}}.

Choose
u_{O}^{\circ}\in O
, and set

I_{O}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\operatorname{Stab}_{H}(u_{O}^{\circ}),\qquad\Omega_{O}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=I_{O}/H^{\circ}.

By Theorem
7.10
,
u_{O}^{\circ}
admits a canonical preferred
extension

\dot{u}_{O}\in\operatorname{Irr}(I_{O}).

For
\xi\in\Omega_{O}^{\vee}=\operatorname{Irr}(\Omega_{O})
, define

u_{O,\xi}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\operatorname{Ind}_{I_{O}}^{H}(\dot{u}_{O}\otimes\xi)\in\operatorname{Irr}(H).

By Clifford theory, each
u_{O,\xi}
is irreducible, and the map

\Omega_{O}^{\vee}\longrightarrow\operatorname{Uch}(H)_{O},\qquad\xi\longmapsto u_{O,\xi},

is a bijection onto

\operatorname{Uch}(H)_{O}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\left\{u\in\operatorname{Uch}(H)_{\operatorname{cusp}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\operatorname{Res}^{H}_{H^{\circ}}u=\sum_{\lambda\in O}\lambda\right\}.

This parametrization is independent of the chosen representative
u_{O}^{\circ}\in O
.
Indeed, if
u_{1}^{\circ}={}^{h}u_{O}^{\circ}
for some
h\in H
, then

I_{1}=hI_{O}h^{-1}.

Since
H/H^{\circ}
is abelian, conjugation by
h
induces the identity on

I_{O}/H^{\circ}=\Omega_{O}.

Moreover, by canonicity of the preferred extension, the preferred extension of
u_{1}^{\circ}
is

{}^{h}\!\dot{u}_{O}.

Hence for every
\xi\in\Omega_{O}^{\vee}
,

\operatorname{Ind}_{I_{1}}^{H}\bigl({}^{h}\!\dot{u}_{O}\otimes\xi\bigr)=\operatorname{Ind}_{I_{O}}^{H}(\dot{u}_{O}\otimes\xi),

so the characters
u_{O,\xi}
depend only on the orbit
O
. In particular,

(8.1)

|\operatorname{Uch}(H)_{O}|=|\Omega_{O}|.

Now put

A\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=G_{\operatorname{ad}}/G.

If
\rho,\rho^{\prime}\in F_{O}
, then
\rho^{\prime}=\rho\circ\operatorname{ad}(a)
for some
a\in A
, and since
A
is
abelian we have

\operatorname{Stab}_{A}(\rho^{\prime})=\operatorname{Stab}_{A}(\rho).

Thus the stabilizer
A_{\rho}
depends only on the orbit
O
; we denote it by

A_{O}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=A_{\rho}\qquad(\rho\in F_{O}).

By the construction of the disconnected Jordan decomposition together with the orthogonality
statement in Remark
3.15
, the subgroup
A_{O}
is the orthogonal complement of

\Omega_{O}
under the pairing induced by

\alpha\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}A\longrightarrow(H/H^{\circ})^{\vee}.

Hence
\alpha
induces an isomorphism

(8.2)

\bar{\alpha}_{O}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\ A/A_{O}\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }\Omega_{O}^{\vee}.

Since
F_{O}
is a single
A
-orbit and
|F_{O}|=|\Omega_{O}|
by
Theorem
3.16
, we obtain

(8.3)

|F_{O}|=|A/A_{O}|=|\Omega_{O}|.

For every
\rho\in\mathcal{E}(G,s)_{\operatorname{cusp}}
, with
O=\bar{J}_{s}(\rho)
, we now attach a class

c_{\rho}\in A/A_{O}

canonically determined by
\rho
and the chosen pinning
\mathcal{P}
.

Case (1): If
\mathbf{G}
is simple and simply connected, let
c_{\rho}
be the class
g_{\rho}A_{\rho}
obtained
as follows: let

\chi_{\rho}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=m_{\mathcal{P},O}(\rho)\in\mathcal{S}_{s}

be the
\mathcal{P}
-preferred semisimple character attached to
\rho
by
Theorem
8.1
. Let
\rho_{s,\psi}
be the semisimple character attached to the pinned Whittaker datum, and choose

g_{\rho}\in G_{\operatorname{ad}}
such that

\chi_{\rho}\circ\operatorname{ad}(g_{\rho})=\rho_{s,\psi}.

Then
c_{\rho}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=g_{\rho}A_{\rho}
.

Case (2): If
\mathbf{G}
is semisimple and simply connected, write

\mathbf{G}=\prod_{i}\mathbf{G}_{i},\qquad\rho=\boxtimes_{i}\rho_{i},

and define
c_{\rho}
as the product of the classes attached to the factors by the preceding case.

Case (3): If
\mathbf{G}_{\operatorname{der}}
is simply connected, choose a semisimple character

\chi_{0}\in\mathcal{E}(G_{0},s_{0})

corresponding to
\rho_{0}
. Let

\chi_{\rho}\in\mathcal{E}(G,s)

be the unique semisimple character lying above
\chi_{0}
, whose existence and
uniqueness are given by
[
22
, Lem. 4.1(b)]
. The reduction argument in

[
22
, proof of Thm. 1, immediately after Lem. 4.1]
shows that the
stabilizer-matching statement for
G_{0}=[G,G]
passes to
G
: indeed,

G_{0}
is characteristic in
G
, and both
\rho
and
\chi_{\rho}
are uniquely
determined, inside the fixed Lusztig series
\mathcal{E}(G,s)
, by their constituents

\rho_{0}
and
\chi_{0}
on
G_{0}
. Since
\rho_{0}
and
\chi_{0}
have the same
stabilizer under diagonal automorphisms by the simply connected derived case, it
follows that

\operatorname{Stab}_{A}(\chi_{\rho})=\operatorname{Stab}_{A}(\rho)=A_{\rho}.

Hence

\operatorname{Stab}_{A}(\chi_{\rho})=\operatorname{Stab}_{A}(\rho)=A_{\rho}.

Choose
g_{\rho}\in G_{\operatorname{ad}}
such that

\chi_{\rho}\circ\operatorname{ad}(g_{\rho})=\rho_{s,\psi},

and set

c_{\rho}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=g_{\rho}A_{\rho}.

Case (4): Finally, for general
G
, choose any
z
-extension

1\longrightarrow Z\longrightarrow G^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\,f\,}G\longrightarrow 1

defined over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
, with
Z
a central torus and
G^{\prime}_{\operatorname{der}}
simply connected. Choose a lift

s^{\prime}\in G^{\prime*}
of
s
, and let
\rho^{\prime}
be the inflation of
\rho
to
G^{\prime F}
. Applying the
preceding case to
(G^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})
, we obtain a class

c_{\rho^{\prime}}\in G^{\prime}_{\operatorname{ad}}/G^{\prime}.

Via the canonical identification

G^{\prime}_{\operatorname{ad}}/G^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\sim}G_{\operatorname{ad}}/G=A

of Remark
3.13
, we transport
c_{\rho^{\prime}}
to a class
c_{\rho}\in A/A_{O}
.
By Lemma
8.3
below, this class is independent of the chosen
z
-extension.
Thus
c_{\rho}
is canonically determined in all cases.

The construction is
A
-equivariant: for
a\in A
,

(8.4)

c_{\rho\circ\operatorname{ad}(a)}=a^{-1}c_{\rho}.

In Cases (1)–(3) this is immediate from the definitions, and in Case (4) it
follows after inflation to a
z
-extension and descent via Lemma
8.3
.

We may therefore define

\mathbb{J}_{s,\mathcal{P}}(\rho)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=u_{O,\bar{\alpha}_{O}(c_{\rho})},\qquad O=\bar{J}_{s}(\rho).

Everything entering this definition is canonical once
\mathcal{P}
is fixed, so

\mathbb{J}_{s,\mathcal{P}}
is canonically defined.

It remains to prove that
\mathbb{J}_{s,\mathcal{P}}
is a bijection. Fix an orbit
O
, and choose

\rho_{O}\in F_{O}
. Every
\rho\in F_{O}
may be written uniquely in the form

\rho=\rho_{O}\circ\operatorname{ad}(a)

with
aA_{O}\in A/A_{O}
. By (
8.4
),

c_{\rho}=a^{-1}c_{\rho_{O}}.

Hence

\mathbb{J}_{s,\mathcal{P}}(\rho)=u_{O,\bar{\alpha}_{O}(a^{-1}c_{\rho_{O}})}.

Translation by the fixed class
c_{\rho_{O}}
is a bijection of
A/A_{O}
, and
(
8.2
) is an isomorphism. Therefore the map

F_{O}\longrightarrow\operatorname{Uch}(H)_{O},\qquad\rho\longmapsto\mathbb{J}_{s,\mathcal{P}}(\rho),

is a bijection. Taking the disjoint union over all
H/H^{\circ}
-orbits
O
yields a bijection

\mathbb{J}_{s,\mathcal{P}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathcal{E}(G,s)_{\operatorname{cusp}}\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }\operatorname{Uch}(H)_{\operatorname{cusp}}.

Finally, let
\rho\in F_{O}
. By construction,

\operatorname{Res}^{H}_{H^{\circ}}\mathbb{J}_{s,\mathcal{P}}(\rho)=\sum_{\lambda\in O}\lambda.

Therefore Theorem
3.16
(3) gives

\langle R_{\mathbf{T}^{*}}^{\mathbf{G}}(s),\rho\rangle_{G}=\epsilon_{\mathbf{G}}\epsilon_{\mathbf{H}^{\circ}}\sum_{\lambda\in O}\langle R_{\mathbf{T}^{*}}^{\mathbf{H}^{\circ}}(1),\lambda\rangle_{H^{\circ}}=\epsilon_{\mathbf{G}}\epsilon_{\mathbf{H}^{\circ}}\Bigl\langle R_{\mathbf{T}^{*}}^{\mathbf{H}^{\circ}}(1),\operatorname{Res}^{H}_{H^{\circ}}\mathbb{J}_{s,\mathcal{P}}(\rho)\Bigr\rangle_{H^{\circ}}.

By the definition of
R_{\mathbf{T}^{*}}^{\mathbf{H}}(1)
for the disconnected group
H
, the last expression is

\epsilon_{\mathbf{G}}\epsilon_{\mathbf{H}}\,\langle R_{\mathbf{T}^{*}}^{\mathbf{H}}(1),\mathbb{J}_{s,\mathcal{P}}(\rho)\rangle_{H}.

This is the required formula.
∎

Lemma 8.3

(Independence of the
z
-extension)
.

Let

1\longrightarrow Z_{i}\longrightarrow G_{i}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\,f_{i}\,}G\longrightarrow 1\qquad(i=1,2)

be two
F
-stable
z
-extensions of
G
, with
Z_{i}
a connected central torus and

(G_{i}^{\prime})_{\operatorname{der}}
simply connected. Fix a pinning
\mathcal{P}
of
(\mathbf{G},F)
, and equip each

G_{i}^{\prime}
with the induced pinning. Let
s_{i}^{\prime}\in(G_{i}^{\prime*})^{F}
be the image of
s
under the
dual regular embedding
G^{*}\hookrightarrow G_{i}^{\prime*}
, and let

\rho_{i}^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\rho\circ f_{i}^{F}\in\mathcal{E}(G_{i}^{\prime},s_{i}^{\prime})_{\operatorname{cusp}}

be the inflation of
\rho\in\mathcal{E}(G,s)_{\operatorname{cusp}}
.

Apply Case
(3)
to
(G_{i}^{\prime},\rho_{i}^{\prime})
, and let

c_{i}(\rho)\in G_{\operatorname{ad}}/G

be the resulting class, via the natural identification of the corresponding diagonal-automorphism
quotients with
G_{\operatorname{ad}}/G
. Then

c_{1}(\rho)=c_{2}(\rho).

Consequently the character

\mathbb{J}_{s}(\rho)=u_{\rho}\otimes\alpha(c_{i}(\rho))

is independent of the chosen
z
-extension.

Proof.

Consider the fibre product

\widetilde{G}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=G_{1}^{\prime}\times_{G}G_{2}^{\prime}=\{(g_{1},g_{2})\in G_{1}^{\prime}\times G_{2}^{\prime}\mid f_{1}(g_{1})=f_{2}(g_{2})\}.

Then
\widetilde{G}
is a connected reductive
F
-stable group, and the projections

p_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\widetilde{G}\twoheadrightarrow G_{i}^{\prime}

have kernels

\ker(p_{1})=\{(1,z_{2})\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}z_{2}\in Z_{2}\}\cong Z_{2},\qquad\ker(p_{2})=\{(z_{1},1)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}z_{1}\in Z_{1}\}\cong Z_{1},

which are connected central tori. Thus
\widetilde{G}
is again a
z
-extension of each

G_{i}^{\prime}
, and also of
G
.

Moreover, each
p_{i}
induces an isomorphism on derived groups:

p_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\widetilde{G}_{\operatorname{der}}\xrightarrow{\sim}(G_{i}^{\prime})_{\operatorname{der}}.

Indeed, surjectivity follows from the surjectivity of
p_{i}
, while

\ker(p_{i})\cap\widetilde{G}_{\operatorname{der}}
is a connected subgroup of the torus
\ker(p_{i})
contained
in the semisimple group
\widetilde{G}_{\operatorname{der}}
, hence is trivial. In particular,

\widetilde{G}_{\operatorname{der}}
is simply connected.

It is therefore enough to prove the following functoriality statement: if

f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\widetilde{G}\twoheadrightarrow G^{\prime}

is a surjective
F
-morphism of connected reductive groups whose kernel is a connected central
torus, and if both
\widetilde{G}_{\operatorname{der}}
and
G^{\prime}_{\operatorname{der}}
are simply connected, then the class
constructed in Case (3) for the inflated character on
\widetilde{G}
is the same as the
class constructed for
G^{\prime}
.

So let
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\widetilde{G}\twoheadrightarrow G^{\prime}
be such a morphism, let
s^{\prime}\in(G^{\prime*})^{F}
be the
image of
s
, let
\widetilde{s}\in(\widetilde{G}^{*})^{F}
be the image of
s^{\prime}
under the dual
regular embedding
G^{\prime*}\hookrightarrow\widetilde{G}^{*}
, and let

\rho^{\prime}\in\mathcal{E}(G^{\prime},s^{\prime})_{\operatorname{cusp}},\qquad\widetilde{\rho}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\rho^{\prime}\circ f^{F}\in\mathcal{E}(\widetilde{G},\widetilde{s})_{\operatorname{cusp}}.

Write

G_{0}^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=G^{\prime}_{\operatorname{der}},\qquad\widetilde{G}_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\widetilde{G}_{\operatorname{der}}.

As observed above, the induced morphism

f_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\widetilde{G}_{0}\xrightarrow{\sim}G_{0}^{\prime}

is an isomorphism.

Since Case (3) is already canonical once the pinning is fixed, we may compute both
classes using compatible auxiliary choices. Choose an irreducible constituent

\rho_{0}\in\operatorname{Irr}(G_{0}^{\prime F}\mid\rho^{\prime})

and transport it via
f_{0}
to

\widetilde{\rho}_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\rho_{0}\circ f_{0}\in\operatorname{Irr}(\widetilde{G}_{0}^{F}\mid\widetilde{\rho}).

Applying Case (2) to
G_{0}^{\prime}
and
\widetilde{G}_{0}
, we obtain corresponding semisimple
characters

\chi_{0}\in\mathcal{E}(G_{0}^{\prime},s_{0}^{\prime}),\qquad\widetilde{\chi}_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\chi_{0}\circ f_{0}\in\mathcal{E}(\widetilde{G}_{0},\widetilde{s}_{0}),

with the same stabilizers as
\rho_{0}
and
\widetilde{\rho}_{0}
, respectively.

Now apply
[
22
, Lem. 4.1(b)]
. There is a unique semisimple character

\rho_{s}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{E}(G^{\prime},s^{\prime})

lying above
\chi_{0}
, and a unique semisimple character

\widetilde{\rho}_{s}\in\mathcal{E}(\widetilde{G},\widetilde{s})

lying above
\widetilde{\chi}_{0}
. Since
f^{F}
is surjective and
\widetilde{\chi}_{0}
is a constituent
of the restriction of
\operatorname{Inf}_{f}(\rho_{s}^{\prime})
to
\widetilde{G}_{0}^{F}
, the uniqueness in

[
22
, Lem. 4.1(b)]
forces

\widetilde{\rho}_{s}=\operatorname{Inf}_{f}(\rho_{s}^{\prime}).

Let
\rho^{\prime}_{s,\psi}
and
\widetilde{\rho}_{s,\psi}
denote the semisimple characters attached to the
pinned Whittaker data on
G^{\prime}
and
\widetilde{G}
, respectively. Because the pinning on

\widetilde{G}
is the pullback of that on
G^{\prime}
, the corresponding maximal unipotent subgroups
and nondegenerate characters are compatible under
f
. Hence the Gelfand–Graev characters are
related by inflation, and therefore so are their Alvis–Curtis duals:

\widetilde{\rho}_{s,\psi}=\operatorname{Inf}_{f}(\rho^{\prime}_{s,\psi}).

Let
g\in G^{\prime}_{\operatorname{ad}}
. Via the canonical identification
\widetilde{G}_{\operatorname{ad}}\cong G^{\prime}_{\operatorname{ad}}
, we may
also regard
g
as an element of
\widetilde{G}_{\operatorname{ad}}
. Then

\rho_{s}^{\prime}\circ\operatorname{ad}(g)=\rho^{\prime}_{s,\psi}

if and only if

\widetilde{\rho}_{s}\circ\operatorname{ad}(g)=\widetilde{\rho}_{s,\psi},

because inflation commutes with conjugation and
f^{F}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\widetilde{G}^{F}\twoheadrightarrow G^{\prime F}
is
surjective. Thus the same element
g
is admissible in the Case (3) construction for
both
G^{\prime}
and
\widetilde{G}
, and so the resulting classes coincide.

Applying this functoriality to the two projections

p_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\widetilde{G}\to G_{i}^{\prime}\qquad(i=1,2)

gives

c_{1}(\rho)=c_{\widetilde{G}}(\widetilde{\rho})=c_{2}(\rho).

This proves the lemma. The final assertion follows immediately, since
u_{\rho}
depends only on the

H/H^{\circ}
-orbit attached to
\rho
.
∎

9.
Preliminaries about disconnected groups

This section recalls some basic facts about disconnected reductive groups over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
. The contents are taken from
[
4
]
.

9.1.
Disconnected reductive groups over
\mathbb{F}_{q}

General set-up.

Let
\mathbf{G}
be a (not necessarily connected) reductive algebraic group defined over
\mathbb{F}_{q}

with Frobenius endomorphism
F\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbf{G}\to\mathbf{G}
.
Write
\mathbf{G}^{\circ}
for the identity component and set

G\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathbf{G}^{F},\qquad G^{\circ}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=(\mathbf{G}^{\circ})^{F},\qquad\Omega\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=G/G^{\circ}\cong(\mathbf{G}/\mathbf{G}^{\circ})^{F}.

9.1.1.
Parabolics, Levis, quasi-Borels, quasi-tori, Weyl groups

Definition 9.1

(Parabolics and Levis in
\mathbf{G}

[
4
, Def. 6.1.1–6.1.2]
)
.

A
parabolic subgroup
of
\mathbf{G}
is a closed subgroup
\mathbf{P}\leq\mathbf{G}

containing a Borel subgroup of
\mathbf{G}^{\circ}
.
Let
\mathbf{U}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=R_{u}(\mathbf{P}^{\circ})
be the unipotent radical of
\mathbf{P}^{\circ}
.
Choose a Levi subgroup
\mathbf{L}_{0}\leq\mathbf{P}^{\circ}
and put

\mathbf{L}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=N_{\mathbf{P}}(\mathbf{L}_{0}).

Then
\mathbf{L}^{\circ}=\mathbf{L}_{0}
and
\mathbf{P}=\mathbf{L}\ltimes\mathbf{U}
.
We call
\mathbf{L}
a
Levi subgroup
of
\mathbf{P}
.
A subgroup
\mathbf{L}\leq\mathbf{G}
is called
regular
if it is a Levi of some parabolic of
\mathbf{G}
.

Definition 9.2

(Quasi-Borels and quasi-maximal tori
[
4
, Def. 6.1.3]
)
.

A
quasi-Borel
of
\mathbf{G}
is the normalizer
N_{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbf{B}^{\circ})

of a Borel subgroup
\mathbf{B}^{\circ}\leq\mathbf{G}^{\circ}
.
A
quasi-maximal torus
of
\mathbf{G}
is a Levi subgroup of a quasi-Borel.

Definition 9.3

(Weyl group of
\mathbf{G}
relative to a torus
[
4
, Def. 6.1.4]
)
.

If
\mathbf{T}^{\circ}
is a maximal torus of
\mathbf{G}^{\circ}
, set

W_{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbf{T}^{\circ})\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=N_{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbf{T}^{\circ})/\mathbf{T}^{\circ}.

If
\mathbf{B}^{\circ}\leq\mathbf{G}^{\circ}
is a Borel containing
\mathbf{T}^{\circ}
and we put

\mathbf{T}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=N_{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbf{T}^{\circ},\mathbf{B}^{\circ})
,
then
\mathbf{T}
is a quasi-maximal torus of
\mathbf{G}
with
(\mathbf{T})^{\circ}=\mathbf{T}^{\circ}
and

W_{\mathbf{G}^{\circ}}(\mathbf{T}^{\circ})=N_{\mathbf{G}^{\circ}}(\mathbf{T}^{\circ})/\mathbf{T}^{\circ}\trianglelefteq W_{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbf{T}^{\circ})=N_{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbf{T}^{\circ})/\mathbf{T}^{\circ}.

Moreover, multiplication induces an isomorphism

W_{\mathbf{G}^{\circ}}(\mathbf{T}^{\circ})\rtimes(\mathbf{T}/\mathbf{T}^{\circ})\xrightarrow{\;\sim\;}W_{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbf{T}^{\circ}),

where
\mathbf{T}/\mathbf{T}^{\circ}
acts on

W_{\mathbf{G}^{\circ}}(\mathbf{T}^{\circ})
by conjugation.

9.1.2.
Pinning and pinned representatives for the component action

Pinning of
\mathbf{G}^{\circ}
.

Fix an
F
-stable Borel
\mathbf{B}^{\circ}\leq\mathbf{G}^{\circ}
and an
F
-stable maximal torus

\mathbf{T}^{\circ}\leq\mathbf{B}^{\circ}
.
Let
\Phi
be the root system of
\mathbf{G}^{\circ}
relative to
\mathbf{T}^{\circ}
and

\Delta\subset\Phi
the simple roots determined by
\mathbf{B}^{\circ}
.
A (standard)
pinning
of
\mathbf{G}^{\circ}
is the data

\mathcal{P}=(\mathbf{G}^{\circ},\mathbf{B}^{\circ},\mathbf{T}^{\circ},(x_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\Delta}),

where
x_{\alpha}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbb{G}_{a}\to\mathbf{U}_{\alpha}
are root group isomorphisms for the simple roots.
We say
\mathcal{P}
is
F
-stable
if
F
permutes
\Delta
and

F(x_{\alpha}(t))=x_{F(\alpha)}(t^{q})
(after identifying
F
on
\mathbb{G}_{a}
with
t\mapsto t^{q}
).

Lemma 9.4

(Bonnafé
[
4
, Lem. 6.2.1]
)
.

Let
\mathbf{T}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=N_{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbf{T}^{\circ},\mathbf{B}^{\circ})
.
There exists a closed
F
-stable subgroup
\mathbf{A}\leq\mathbf{T}
such that

(a)

\mathbf{G}=\mathbf{G}^{\circ}\cdot\mathbf{A}
;

(b)

\mathbf{A}\cap\mathbf{G}^{\circ}\subset Z(\mathbf{G}^{\circ})
;

(c)

every
a\in\mathbf{A}
induces a
quasi-central
automorphism of
\mathbf{G}^{\circ}

(in the sense of Digne–Michel).

Moreover one may take

\mathbf{A}=\bigl\{a\in\mathbf{T}\ \big|\ a\,x_{\alpha}(t)\,a^{-1}=x_{a(\alpha)}(t)\ \text{for all }\alpha\in\Delta,\ t\in\mathbb{G}_{a}\bigr\},

so that conjugation by
\mathbf{A}
preserves the chosen pinning
\mathcal{P}
.

Definition 9.5

(Pinned representatives of the algebraic component action)
.

Conjugation by
\mathbf{G}
induces a homomorphism

\Omega=\mathbf{G}/\mathbf{G}^{\circ}\longrightarrow\operatorname{Out}(\mathbf{G}^{\circ}).

The pinning
\mathcal{P}
identifies
\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbf{G}^{\circ},\mathcal{P})
with the automorphism group of the

based
root datum of
(\mathbf{G}^{\circ},\mathbf{T}^{\circ},\Delta)
and yields the usual

pinned lift
section
\operatorname{Out}(\mathbf{G}^{\circ})\to\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbf{G}^{\circ},\mathcal{P})
.
Composing, we obtain a canonical homomorphism

\Omega\longrightarrow\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbf{G}^{\circ},\mathcal{P}),\qquad\omega\longmapsto\sigma_{\omega},

whose image consists of pinning-preserving automorphisms representing the algebraic outer action class of
\omega
.
This definition records the algebraic outer action only.

9.1.3.
Lusztig induction/restriction for disconnected groups

Deligne–Lusztig varieties and Lusztig functors.

Let
\mathbf{P}
be a parabolic subgroup of
\mathbf{G}
with unipotent radical
\mathbf{U}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=R_{u}(\mathbf{P}^{\circ})
,
and let
\mathbf{L}
be an
F
-stable Levi subgroup of
\mathbf{P}
.
Set

Y_{\mathbf{U}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\{g\in\mathbf{G}\mid g^{-1}F(g)\in\mathbf{U}\}.

Then
G
acts on
Y_{\mathbf{U}}
by left translation and
L\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathbf{L}^{F}
acts by right translation.
The virtual
(G,L)
–bimodule
H_{c}^{\ast}(Y_{\mathbf{U}})
yields Lusztig induction and restriction functors

R_{\mathbf{L}\subset\mathbf{P}}^{\mathbf{G}}\quad\text{and}\quad{}^{\ast}R_{\mathbf{L}\subset\mathbf{P}}^{\mathbf{G}}

between Grothendieck groups (or between spaces of central class functions), as in

[
4
, §6.3]
(cf.
[
8
, Def. 2.2]
.
If
\mathbf{L}\supset\mathbf{G}^{\circ}
then
\mathbf{U}=1
and

R_{\mathbf{L}\subset\mathbf{L}}^{\mathbf{G}}=\operatorname{Ind}_{L}^{G},\qquad{}^{\ast}R_{\mathbf{L}\subset\mathbf{L}}^{\mathbf{G}}=\operatorname{Res}_{L}^{G}

[
4
, Prop. 6.3.2]
.
These functors satisfy the usual transitivity for chains of parabolics

[
4
, Prop. 6.3.3]
.

We now state a Lemma of Digne–Michel
[
8
, Cor. 2.4]
to be used in §
13
.

Lemma 9.6

(Restriction to the identity component for Lusztig functors)
.

Let
G
be a (possibly disconnected) reductive
\mathbb{F}_{q}
–group,
P
an
F
–stable “parabolic” of
G

(in the sense of
[
8
, Def. 1.4]
) with Levi
L
and unipotent radical
U=R_{u}(P^{\circ})\subset G^{\circ}
.
Let
P^{\circ}=P\cap G^{\circ}
and
L^{\circ}=L\cap G^{\circ}
.
Then for any class function (or virtual character)
\varphi
of
L^{F}
one has, as class functions on
G^{\circ F}
,

\operatorname{Res}^{G^{F}}_{G^{\circ F}}\bigl(R^{G}_{L\subset P}(\varphi)\bigr)\;=\;\sum_{a\in G^{F}/(L^{F}\cdot G^{\circ F})}{}^{a}\!\Bigl(R^{G^{\circ}}_{L^{\circ}\subset P^{\circ}}\bigl(\operatorname{Res}^{L^{F}}_{L^{\circ F}}\varphi\bigr)\Bigr),

and for any class function
\psi
of
G^{F}
,

\operatorname{Res}^{L^{F}}_{L^{\circ F}}\bigl({}^{*}R^{G}_{L\subset P}(\psi)\bigr)\;=\;{}^{*}R^{G^{\circ}}_{L^{\circ}\subset P^{\circ}}\bigl(\operatorname{Res}^{G^{F}}_{G^{\circ F}}\psi\bigr).

Corollary 9.7
.

With the notation of Lemma
9.6
,

\operatorname{Res}^{G^{F}}_{G^{\circ F}}R^{G}_{L\subset P}(\tau)
contains
R^{G^{\circ}}_{L^{\circ}\subset P^{\circ}}(\tau^{\circ})

as a direct summand for every
\tau^{\circ}\prec\operatorname{Res}^{L^{F}}_{L^{\circ F}}\tau
.

9.1.4.
Unipotent characters for disconnected groups

Unipotent characters.
