Following
[
4
, §6.4]
, an irreducible character
\gamma\in\operatorname{Irr}(G)
is called

unipotent
if it occurs in some
R_{\mathbf{T}^{\circ}\subset\mathbf{B}^{\circ}}^{\mathbf{G}}(1)
,
where
\mathbf{T}^{\circ}\leq\mathbf{G}^{\circ}
is an
F
-stable maximal torus and
\mathbf{B}^{\circ}\leq\mathbf{G}^{\circ}

a Borel containing it.
Equivalently,
\gamma
is unipotent iff it occurs in
\operatorname{Ind}_{G^{\circ}}^{G}(\gamma^{\circ})
for some unipotent

\gamma^{\circ}\in\operatorname{Irr}(G^{\circ})

[
4
, Lem. 6.4.2]
.

Definition 9.8

(A semidirect-product model for
G^{*}
)
.

Let
\mathbf{G}
be a (possibly disconnected) reductive algebraic group over

\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
equipped with a Frobenius endomorphism
F\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbf{G}\to\mathbf{G}
.
Write
\mathbf{G}^{\circ}
for the identity component and

\Omega\ \mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\ \pi_{0}(\mathbf{G})\ \cong\ \mathbf{G}/\mathbf{G}^{\circ}.

Fix an
F
-stable pinning

\mathcal{P}=(\mathbf{B},\mathbf{T},\{x_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in\Delta})\qquad\text{of }\mathbf{G}^{\circ}.

Let
\mathbf{G}^{\circ*}
be the Langlands dual complex reductive group of

\mathbf{G}^{\circ}
, equipped with the
dual pinning

\mathcal{P}^{*}=(\mathbf{B}^{*},\mathbf{T}^{*},\{x_{\alpha^{*}}\}_{\alpha^{*}\in\Delta^{*}})

and the dual Frobenius automorphism
F^{*}
.

For each
\omega\in\Omega
, let

\sigma_{\omega}\in\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbf{G}^{\circ},\mathcal{P})

be the pinned automorphism representing the algebraic outer action of
\omega
,
as in Definition
9.5
. This semidirect-product
model records the pinned algebraic component action on the dual side.

By duality of pinned automorphisms,
\sigma_{\omega}
corresponds uniquely to a
pinned automorphism

\sigma_{\omega}^{*}\ \in\ \operatorname{Aut}(\mathbf{G}^{\circ*},\mathcal{P}^{*}),

and
\omega\mapsto\sigma_{\omega}^{*}
defines a group homomorphism

\iota\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\Omega\longrightarrow\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbf{G}^{\circ*}),\qquad\omega\longmapsto\sigma_{\omega}^{*}.

Definition of
\,\mathbf{G}^{*}
:

Define the
(possibly disconnected) dual group
of
\mathbf{G}
to be the
semidirect product

\mathbf{G}^{*}\ \mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\ \mathbf{G}^{\circ*}\rtimes_{\iota}\Omega,

i.e. as a variety
\mathbf{G}^{*}=\mathbf{G}^{\circ*}\times\Omega
with multiplication

(g_{1},\omega_{1})\cdot(g_{2},\omega_{2})\ \mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\ \bigl(g_{1}\,\iota(\omega_{1})(g_{2}),\ \omega_{1}\omega_{2}\bigr),\qquad g_{i}\in\mathbf{G}^{\circ*},\ \omega_{i}\in\Omega.

Then
(\mathbf{G}^{*})^{\circ}=\mathbf{G}^{\circ*}
and
\pi_{0}(\mathbf{G}^{*})\cong\Omega
.

Frobenius on
\,\mathbf{G}^{*}
:

Let
F_{\Omega}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\Omega\to\Omega
be the action induced by
F
on components.
Assuming the pinning
\mathcal{P}
is
F
-stable, define

F^{*}(g,\omega)\ \mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\ \bigl(F^{*}(g),\,F_{\Omega}(\omega)\bigr),\qquad(g,\omega)\in\mathbf{G}^{\circ*}\rtimes\Omega,

so that
F^{*}
is an algebraic automorphism of
\mathbf{G}^{*}
and restricts to the
usual dual Frobenius on
(\mathbf{G}^{*})^{\circ}=\mathbf{G}^{\circ*}
.

10.
Disconnected Howlett–Lehrer theory

10.1.
Clifford labels for component extensions

Let
N\lhd M
be finite with
M/N
abelian and
\theta\in\operatorname{Irr}(N)
. Put

I_{M}(\theta)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\{m\in M\mid{}^{m}\theta\simeq\theta\},\qquad\Omega_{\theta}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=I_{M}(\theta)/N.

Clifford theory attaches to
\theta
a canonical cohomology class

[\alpha_{\theta}]\in H^{2}(\Omega_{\theta},\mathbb{C}^{\times})
. We use the following
convention throughout the paper. Choose a projective extension
\tilde{\theta}

of
\theta
to
I_{M}(\theta)
, and let

c_{\theta}\in Z^{2}(\Omega_{\theta},\mathbb{C}^{\times})
be its multiplier:

\tilde{\theta}(i_{1})\tilde{\theta}(i_{2})=c_{\theta}(\bar{i}_{1},\bar{i}_{2})\tilde{\theta}(i_{1}i_{2}),\qquad i_{1},i_{2}\in I_{M}(\theta).

The Clifford-label cocycle is defined to be

\alpha_{\theta}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=c_{\theta}^{-1}.

Thus
\alpha_{\theta}
is the cocycle whose projective representations cancel the
multiplier of
\tilde{\theta}
. Changing
\tilde{\theta}
changes
c_{\theta}
, and
hence
\alpha_{\theta}
, by a coboundary; the cohomology class

[\alpha_{\theta}]
is canonical. With this convention Clifford theory gives a
bijection

\operatorname{Irr}(\mathbb{C}_{\alpha_{\theta}}[\Omega_{\theta}])\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }\operatorname{Irr}(M\mid\theta),

depending on the chosen representative but only through the canonical class

[\alpha_{\theta}]
. Concretely,

E\in\operatorname{Irr}(\mathbb{C}_{\alpha_{\theta}}[\Omega_{\theta}])\quad\longmapsto\quad\operatorname{Ind}_{I_{M}(\theta)}^{M}\bigl(\tilde{\theta}\otimes\operatorname{Inf}_{\Omega_{\theta}}^{I_{M}(\theta)}E\bigr)\ \in\operatorname{Irr}(M\mid\theta).

Here the tensor product is an honest representation of
I_{M}(\theta)
because

\tilde{\theta}
has multiplier
c_{\theta}
while
E
has multiplier

c_{\theta}^{-1}
.

10.2.
A semisimple crossed-product description

The next lemma gives the corresponding semisimple crossed-product description at the
level of endomorphism algebras.

Let
\mathbf{X}
be a possibly disconnected reductive group over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
with Frobenius
endomorphism
F
, and let
\mathbf{P}
be an
F
-stable parabolic subgroup of
\mathbf{X}
with

F
-stable Levi factor
\mathbf{M}
. Write

X\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathbf{X}^{F},\qquad X^{\circ}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=(\mathbf{X}^{\circ})^{F},\qquad M\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathbf{M}^{F},\qquad M^{\circ}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=(\mathbf{M}^{\circ})^{F},\qquad P^{\circ}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=(\mathbf{P}^{\circ})^{F}.

Let
\theta^{\circ}\in\operatorname{Irr}(M^{\circ})
be cuspidal, and put

\Omega_{\theta^{\circ}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=I_{M}(\theta^{\circ})/M^{\circ}.

Choose a normalized
2
–cocycle

c=c_{\theta^{\circ}}\in Z^{2}(\Omega_{\theta^{\circ}},\mathbb{C}^{\times}),

a section

\Omega_{\theta^{\circ}}\longrightarrow I_{M}(\theta^{\circ}),\qquad\omega\longmapsto\dot{\omega},\qquad\dot{1}=1,

and intertwiners

\phi_{\omega}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\theta^{\circ}\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }{}^{\dot{\omega}}\theta^{\circ},\qquad\phi_{1}=\mathrm{id},

realizing the factor set
c
. In accordance with
§
10.1
, the corresponding Clifford-label cocycle is

\alpha=\alpha_{\theta^{\circ}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=c^{-1}.

Set

M_{X^{\circ}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=R^{X^{\circ}}_{M^{\circ}\subset P^{\circ}}(\theta^{\circ}),\qquad N_{X}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=R^{X}_{M\subset P}\!\bigl(\operatorname{Ind}_{M^{\circ}}^{M}\theta^{\circ}\bigr).

Assume, as in the later applications, that the chosen parabolics are compatible so that
transitivity of Lusztig induction yields

N_{X}\;\cong\;\operatorname{Ind}_{X^{\circ}}^{X}(M_{X^{\circ}}),

and that the chosen
\Omega_{\theta^{\circ}}
–equivariant structure on
\theta^{\circ}

induces an action of
\Omega_{\theta^{\circ}}
on
M_{X^{\circ}}
and identifies

\Omega_{\theta^{\circ}}
with the stabilizer of
M_{X^{\circ}}
in
X/X^{\circ}
.

For each
\omega\in\Omega_{\theta^{\circ}}
, Harish–Chandra functoriality gives an
isomorphism

\Phi_{\omega}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}M_{X^{\circ}}=R^{X^{\circ}}_{M^{\circ}\subset P^{\circ}}(\theta^{\circ})\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }R^{X^{\circ}}_{M^{\circ}\subset P^{\circ}}({}^{\dot{\omega}}\theta^{\circ})\cong{}^{\dot{\omega}}M_{X^{\circ}}.

These isomorphisms satisfy the same factor-set relation as the
\phi_{\omega}
.
They therefore define an action of
\Omega_{\theta^{\circ}}
on

E_{X^{\circ}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\operatorname{End}_{X^{\circ}}(M_{X^{\circ}})

by

\omega\cdot f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\Phi_{\omega}^{-1}\circ{}^{\dot{\omega}}\!f\circ\Phi_{\omega},\qquad f\in E_{X^{\circ}}.

Lemma 10.1
.

With the above notation, there is a canonical algebra isomorphism

\operatorname{End}_{X}(N_{X})\xrightarrow{\sim}E_{X^{\circ}}\rtimes_{c}\Omega_{\theta^{\circ}},

where the crossed product is formed with respect to the above action of

\Omega_{\theta^{\circ}}
on
E_{X^{\circ}}
and the multiplier
c
of the chosen
projective equivariant structure.

Proof.

By the transitivity assumption,

N_{X}\cong\operatorname{Ind}_{X^{\circ}}^{X}(M_{X^{\circ}}).

Hence Frobenius reciprocity and Mackey theory for the normal subgroup

X^{\circ}\lhd X
give

\operatorname{End}_{X}(N_{X})\cong\bigoplus_{x\in X/X^{\circ}}\operatorname{Hom}_{X^{\circ}}\!\bigl(M_{X^{\circ}},{}^{x}M_{X^{\circ}}\bigr).

By the hypothesis that
\Omega_{\theta^{\circ}}
identifies with the stabilizer of

M_{X^{\circ}}
in
X/X^{\circ}
, the summand indexed by
xX^{\circ}
is nonzero if and only if

xX^{\circ}\in\Omega_{\theta^{\circ}}
. Thus

\operatorname{End}_{X}(N_{X})\cong\bigoplus_{\omega\in\Omega_{\theta^{\circ}}}\operatorname{Hom}_{X^{\circ}}\!\bigl(M_{X^{\circ}},{}^{\dot{\omega}}M_{X^{\circ}}\bigr).

For each
\omega\in\Omega_{\theta^{\circ}}
, composition with
\Phi_{\omega}
identifies

E_{X^{\circ}}=\operatorname{End}_{X^{\circ}}(M_{X^{\circ}})\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname{Hom}_{X^{\circ}}\!\bigl(M_{X^{\circ}},{}^{\dot{\omega}}M_{X^{\circ}}\bigr),\qquad f\longmapsto\Phi_{\omega}\circ f.

Under these identifications,
\operatorname{End}_{X}(N_{X})
becomes, as a vector space,

\operatorname{End}_{X}(N_{X})\cong\bigoplus_{\omega\in\Omega_{\theta^{\circ}}}E_{X^{\circ}}T_{\omega},

where
T_{\omega}
denotes the basis element corresponding to
\Phi_{\omega}
.

Now let
f,g\in E_{X^{\circ}}
and
\omega,\omega^{\prime}\in\Omega_{\theta^{\circ}}
.
Using the definition of the action of
\Omega_{\theta^{\circ}}
on
E_{X^{\circ}}
and the
factor-set relation satisfied by the
\Phi_{\omega}
, one computes that

(fT_{\omega})(gT_{\omega^{\prime}})=f\,(\omega\cdot g)\,c(\omega,\omega^{\prime})\,T_{\omega\omega^{\prime}}.

This is exactly the defining multiplication in the crossed product

E_{X^{\circ}}\rtimes_{c}\Omega_{\theta^{\circ}}
.
Therefore the assignment

fT_{\omega}\longmapsto fT_{\omega}

extends to an algebra isomorphism

E_{X^{\circ}}\rtimes_{c}\Omega_{\theta^{\circ}}\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }\operatorname{End}_{X}(N_{X}).

This proves the lemma.
∎

The formulation is deliberately at the level of the full Harish–Chandra induced
module
M_{X^{\circ}}
, rather than at the level of its irreducible constituents.
Thus no irreducibility assumption is imposed on

M_{X^{\circ}}=R^{X^{\circ}}_{M^{\circ}\subset P^{\circ}}(\theta^{\circ});

the connected Howlett–Lehrer algebra is retained as the coefficient algebra in
the crossed product.

10.3.
A corner lemma

Keep the notation of §
10.2
. In addition, put

\mathcal{A}_{\theta^{\circ}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathbb{C}_{\alpha}[\Omega_{\theta^{\circ}}],

where
\alpha=\alpha_{\theta^{\circ}}=c_{\theta^{\circ}}^{-1}
is the
Clifford-label cocycle. Thus
\mathcal{A}_{\theta^{\circ}}
is the twisted group algebra
which parametrizes the irreducible representations of
M
lying above

\theta^{\circ}
. Equivalently, it is the component algebra that appears after
passing from the
c_{\theta^{\circ}}
-twisted crossed product in
Lemma
10.1
to the opposite algebra.

Let
A_{\mathbf{M}}
denote the maximal
F
-split torus in
Z(\mathbf{M}^{\circ})
. Thus

\mathbf{M}^{\circ}=C_{\mathbf{X}^{\circ}}(A_{\mathbf{M}}),

and we write

N_{X^{\circ}}(A_{\mathbf{M}})\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=N_{\mathbf{X}^{\circ}}(A_{\mathbf{M}})^{F}.

Set, as before,

N_{X}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=R^{X}_{M\subset P}\!\bigl(\operatorname{Ind}_{M^{\circ}}^{M}\theta^{\circ}\bigr),\qquad M_{X^{\circ}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=R^{X^{\circ}}_{M^{\circ}\subset P^{\circ}}(\theta^{\circ}).

Let

W_{\theta^{\circ}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\Bigl\{w\in N_{X^{\circ}}(A_{\mathbf{M}})/M^{\circ}\;\Bigm|\;{}^{\,w}\theta^{\circ}\simeq\theta^{\circ}\Bigr\}

be the connected relative Weyl group attached to the cuspidal pair

(M^{\circ},\theta^{\circ})
inside
X^{\circ}
. By the connected
Howlett–Lehrer theorem, after choosing the usual normalized
Howlett–Lehrer intertwiners, we have an algebra isomorphism

\operatorname{End}_{X^{\circ}}(M_{X^{\circ}})^{\operatorname{op}}\;\cong\;\mathbb{C}[W_{\theta^{\circ}}].

For each

E\in\operatorname{Irr}(\mathcal{A}_{\theta^{\circ}}),

let

\sigma_{E}\in\operatorname{Irr}(M\mid\theta^{\circ})

be the irreducible representation corresponding to
E
under the
Clifford-theoretic bijection of §
10.1
. If

w\in W_{\theta^{\circ}}
, then
{}^{\,w}\sigma_{E}
again lies above

\theta^{\circ}
, and hence there exists a unique

w\cdot E\in\operatorname{Irr}(\mathcal{A}_{\theta^{\circ}})

such that

{}^{\,w}\sigma_{E}\;\cong\;\sigma_{\,w\cdot E}.

Thus
W_{\theta^{\circ}}
acts on
\operatorname{Irr}(\mathcal{A}_{\theta^{\circ}})
, and hence on the
set of primitive central idempotents of
\mathcal{A}_{\theta^{\circ}}
, by

w\cdot e_{E}=e_{\,w\cdot E}.

Put

W_{E}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\operatorname{Stab}_{W_{\theta^{\circ}}}(E)=\Bigl\{w\in W_{\theta^{\circ}}\;\Bigm|\;{}^{\,w}\sigma_{E}\simeq\sigma_{E}\Bigr\}.

The following consequence of Lemma
10.1

and the connected Howlett–Lehrer theorem will be used to pass from the
full induced object containing all Clifford extensions to the summand
attached to a fixed Clifford label.

Lemma 10.2
.

With the above notation, there is a natural algebra isomorphism

\operatorname{End}_{X}(N_{X})^{\operatorname{op}}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{A}_{\theta^{\circ}}\rtimes W_{\theta^{\circ}},

where the skew group algebra is formed with respect to the above action of

W_{\theta^{\circ}}
on
\mathcal{A}_{\theta^{\circ}}
.

Let
E\in\operatorname{Irr}(\mathcal{A}_{\theta^{\circ}})
, and let

e_{E}\in Z(\mathcal{A}_{\theta^{\circ}})
be the corresponding primitive central
idempotent. Then there exists a
2
-cocycle

\beta_{E}\in Z^{2}(W_{E},\mathbb{C}^{\times})

such that

e_{E}\bigl(\mathcal{A}_{\theta^{\circ}}\rtimes W_{\theta^{\circ}}\bigr)e_{E}\;\cong\;\operatorname{End}_{\mathbb{C}}(E^{\vee})\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{C}_{\beta_{E}}[W_{E}].

In particular, if the induced projective action of
W_{E}
on
E^{\vee}
lifts to
a genuine linear action, equivalently if
[\beta_{E}]=1
in

H^{2}(W_{E},\mathbb{C}^{\times})
, then

e_{E}\bigl(\mathcal{A}_{\theta^{\circ}}\rtimes W_{\theta^{\circ}}\bigr)e_{E}\;\cong\;\operatorname{End}_{\mathbb{C}}(E^{\vee})\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{C}[W_{E}].

Proof.

By Clifford theory and the convention
\alpha=c_{\theta^{\circ}}^{-1}
,

\operatorname{Ind}_{M^{\circ}}^{M}\theta^{\circ}\;\cong\;\bigoplus_{E\in\operatorname{Irr}(\mathcal{A}_{\theta^{\circ}})}\sigma_{E}\boxtimes E^{\vee}

as an
M
-module with its natural right

\mathcal{A}_{\theta^{\circ}}
-action on the multiplicity factor. The dual
multiplicity space
E^{\vee}
has multiplier
c_{\theta^{\circ}}
, matching the
component intertwiners in Lemma
10.1
; this is
precisely why
\mathcal{A}_{\theta^{\circ}}
appears after taking the opposite algebra.
Applying Harish–Chandra induction gives

N_{X}\;\cong\;\bigoplus_{E\in\operatorname{Irr}(\mathcal{A}_{\theta^{\circ}})}R^{X}_{M\subset P}(\sigma_{E})\boxtimes E^{\vee}

as an
X
-module with the induced right
\mathcal{A}_{\theta^{\circ}}
-action.

For each
w\in W_{\theta^{\circ}}
, let

B_{w}\in\operatorname{End}_{X^{\circ}}(M_{X^{\circ}})^{\operatorname{op}}

denote the Howlett–Lehrer operator corresponding to
w
. Under the
identification

\operatorname{End}_{X^{\circ}}(M_{X^{\circ}})^{\operatorname{op}}\cong\mathbb{C}[W_{\theta^{\circ}}],

the operator
B_{w}
acts on the Clifford labels by

B_{w}\,e_{E}\;=\;e_{\,w\cdot E}\,B_{w}.

Equivalently,
B_{w}
sends the
E
-isotypic summand

R^{X}_{M\subset P}(\sigma_{E})\boxtimes E^{\vee}

isomorphically onto the
(w\cdot E)
-isotypic summand

R^{X}_{M\subset P}(\sigma_{w\cdot E})\boxtimes(w\cdot E)^{\vee}.

It follows that the subalgebra of
\operatorname{End}_{X}(N_{X})^{\operatorname{op}}
generated by

\mathcal{A}_{\theta^{\circ}}
and the operators
B_{w}
,
w\in W_{\theta^{\circ}}
, is a
homomorphic image of the skew group algebra

\mathcal{A}_{\theta^{\circ}}\rtimes W_{\theta^{\circ}}.

This homomorphism is an isomorphism. Indeed, by
Lemma
10.1
and the connected
Howlett–Lehrer theorem,

\operatorname{dim}_{\mathbb{C}}\operatorname{End}_{X}(N_{X})=|\Omega_{\theta^{\circ}}|\cdot\operatorname{dim}_{\mathbb{C}}\operatorname{End}_{X^{\circ}}(M_{X^{\circ}})=|\Omega_{\theta^{\circ}}|\,|W_{\theta^{\circ}}|=\operatorname{dim}_{\mathbb{C}}\bigl(\mathcal{A}_{\theta^{\circ}}\rtimes W_{\theta^{\circ}}\bigr).

Thus the above surjective homomorphism between finite-dimensional algebras is
bijective, proving the first assertion.

We now compute the corner attached to a fixed

E\in\operatorname{Irr}(\mathcal{A}_{\theta^{\circ}})
. Inside the skew group algebra

\mathcal{A}_{\theta^{\circ}}\rtimes W_{\theta^{\circ}}
, write
[w]
for the standard
basis element corresponding to
w\in W_{\theta^{\circ}}
. The defining relation is

[w]\,e_{E}=e_{\,w\cdot E}\,[w].

Hence

e_{E}[w]e_{E}=0\qquad\text{if }w\notin W_{E},

and therefore

e_{E}\bigl(\mathcal{A}_{\theta^{\circ}}\rtimes W_{\theta^{\circ}}\bigr)e_{E}=\bigoplus_{w\in W_{E}}e_{E}\mathcal{A}_{\theta^{\circ}}[w]e_{E}.

The block algebra

e_{E}\mathcal{A}_{\theta^{\circ}}e_{E}

is canonically isomorphic to
\operatorname{End}_{\mathbb{C}}(E^{\vee})
. For
w\in W_{E}
, the element

[w]
normalizes this full matrix algebra. Since every automorphism of a full
matrix algebra is inner, we may choose elements

u_{w}\in\bigl(e_{E}\mathcal{A}_{\theta^{\circ}}e_{E}\bigr)^{\times}\qquad(w\in W_{E})

such that

[w]\,a\,[w]^{-1}=u_{w}au_{w}^{-1}\qquad\bigl(a\in e_{E}\mathcal{A}_{\theta^{\circ}}e_{E}\bigr).

Set

T_{w}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=u_{w}^{-1}e_{E}[w]e_{E}\qquad(w\in W_{E}).

Then each
T_{w}
commutes with
e_{E}\mathcal{A}_{\theta^{\circ}}e_{E}
. Consequently the
products
T_{w}T_{w^{\prime}}
are scalar multiples of
T_{ww^{\prime}}
, and there is a
uniquely determined function

\beta_{E}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}W_{E}\times W_{E}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}^{\times}

such that

T_{w}T_{w^{\prime}}=\beta_{E}(w,w^{\prime})T_{ww^{\prime}}.

Associativity implies that
\beta_{E}
is a
2
-cocycle. Thus

e_{E}\bigl(\mathcal{A}_{\theta^{\circ}}\rtimes W_{\theta^{\circ}}\bigr)e_{E}\;\cong\;e_{E}\mathcal{A}_{\theta^{\circ}}e_{E}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{C}_{\beta_{E}}[W_{E}]\;\cong\;\operatorname{End}_{\mathbb{C}}(E^{\vee})\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{C}_{\beta_{E}}[W_{E}].

If the projective action of
W_{E}
on
E^{\vee}
lifts to a genuine linear
action, then the elements
T_{w}
may be rescaled so that

T_{w}T_{w^{\prime}}=T_{ww^{\prime}}.

Equivalently, the cohomology class of
\beta_{E}
is trivial. In this case the
corner is untwisted:

e_{E}\bigl(\mathcal{A}_{\theta^{\circ}}\rtimes W_{\theta^{\circ}}\bigr)e_{E}\;\cong\;\operatorname{End}_{\mathbb{C}}(E^{\vee})\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{C}[W_{E}].

∎

Remark 10.3
.

The cocycle
\beta_{E}
measures only the possible projectivity of the stabilizer
action of
W_{E}
on the Clifford multiplicity space
E^{\vee}
. It is separate
from the Clifford-label cocycle
\alpha
, which has already been absorbed into the
twisted group algebra
\mathcal{A}_{\theta^{\circ}}
. In the abelian extension case used
below, where
\theta^{\circ}
extends to its inertia group, one has

\mathcal{A}_{\theta^{\circ}}=\mathbb{C}[\Omega_{\theta^{\circ}}],

and the relevant Clifford labels are one-dimensional. In that situation the
corner cocycle is trivial, and the corner is the ordinary group algebra of the
stabilizer. Without this simplification, the same statements remain valid after
replacing
\mathbb{C}[W_{E}]
by
\mathbb{C}_{\beta_{E}}[W_{E}]
.

10.4.
Disconnected Howlett–Lehrer theorem when the component group is abelian

Let
\mathbf{X}
be a possibly disconnected reductive group over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
with Frobenius
endomorphism
F
, and let
\mathbf{P}
be an
F
-stable parabolic subgroup of
\mathbf{X}
with

F
-stable Levi factor
\mathbf{M}
. Write

X\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathbf{X}^{F},\qquad X^{\circ}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=(\mathbf{X}^{\circ})^{F},\qquad M\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathbf{M}^{F},\qquad M^{\circ}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=(\mathbf{M}^{\circ})^{F},\qquad P^{\circ}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=(\mathbf{P}^{\circ})^{F}.

Assume that
X/X^{\circ}
is abelian. Let

\theta^{\circ}\in\operatorname{Irr}(M^{\circ})

be cuspidal, and put

I\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=I_{M}(\theta^{\circ})=\{\,m\in M\mid{}^{\,m}\theta^{\circ}\simeq\theta^{\circ}\,\},\qquad\Omega_{\theta^{\circ}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=I/M^{\circ}.

Since
X/X^{\circ}
is abelian, the quotient
\Omega_{\theta^{\circ}}
is abelian.
Assume that
\theta^{\circ}
extends to
I
, and fix such an extension

\widetilde{\theta}\in\operatorname{Irr}(I).

For
\eta\in\operatorname{Irr}(\Omega_{\theta^{\circ}})
, inflated to
I
, define

\widetilde{\theta}_{\eta}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\widetilde{\theta}\otimes\eta,\qquad\sigma_{\eta}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\operatorname{Ind}_{I}^{M}(\widetilde{\theta}_{\eta})\in\operatorname{Irr}(M).

By Clifford theory, the characters
\sigma_{\eta}
, for
\eta\in\operatorname{Irr}(\Omega_{\theta^{\circ}})
,
are precisely the irreducible characters of
M
lying above
\theta^{\circ}
, and

\operatorname{Ind}_{M^{\circ}}^{M}\theta^{\circ}\;\cong\;\bigoplus_{\eta\in\operatorname{Irr}(\Omega_{\theta^{\circ}})}\sigma_{\eta}.

Set

M_{X^{\circ}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=R^{X^{\circ}}_{M^{\circ}\subset P^{\circ}}(\theta^{\circ}),\qquad N_{X}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=R^{X}_{M\subset P}\!\bigl(\operatorname{Ind}_{M^{\circ}}^{M}\theta^{\circ}\bigr).

Let

W_{\theta^{\circ}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\Bigl\{w\in N_{X^{\circ}}(A_{\mathbf{M}})/M^{\circ}\;\Bigm|\;{}^{\,w}\theta^{\circ}\simeq\theta^{\circ}\Bigr\}

be the connected relative Weyl group attached to
(M^{\circ},\theta^{\circ})
.
As in §
10.3
,
W_{\theta^{\circ}}
acts on

\operatorname{Irr}(\Omega_{\theta^{\circ}})
, characterized by

{}^{\,w}\sigma_{\eta}\;\cong\;\sigma_{\,w\cdot\eta}.

For
\eta\in\operatorname{Irr}(\Omega_{\theta^{\circ}})
, put

W_{\sigma_{\eta}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\Bigl\{w\in W_{\theta^{\circ}}\;\Bigm|\;{}^{\,w}\sigma_{\eta}\simeq\sigma_{\eta}\Bigr\}=\operatorname{Stab}_{W_{\theta^{\circ}}}(\eta).

Theorem 10.4

(Disconnected Howlett–Lehrer theorem in the abelian extension case)
.

Assume that
X/X^{\circ}
is abelian and that

\theta^{\circ}\in\operatorname{Irr}(M^{\circ})
extends to its inertia group

I=I_{M}(\theta^{\circ}).

Fix an extension
\widetilde{\theta}\in\operatorname{Irr}(I)
. For

\eta\in\operatorname{Irr}(\Omega_{\theta^{\circ}})
, where

\Omega_{\theta^{\circ}}=I/M^{\circ}
, set

\widetilde{\theta}_{\eta}=\widetilde{\theta}\otimes\eta,\qquad\sigma_{\eta}=\operatorname{Ind}_{I}^{M}(\widetilde{\theta}_{\eta}).

Then, for every
\eta\in\operatorname{Irr}(\Omega_{\theta^{\circ}})
, there is an algebra
isomorphism

\operatorname{End}_{X}\!\bigl(R^{X}_{M\subset P}(\sigma_{\eta})\bigr)^{\operatorname{op}}\cong\mathbb{C}[W_{\sigma_{\eta}}],

where

W_{\sigma_{\eta}}=\{\,w\in W_{\theta^{\circ}}\mid{}^{w}\sigma_{\eta}\simeq\sigma_{\eta}\,\}.

Consequently,

\operatorname{Irr}\bigl(X,(M,\sigma_{\eta})\bigr)\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname{Irr}(W_{\sigma_{\eta}}).

Equivalently, for any
\sigma\in\operatorname{Irr}(M\mid\theta^{\circ})
, one has

\operatorname{End}_{X}\!\bigl(R^{X}_{M\subset P}(\sigma)\bigr)^{\operatorname{op}}\cong\mathbb{C}[W_{\sigma}].

Proof.

Since
\theta^{\circ}
extends to
I
, the Clifford-label cocycle attached to

\theta^{\circ}
is trivial, and so the algebra
\mathcal{A}
of
§
10.3
is the ordinary group algebra

\mathcal{A}=\mathbb{C}[\Omega_{\theta^{\circ}}].

By the decomposition of
\operatorname{Ind}_{M^{\circ}}^{M}\theta^{\circ}
above, we have

N_{X}=R^{X}_{M\subset P}\!\bigl(\operatorname{Ind}_{M^{\circ}}^{M}\theta^{\circ}\bigr)\;\cong\;\bigoplus_{\eta\in\operatorname{Irr}(\Omega_{\theta^{\circ}})}R^{X}_{M\subset P}(\sigma_{\eta})

as an
(X\times\mathcal{A})
-module.

For
\eta\in\operatorname{Irr}(\Omega_{\theta^{\circ}})
, let
e_{\eta}\in Z(\mathcal{A})
be the primitive
central idempotent corresponding to
\eta
. Since
\eta
is one-dimensional, the

\eta
-isotypic component of
N_{X}
is exactly

e_{\eta}N_{X}\;\cong\;R^{X}_{M\subset P}(\sigma_{\eta}).

Hence

(10.1)

e_{\eta}\operatorname{End}_{X}(N_{X})^{\operatorname{op}}e_{\eta}\;\cong\;\operatorname{End}_{X}\!\bigl(R^{X}_{M\subset P}(\sigma_{\eta})\bigr)^{\operatorname{op}}.

By Lemma
10.2
, we have

\operatorname{End}_{X}(N_{X})^{\operatorname{op}}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{A}\rtimes W_{\theta^{\circ}},

where the action of
W_{\theta^{\circ}}
on
\mathcal{A}=\mathbb{C}[\Omega_{\theta^{\circ}}]
is the one
induced by its action on
\operatorname{Irr}(\Omega_{\theta^{\circ}})
.
We now compute the corner
e_{\eta}(\mathcal{A}\rtimes W_{\theta^{\circ}})e_{\eta}
.

Let
[w]
denote the standard basis element attached to
w\in W_{\theta^{\circ}}
.
Since
w
acts on the primitive central idempotents by

[w]\,e_{\eta}=e_{w\cdot\eta}\,[w],

it follows that

e_{\eta}[w]e_{\eta}=0\qquad\text{if }w\notin W_{\sigma_{\eta}}.

On the other hand, if
w\in W_{\sigma_{\eta}}
, then
w\cdot\eta=\eta
, so

[w]\,e_{\eta}=e_{\eta}\,[w]

and therefore

e_{\eta}[w]e_{\eta}=e_{\eta}[w].

Hence

e_{\eta}(\mathcal{A}\rtimes W_{\theta^{\circ}})e_{\eta}=\bigoplus_{w\in W_{\sigma_{\eta}}}\mathbb{C}\,e_{\eta}[w].

Moreover, for
w,w^{\prime}\in W_{\sigma_{\eta}}
,

(e_{\eta}[w])(e_{\eta}[w^{\prime}])=e_{\eta}[w]e_{\eta}[w^{\prime}]=e_{\eta}[w][w^{\prime}]=e_{\eta}[ww^{\prime}].

Thus the assignment

T_{w}\longmapsto e_{\eta}[w]\qquad(w\in W_{\sigma_{\eta}})

extends to an algebra isomorphism

(10.2)

\mathbb{C}[W_{\sigma_{\eta}}]\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }e_{\eta}(\mathcal{A}\rtimes W_{\theta^{\circ}})e_{\eta}.

Combining (
10.1
) and (
10.2
),
we obtain

\operatorname{End}_{X}\!\bigl(R^{X}_{M\subset P}(\sigma_{\eta})\bigr)^{\operatorname{op}}\;\cong\;\mathbb{C}[W_{\sigma_{\eta}}],

as required.

Finally,
R^{X}_{M\subset P}(\sigma_{\eta})
is semisimple over
\mathbb{C}
, so its irreducible
constituents are in canonical bijection with the simple right modules of its
endomorphism algebra. The above isomorphism therefore yields a canonical bijection

\operatorname{Irr}\bigl(X,(M,\sigma_{\eta})\bigr)\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname{Irr}(W_{\sigma_{\eta}}).

∎

Remark 10.5
.

Theorem
10.4
is the form needed later in the construction of
Jordan decomposition for connected groups. In that application one takes

X=H=C_{G^{*}}(s),\qquad M=H_{L}=C_{L^{*}}(s),

and
\theta^{\circ}=u_{\tau}^{\circ}
is a cuspidal unipotent character of
H_{L}^{\circ}
.
By the preferred-extension results proved earlier,
u_{\tau}
is of the form

u_{\tau}=\operatorname{Ind}_{I_{\tau}}^{H_{L}}(\widetilde{u}_{\tau}),\qquad I_{\tau}=\operatorname{Stab}_{H_{L}}(u_{\tau}^{\circ}),

with
\widetilde{u}_{\tau}
an extension of
u_{\tau}^{\circ}
to
I_{\tau}
. Since

H/H^{\circ}
is abelian by Lemma
3.10
, the theorem applies.

11.
Construction of unique Jordan decomposition for connected groups

11.1.
Weyl equivariance of the cuspidal correspondence

Proposition 11.1

(Weyl transport of the cuspidal Jordan decomposition)
.

Let
\mathbf{G}
be a connected reductive group defined over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
, and let
\mathbf{L}
be an

F
-stable Levi factor of an
F
-stable parabolic subgroup of
\mathbf{G}
. Let

\tau\in\mathcal{E}(L,s)_{\operatorname{cusp}},

and let
w\in W_{\mathbf{G}}(A_{\mathbf{L}})
. Denote by
w^{*}\in W_{\mathbf{G}^{*}}(A_{\mathbf{L}^{*}})
the dual Weyl element. Choose representatives, still
denoted
w
and
w^{*}
, inducing dual conjugation automorphisms of
\mathbf{L}

and
\mathbf{L}^{*}
. Put

s^{w}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\operatorname{ad}(w^{*})(s),\qquad\tau^{w}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\tau\circ\operatorname{ad}(w).

Then
\tau^{w}\in\mathcal{E}(L,s^{w})_{\operatorname{cusp}}
, and

(11.1)

\mathbb{J}_{s^{w}}^{L}(\tau^{w})=\mathbb{J}_{s}^{L}(\tau)\circ\operatorname{ad}((w^{*})^{-1}).

Here the right hand side is viewed as a unipotent character of

C_{\mathbf{L}^{*}}(s^{w})^{F^{*}}
through the isomorphism

\operatorname{ad}((w^{*})^{-1})\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}C_{\mathbf{L}^{*}}(s^{w})^{F^{*}}\xrightarrow{\sim}C_{\mathbf{L}^{*}}(s)^{F^{*}}.

In particular, if
w
stabilizes the isomorphism class of
\tau
, then

s^{w}
is
L^{*}
-conjugate to
s
, and after the usual identification of

C_{\mathbf{L}^{*}}(s^{w})
with
C_{\mathbf{L}^{*}}(s)
, the formula becomes the expected
stabilizer-equivariance statement.

Proof.

The assertion that
\tau^{w}\in\mathcal{E}(L,s^{w})
is the standard compatibility of
Lusztig series with dual automorphisms. The equality
(
11.1
) follows from the way the cuspidal map

\mathbb{J}_{s}^{L}
was constructed. The map
\operatorname{ad}(w)
transports the source
diagonal-automorphism orbit and its Clifford data to those attached to

\tau^{w}
, while
\operatorname{ad}(w^{*})
transports the connected centralizer

C_{\mathbf{L}^{*}}(s)
to
C_{\mathbf{L}^{*}}(s^{w})
. The homomorphism

L_{\operatorname{ad}}/L\to(H_{L}/H_{L}^{\circ})^{\vee}
used to match the two Clifford pictures
commutes with these Weyl actions, as recalled in
Remark
3.13
. Finally, the
preferred-extension normalization is stable under transport by pinned
automorphisms: Lusztig’s preferred extension is defined from the based root
datum, and Malle’s matching is used with this transported normalization.
Therefore the extension, Clifford label, and cuspidal unipotent character
obtained from
\tau^{w}
are precisely the
w^{*}
-transports of those obtained
from
\tau
, which is exactly (
11.1
).
∎

Corollary 11.2
.

Let
\tau\in\mathcal{E}(L,s)_{\operatorname{cusp}}
, and let

u_{\tau}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathbb{J}_{s}^{L}(\tau)\in\operatorname{Uch}(H_{L})_{\operatorname{cusp}}.

Then the duality isomorphism identifies the stabilizer of the cuspidal pair

(L,\tau)
with the stabilizer of the corresponding unipotent cuspidal pair

(H_{L},u_{\tau})
:

W_{\tau}\cong W_{u_{\tau}}.

Proof.

Let
w\in W_{\tau}
. Then
\tau^{w}\simeq\tau
, so the preceding proposition
implies that
s^{w}
is
L^{*}
-conjugate to
s
. Replacing the representative

w^{*}
by the corresponding element in
W_{\mathbf{H}}(A_{\mathbf{L}^{*}})
, where

\mathbf{H}=C_{\mathbf{G}^{*}}(s)
, the transport formula
(
11.1
) gives

u_{\tau}\circ\operatorname{ad}((w^{*})^{-1})\simeq u_{\tau}.

Thus
w^{*}\in W_{u_{\tau}}
. The same argument applied to the inverse Weyl
element gives the converse inclusion. Hence duality identifies the two
stabilizers.
∎

11.2.
Passage from cuspidal pairs to Harish–Chandra series

Fix a semisimple element
s\in G^{*}
, and put

\mathbf{H}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=C_{\mathbf{G}^{*}}(s),\qquad H\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathbf{H}^{F^{*}}.

For every
F
-stable Levi subgroup
\mathbf{L}\leq\mathbf{G}
and every

\tau\in\mathcal{E}(L,s)_{\operatorname{cusp}},

write

\mathbf{H}_{L}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=C_{\mathbf{L}^{*}}(s),\qquad H_{L}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathbf{H}_{L}^{F^{*}},\qquad u_{\tau}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathbb{J}_{s}^{L}(\tau)\in\operatorname{Uch}(H_{L})_{\operatorname{cusp}},

where
\mathbb{J}_{s}^{L}
is the cuspidal Jordan decomposition for the connected group
\mathbf{L}

constructed in Lemma
8.2
.

Proposition 11.3
.

Let
\mathbf{L}
be an
F
-stable Levi factor of an
F
-stable parabolic subgroup of

\mathbf{G}
, and let

\tau\in\mathcal{E}(L,s)_{\operatorname{cusp}}.

Then there is a canonically defined bijection

\mathbb{J}_{s,(L,\tau)}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\operatorname{Irr}\bigl(G,(L,\tau)\bigr)\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }\operatorname{Irr}\bigl(H,(H_{L},u_{\tau})\bigr).

More precisely, let

\operatorname{Irr}(W_{\tau})=\{\phi\}

and let

\rho_{\phi}\in\operatorname{Irr}\bigl(G,(L,\tau)\bigr)

be the constituent corresponding to
\phi
under the connected
Howlett–Lehrer comparison theorem. By Corollary
11.2
, we have a
canonical identification

W_{\tau}\xrightarrow{\sim}W_{u_{\tau}}.

Let

u_{\phi}\in\operatorname{Irr}\bigl(H,(H_{L},u_{\tau})\bigr)

be the constituent corresponding to the same
\phi
, now viewed as an irreducible
character of
W_{u_{\tau}}
via the above identification and
Theorem
10.4
. Then

\mathbb{J}_{s,(L,\tau)}(\rho_{\phi})\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=u_{\phi}

defines the required bijection.

Furthermore, if
\mathbf{T}^{*}\subseteq\mathbf{L}^{*}
is an
F^{*}
-stable maximal torus containing

s
, then

\bigl\langle R_{\mathbf{T}^{*}}^{\mathbf{G}}(s),\rho_{\phi}\bigr\rangle_{G}=\epsilon_{\mathbf{G}}\epsilon_{\mathbf{H}}\,\bigl\langle R_{\mathbf{T}^{*}}^{\mathbf{H}}(1),u_{\phi}\bigr\rangle_{H}.

Proof.

By the connected Howlett–Lehrer theorem
(Theorem
4.3
), there is an algebra isomorphism

\operatorname{End}_{G}\!\bigl(R_{\mathbf{L}}^{\mathbf{G}}(\tau)\bigr)^{\operatorname{op}}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathbb{C}[W_{\tau}],

hence a canonical bijection

\operatorname{Irr}\bigl(G,(L,\tau)\bigr)\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname{Irr}(W_{\tau}),\qquad\rho_{\phi}\longleftrightarrow\phi.

Likewise, by
Theorem
10.4
, there is an algebra isomorphism

\operatorname{End}_{H}\!\bigl(R_{\mathbf{H}_{L}}^{\mathbf{H}}(u_{\tau})\bigr)^{\operatorname{op}}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathbb{C}[W_{u_{\tau}}],

hence a canonical bijection

\operatorname{Irr}\bigl(H,(H_{L},u_{\tau})\bigr)\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname{Irr}(W_{u_{\tau}}),\qquad u_{\phi}\longleftrightarrow\phi.

Using Corollary
11.2
to identify
W_{\tau}
with
W_{u_{\tau}}
, we
obtain the bijection
\mathbb{J}_{s,(L,\tau)}
.

We now prove the scalar-product identity. Since
\rho_{\phi}
corresponds to
\phi
,
the regular-module decomposition of

\operatorname{End}_{G}(R_{\mathbf{L}}^{\mathbf{G}}(\tau))\cong\mathbb{C}[W_{\tau}]
yields

R_{\mathbf{L}}^{\mathbf{G}}(\tau)=\sum_{\phi\in\operatorname{Irr}(W_{\tau})}\phi(1)\rho_{\phi}.

By Frobenius reciprocity, this implies

\bigl\langle{}^{*}R_{\mathbf{L}}^{\mathbf{G}}(\rho_{\phi}),\tau\bigr\rangle_{L}=\phi(1).

Since
\tau
is cuspidal, the Harish–Chandra series of
L
attached to

(L,\tau)
is the singleton
\{\tau\}
. Therefore

{}^{*}R_{\mathbf{L}}^{\mathbf{G}}(\rho_{\phi})=\phi(1)\tau.

Using transitivity of Lusztig induction and Frobenius reciprocity, we obtain

\bigl\langle R_{\mathbf{T}^{*}}^{\mathbf{G}}(s),\rho_{\phi}\bigr\rangle_{G}=\bigl\langle R_{\mathbf{T}^{*}}^{\mathbf{L}}(s),{}^{*}R_{\mathbf{L}}^{\mathbf{G}}(\rho_{\phi})\bigr\rangle_{L}=\phi(1)\bigl\langle R_{\mathbf{T}^{*}}^{\mathbf{L}}(s),\tau\bigr\rangle_{L}.

On the
H
-side, the same argument gives

R_{\mathbf{H}_{L}}^{\mathbf{H}}(u_{\tau})=\sum_{\phi\in\operatorname{Irr}(W_{u_{\tau}})}\phi(1)u_{\phi},

hence

{}^{*}R_{\mathbf{H}_{L}}^{\mathbf{H}}(u_{\phi})=\phi(1)u_{\tau},

because
u_{\tau}
is cuspidal in
H_{L}
. Therefore

\bigl\langle R_{\mathbf{T}^{*}}^{\mathbf{H}}(1),u_{\phi}\bigr\rangle_{H}=\bigl\langle R_{\mathbf{T}^{*}}^{\mathbf{H}_{L}}(1),{}^{*}R_{\mathbf{H}_{L}}^{\mathbf{H}}(u_{\phi})\bigr\rangle_{H_{L}}=\phi(1)\bigl\langle R_{\mathbf{T}^{*}}^{\mathbf{H}_{L}}(1),u_{\tau}\bigr\rangle_{H_{L}}.

By Lemma
8.2
, applied to the connected Levi subgroup

\mathbf{L}
, we have

\bigl\langle R_{\mathbf{T}^{*}}^{\mathbf{L}}(s),\tau\bigr\rangle_{L}=\epsilon_{\mathbf{L}}\epsilon_{\mathbf{H}_{L}}\,\bigl\langle R_{\mathbf{T}^{*}}^{\mathbf{H}_{L}}(1),u_{\tau}\bigr\rangle_{H_{L}}.

Since
\mathbf{H}_{L}^{\circ}
is the Levi subgroup of
\mathbf{H}^{\circ}
corresponding to

\mathbf{L}\subseteq\mathbf{G}
, one has

\epsilon_{\mathbf{L}}\epsilon_{\mathbf{H}_{L}}=\epsilon_{\mathbf{G}}\epsilon_{\mathbf{H}}.

Combining the preceding equalities yields

\bigl\langle R_{\mathbf{T}^{*}}^{\mathbf{G}}(s),\rho_{\phi}\bigr\rangle_{G}=\epsilon_{\mathbf{G}}\epsilon_{\mathbf{H}}\,\bigl\langle R_{\mathbf{T}^{*}}^{\mathbf{H}}(1),u_{\phi}\bigr\rangle_{H}.

This proves the proposition.
∎

11.3.
Construction of
\mathbb{J}_{s}

Theorem 11.4
.

Let
\mathbf{G}
be a connected reductive group defined over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
, equipped with a
pinning
\mathcal{P}
. Let
s\in G^{*}
be semisimple, and put

\mathbf{H}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=C_{\mathbf{G}^{*}}(s),\qquad H\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathbf{H}^{F^{*}}.

Then there exists a uniquely determined bijection

\mathbb{J}_{s}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathcal{E}(G,s)\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }\operatorname{Uch}(H)

such that the following conditions hold.

(1)

The restriction of
\mathbb{J}_{s}
to
\mathcal{E}(G,s)_{\operatorname{cusp}}
is the cuspidal bijection

\mathbb{J}_{s}^{G}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathcal{E}(G,s)_{\operatorname{cusp}}\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname{Uch}(H)_{\operatorname{cusp}}

constructed in Lemma
8.2
.

(2)

For every
\rho\in\mathcal{E}(G,s)
and every
F^{*}
-stable maximal torus

\mathbf{T}^{*}\subseteq\mathbf{G}^{*}
containing
s
, one has

\bigl\langle R_{\mathbf{T}^{*}}^{\mathbf{G}}(s),\rho\bigr\rangle_{G}=\epsilon_{\mathbf{G}}\epsilon_{\mathbf{H}}\,\bigl\langle R_{\mathbf{T}^{*}}^{\mathbf{H}}(1),\mathbb{J}_{s}(\rho)\bigr\rangle_{H}.

(3)

If

\mathcal{E}(G,s)=\bigsqcup_{[(L,\tau)]\in\Sigma_{G}(s)}\operatorname{Irr}\bigl(G,(L,\tau)\bigr)

is the Harish–Chandra decomposition of the Lusztig series, then

\mathbb{J}_{s}
restricts on each Harish–Chandra series to the bijection

\mathbb{J}_{s,(L,\tau)}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\operatorname{Irr}\bigl(G,(L,\tau)\bigr)\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname{Irr}\bigl(H,(H_{L},u_{\tau})\bigr)

of Proposition
11.3
, where

u_{\tau}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathbb{J}_{s}^{L}(\tau)\in\operatorname{Uch}(H_{L})_{\operatorname{cusp}}.

Proof.

By Proposition
4.2
, we have a disjoint decomposition

\mathcal{E}(G,s)=\bigsqcup_{[(L,\tau)]\in\Sigma_{G}(s)}\operatorname{Irr}\bigl(G,(L,\tau)\bigr).

On the
H
-side, Harish–Chandra theory for the possibly disconnected group
H

gives a disjoint decomposition

\operatorname{Uch}(H)=\bigsqcup_{[(M,u)]}\operatorname{Irr}\bigl(H,(M,u)\bigr),

where
(M,u)
runs over the
H
-conjugacy classes of cuspidal unipotent pairs in

H
.

By Lemma
8.2
, applied to all connected Levi subgroups of

\mathbf{G}
, together with Proposition
11.1
, the assignment

(L,\tau)\longmapsto\bigl(H_{L},\mathbb{J}_{s}^{L}(\tau)\bigr)

induces a bijection from
\Sigma_{G}(s)
onto the set of
H
-conjugacy classes of
cuspidal unipotent pairs in
H
. Therefore, for each

[(L,\tau)]\in\Sigma_{G}(s)
, Proposition
11.3
provides a
canonical bijection

\mathbb{J}_{s,(L,\tau)}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\operatorname{Irr}\bigl(G,(L,\tau)\bigr)\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname{Irr}\bigl(H,(H_{L},u_{\tau})\bigr).

Taking the disjoint union of these bijections over all

[(L,\tau)]\in\Sigma_{G}(s)
, we obtain a well-defined bijection

\mathbb{J}_{s}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathcal{E}(G,s)\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname{Uch}(H).

Property
(3)
is built into the definition. Property
(1)
follows because when

L=G
, the Harish–Chandra series
\operatorname{Irr}(G,(G,\rho))
is the singleton

\{\rho\}
, and the above construction recovers the cuspidal bijection

\mathbb{J}_{s}^{G}
. Finally, Property
(2)
is exactly the scalar-product identity proved in
Proposition
11.3
on each Harish–Chandra series.

The uniqueness of
\mathbb{J}_{s}
is immediate from the disjoint decomposition of

\mathcal{E}(G,s)
into Harish–Chandra series and the fact that its restriction to each
series is prescribed by Proposition
11.3
.
∎

12.
Cuspidal Jordan decomposition for disconnected
\mathbf{G}

The preceding sections establish the connected Jordan decomposition. We now
pass to the Clifford-theoretic extension needed when the group whose characters
are being decomposed is itself disconnected. From this point onward the
following rational condition is imposed on the finite rational components of
that disconnected group.

Hypothesis 12.1

(Rational pinned-component condition)
.

Let
\mathbf{G}
be a possibly disconnected reductive group over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
, with
identity component
\mathbf{G}^{\circ}
, and put

G\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathbf{G}^{F},\qquad G^{\circ}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathbf{G}^{\circ F},\qquad\Omega_{G}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=G/G^{\circ}.

Fix an
F
-stable pinning
\mathcal{P}
of
\mathbf{G}^{\circ}
. Define

G_{\mathcal{P}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\{g\in G\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\operatorname{Ad}(g)|_{\mathbf{G}^{\circ}}\text{ preserves }\mathcal{P}\}.

We assume that the natural map

G_{\mathcal{P}}\longrightarrow\Omega_{G}

is surjective. Equivalently,

G=G^{\circ}\cdot G_{\mathcal{P}}.

Whenever the disconnected Jordan decomposition is applied to an
F
-stable
regular Levi subgroup
\mathbf{L}\subset\mathbf{G}
, we impose the same condition
on
L\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathbf{L}^{F}
with the pinning induced on
\mathbf{L}^{\circ}
.

Remark 12.2

(Scope of the disconnected theory)
.

Hypothesis
12.1
is imposed only in the
disconnected part of the paper, where the group whose characters are being
decomposed may be disconnected. It is not a hypothesis in the connected Jordan
decomposition theorem: there the character side is the finite group of points of
a connected reductive group, while the possibly disconnected group

C_{\mathbf{G}^{*}}(s)^{F^{*}}
occurs on the unipotent side.

The disconnected results below are not intended as a Jordan decomposition for
every disconnected reductive finite group with abelian component group. The
extra rational pinned-component condition is imposed because the primary
application is to depth-zero local Langlands constructions (see
[
23
]
), where the relevant
finite groups are full reductive quotients of parahoric stabilizers. In that
setting the components come from vertex stabilizers, and the required input is
that their action on the connected reductive quotient is pinning-preserving
modulo inner conjugacy by the connected finite quotient. Thus the disconnected
theory used here is tailored to the finite groups that occur in the local
Langlands application, and it excludes the diagonal-automorphism pathology in
which an algebraically inner, but not rationally inner, automorphism acts
nontrivially on characters of the finite identity component.

Throughout the rest of this section,
\mathbf{G}
is a possibly disconnected reductive
group over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
, equipped with an
F
-stable pinning
\mathcal{P}
of

\mathbf{G}^{\circ}
. Set
G\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathbf{G}^{F}
,
G^{\circ}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathbf{G}^{\circ F}
, and

\Omega\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=G/G^{\circ}
. We assume that
\Omega
is abelian and that

(\mathbf{G},\mathcal{P})
satisfies Hypothesis
12.1
.

Fix
s\in\mathbf{G}^{\circ*\,F^{*}}=(\mathbf{G}^{*})^{\circ F^{*}}
semisimple and put

\mathbf{H}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=C_{\mathbf{G}^{*}}(s),\qquad\mathbf{H}_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=C_{\mathbf{G}^{\circ*}}(s)=C_{(\mathbf{G}^{*})^{\circ}}(s),\qquad\mathbf{H}^{\circ}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathbf{H}_{0}^{\circ}=C_{\mathbf{G}^{\circ*}}(s)^{\circ}.

Set

H\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathbf{H}^{F^{*}},\qquad H_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathbf{H}_{0}^{F^{*}},\qquad H^{\circ}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=(\mathbf{H}^{\circ})^{F^{*}}.

Thus
H_{0}
is, in general, disconnected, while
H^{\circ}
is its identity-component finite subgroup.

For each
\omega\in\Omega
, let

\sigma_{\omega}\in\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbf{G}^{\circ},\mathcal{P})

be the pinned representative of the outer action from Definition
9.5
,
and let

\sigma_{\omega}^{*}\in\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbf{G}^{\circ*},\mathcal{P}^{*})

be the dual pinned automorphism. We shall use these pinned automorphisms below only up to rational conjugacy of
semisimple parameters. Thus, for a semisimple element

s\in\mathbf{G}^{\circ*F^{*}}
, the relevant component group is the stabilizer of
the
\mathbf{G}^{\circ*F^{*}}
-conjugacy class of
s
, not necessarily the
pointwise stabilizer of
s
under the automorphisms
\sigma_{\omega}^{*}
.

12.0.1.
Stabilizer matching and the source Clifford class

Keep the notation fixed at the beginning of this section:

\mathbf{H}=C_{\mathbf{G}^{*}}(s),\qquad\mathbf{H}_{0}=C_{\mathbf{G}^{\circ*}}(s)=C_{(\mathbf{G}^{*})^{\circ}}(s),\qquad H=\mathbf{H}^{F^{*}},\qquad H_{0}=\mathbf{H}_{0}^{F^{*}}.

Thus
H_{0}\lhd H
. The point to keep in mind is that

\mathbf{H}
is not, in general, the semidirect product

\mathbf{H}_{0}\rtimes\Omega_{s}
, where

\Omega_{s}=\{\omega\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\sigma_{\omega}^{*}(s)=s\}
. Rather, the relevant
component group is the stabilizer of the finite rational conjugacy class of

s
.

Put

\Omega_{[s]}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\left\{\omega\in\Omega\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\sigma_{\omega}^{*}(s)\text{ is }\mathbf{G}^{\circ*F^{*}}\text{-conjugate to }s\right\}.

For
\omega\in\Omega_{[s]}
, choose

x_{\omega}\in\mathbf{G}^{\circ*F^{*}},\qquad x_{1}=1,

such that

(12.1)

x_{\omega}\,\sigma_{\omega}^{*}(s)\,x_{\omega}^{-1}=s.

Then

h_{\omega}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=(x_{\omega},\omega)\in\mathbf{G}^{\circ*F^{*}}\rtimes\Omega

belongs to
H
. Moreover, the projection
H\to\Omega
has image

\Omega_{[s]}
, and one has an exact sequence

(12.2)

1\longrightarrow H_{0}\longrightarrow H\longrightarrow\Omega_{[s]}\longrightarrow 1.

For
\omega,\eta\in\Omega_{[s]}
, the chosen section

\omega\mapsto h_{\omega}
has factor set

(12.3)

c_{s}(\omega,\eta)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=h_{\omega}h_{\eta}h_{\omega\eta}^{-1}=x_{\omega}\,\sigma_{\omega}^{*}(x_{\eta})\,x_{\omega\eta}^{-1}\in H_{0}.

Let

\rho^{\circ}\in\mathcal{E}(G^{\circ},s)_{\operatorname{cusp}}

and set

u_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathbb{J}_{s}^{G^{\circ}}(\rho^{\circ})\in\operatorname{Uch}(H_{0})_{\operatorname{cusp}},

where
\mathbb{J}_{s}^{G^{\circ}}
denotes the pinned cuspidal Jordan decomposition for
the connected group
\mathbf{G}^{\circ}
. Define

\Omega_{\rho^{\circ}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\operatorname{im}\bigl(I_{G}(\rho^{\circ})\to G/G^{\circ}=\Omega\bigr),

and

\Omega_{u_{0}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\operatorname{im}\bigl(I_{H}(u_{0})\to H/H_{0}\hookrightarrow\Omega\bigr).

Lemma 12.3

(Stabilizer matching)
.

With the notation above, one has

\Omega_{\rho^{\circ}}=\Omega_{u_{0}}\subseteq\Omega_{[s]}.

Denote this common subgroup by
\Omega_{0}
. Via the projection to

\Omega_{0}
, there are canonical identifications

I_{G}(\rho^{\circ})/G^{\circ}\cong\Omega_{0},\qquad I_{H}(u_{0})/H_{0}\cong\Omega_{0}.

In particular, the Clifford class attached to the source normal inclusion

G^{\circ}\lhd I_{G}(\rho^{\circ})

may be regarded as a canonical class

[\alpha_{\rho^{\circ}}]\in H^{2}(\Omega_{0},\mathbb{C}^{\times}).

Proof.

We first record the centralizer calculation. Since

\mathbf{G}^{*}=\mathbf{G}^{\circ*}\rtimes\Omega,

an element
(g,\omega)\in\mathbf{G}^{\circ*}\rtimes\Omega
centralizes
s
if
and only if

(g,\omega)(s,1)(g,\omega)^{-1}=(g\,\sigma_{\omega}^{*}(s)\,g^{-1},1)=(s,1).

Equivalently,

g\,\sigma_{\omega}^{*}(s)\,g^{-1}=s.

Thus
\omega
need not fix
s
itself; it is enough and necessary that

\sigma_{\omega}^{*}(s)
be
\mathbf{G}^{\circ*}
-conjugate to
s
. On rational
points this gives (
12.2
).

We next compare the two stabilizers. Let
\omega\in\Omega_{\rho^{\circ}}
.
Choose first any representative of
\omega
in
I_{G}(\rho^{\circ})
. By
Hypothesis
12.1
we may replace it,
modulo left multiplication by an element of
G^{\circ}
, by a representative

\dot{\omega}\in I_{G}(\rho^{\circ})\cap G_{\mathcal{P}}.

Inner conjugation by
G^{\circ}
is invisible on characters of
G^{\circ}
. For
this representative, conjugation on
G^{\circ}
is the pinned automorphism

\sigma_{\omega}
. Since pullback by
\sigma_{\omega}^{-1}
sends

\mathcal{E}(G^{\circ},s)\quad\text{onto}\quad\mathcal{E}(G^{\circ},\sigma_{\omega}^{*}(s)),

and since
{}^{\dot{\omega}}\rho^{\circ}\simeq\rho^{\circ}
, the two connected
Lusztig series

\mathcal{E}(G^{\circ},s)\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{E}(G^{\circ},\sigma_{\omega}^{*}(s))

coincide. Hence
s
and
\sigma_{\omega}^{*}(s)
are

\mathbf{G}^{\circ*F^{*}}
-conjugate. Thus
\omega\in\Omega_{[s]}
, and we may
choose
x_{\omega}\in\mathbf{G}^{\circ*F^{*}}
satisfying
(
12.1
). Then

h_{\omega}=(x_{\omega},\omega)\in H.

The connected pinned Jordan decomposition is equivariant for the following
transport operation: first apply the pinned automorphism
\sigma_{\omega}
on
the
G^{\circ}
-side, which sends
s
to
\sigma_{\omega}^{*}(s)
, and then
conjugate on the dual side by
x_{\omega}
, which brings

\sigma_{\omega}^{*}(s)
back to
s
. Therefore, for every

\chi\in\mathcal{E}(G^{\circ},s)_{\operatorname{cusp}}
,

(12.4)

\mathbb{J}_{s}^{G^{\circ}}({}^{\dot{\omega}}\chi)={}^{h_{\omega}}\mathbb{J}_{s}^{G^{\circ}}(\chi).

Applying (
12.4
) to
\chi=\rho^{\circ}
, and
using
{}^{\dot{\omega}}\rho^{\circ}\simeq\rho^{\circ}
, gives

{}^{h_{\omega}}u_{0}={}^{h_{\omega}}\mathbb{J}_{s}^{G^{\circ}}(\rho^{\circ})=\mathbb{J}_{s}^{G^{\circ}}({}^{\dot{\omega}}\rho^{\circ})\simeq\mathbb{J}_{s}^{G^{\circ}}(\rho^{\circ})=u_{0}.

Thus
h_{\omega}\in I_{H}(u_{0})
, and hence

\Omega_{\rho^{\circ}}\subseteq\Omega_{u_{0}}.

Conversely, let
\omega\in\Omega_{u_{0}}
, and choose

h=(x,\omega)\in I_{H}(u_{0})
. Since
h\in H=C_{\mathbf{G}^{*}}(s)^{F^{*}}
, we have

x\,\sigma_{\omega}^{*}(s)\,x^{-1}=s.

By Hypothesis
12.1
, choose a
pinning-preserving representative
\dot{\omega}\in G_{\mathcal{P}}
of
\omega
.
The same equivariance argument gives

\mathbb{J}_{s}^{G^{\circ}}({}^{\dot{\omega}}\rho^{\circ})={}^{h}\mathbb{J}_{s}^{G^{\circ}}(\rho^{\circ})={}^{h}u_{0}\simeq u_{0}=\mathbb{J}_{s}^{G^{\circ}}(\rho^{\circ}).

Since
\mathbb{J}_{s}^{G^{\circ}}
is injective on
\mathcal{E}(G^{\circ},s)_{\operatorname{cusp}}
, it follows
that

{}^{\dot{\omega}}\rho^{\circ}\simeq\rho^{\circ}.

Hence
\omega\in\Omega_{\rho^{\circ}}
. Therefore

\Omega_{\rho^{\circ}}=\Omega_{u_{0}}=\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\Omega_{0}.

The projection maps now give the announced identifications

I_{G}(\rho^{\circ})/G^{\circ}\cong\Omega_{0},\qquad I_{H}(u_{0})/H_{0}\cong\Omega_{0},

because their kernels are respectively
G^{\circ}
and
H_{0}
. Clifford
theory for the normal inclusion
G^{\circ}\lhd I_{G}(\rho^{\circ})
therefore gives
the stated canonical cohomology class on
\Omega_{0}
.
∎

Definition 12.4

(Projective labels with prescribed multiplier)
.

Let
A
be a finite group and let
[\alpha]\in H^{2}(A,\mathbb{C}^{\times})
. We write

\operatorname{Irr}_{[\alpha]}(A)

for the set of irreducible projective representations of
A
with multiplier
in the class
[\alpha]
. Equivalently, after choosing a normalized cocycle
representative
\alpha\in Z^{2}(A,\mathbb{C}^{\times})
, it is the set

\operatorname{Irr}(\mathbb{C}_{\alpha}[A])
, with the usual identification under replacing

\alpha
by a cohomologous cocycle.

Definition 12.5

(Cuspidal enriched unipotent target)
.

With the above notation, define

\operatorname{Uch}^{\mathrm{enh}}_{G,s}(H)_{\operatorname{cusp}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\left\{(u_{0},[\alpha_{\rho^{\circ}}],E)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\begin{array}[]{l}u_{0}=\mathbb{J}_{s}^{G^{\circ}}(\rho^{\circ}),\quad\rho^{\circ}\in\mathcal{E}(G^{\circ},s)_{\operatorname{cusp}},\\[5.69054pt]
E\in\operatorname{Irr}_{[\alpha_{\rho^{\circ}}]}(\Omega_{\rho^{\circ}})\end{array}\right\}/\Omega_{[s]}.

Here
\Omega_{[s]}
acts by conjugating
\rho^{\circ}
, hence also the
corresponding unipotent character
u_{0}
, the stabilizer

\Omega_{\rho^{\circ}}
, the Clifford class
[\alpha_{\rho^{\circ}}]
, and the
projective label
E
. Thus the notation records precisely the source
Clifford class and source Clifford label, transported to the unipotent side as
part of the enhanced datum.

12.1.
The cuspidal disconnected JD

12.1.1.
Lusztig series for
G^{F}
attached to
s\in(G^{*})^{\circ F^{*}}

We keep the standing assumptions and notation from §9:
G
is a (possibly disconnected) reductive

\mathbb{F}_{q}
–group with Frobenius
F
,
G^{\circ}\lhd G
is the identity component, and
\Omega\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=G^{F}/G^{\circ F}

is abelian. Fix an
F
–stable pinning
\mathbf{P}
of
G^{\circ}
, and assume the rational
pinned-component condition of Hypothesis
12.1
. As in Definition
9.8
, this yields a semidirect-product model

G^{*}\;=\;G^{\circ*}\rtimes\Omega,

together with pinned automorphisms
\sigma_{\omega}\in\operatorname{Aut}(G^{\circ},\mathbf{P})
commuting with
F
and their
duals
\sigma_{\omega}^{*}\in\operatorname{Aut}(G^{\circ*})
for
\omega\in\Omega
.

Definition 12.6

(Disconnected Lusztig series in the identity-component case)
.

Let
s\in(G^{*})^{\circ F^{*}}=G^{\circ*F^{*}}
be semisimple. Define

\mathcal{E}(G^{F},s)\;\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\;\Bigl\{\;\chi\in\operatorname{Irr}(G^{F})\ \Bigm|\ \operatorname{Res}^{G^{F}}_{G^{\circ F}}\chi\ \text{has some constituent }\chi^{\circ}\in\mathcal{E}(G^{\circ F},s)\Bigr\}.

Equivalently,

\mathcal{E}(G^{F},s)\;=\;\bigcup_{\chi^{\circ}\in\mathcal{E}(G^{\circ F},s)}\operatorname{Irr}(G^{F}\mid\chi^{\circ}),

where
\operatorname{Irr}(G^{F}\mid\chi^{\circ})
denotes the set of irreducible characters of
G^{F}
lying above
\chi^{\circ}
.

Proposition 12.7

(Partition of
\operatorname{Irr}(G^{F})
by identity-component series)
.

As
s
varies over the
G^{*F^{*}}
–conjugacy classes of semisimple elements of
(G^{*})^{\circ F^{*}}
, the sets

\mathcal{E}(G^{F},s)
form a partition of
\operatorname{Irr}(G^{F})
:
Let

\mathcal{S}(G^{*})\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\{\,s\in(G^{*})^{\circ F^{*}}\mid s\text{ semisimple}\,\}/G^{*F^{*}}.

Then

\operatorname{Irr}(G^{F})=\bigsqcup_{[s]\in\mathcal{S}(G^{*})}\mathcal{E}(G^{F},s).

Proof.

Let first
s,s^{\prime}\in(G^{*})^{\circ F^{*}}
be semisimple elements whose images

(s,1)
and
(s^{\prime},1)
are
G^{*F^{*}}
–conjugate. Choose

(g,\omega)\in G^{*F^{*}}=G^{\circ*F^{*}}\rtimes\Omega
such that

(s^{\prime},1)=(g,\omega)(s,1)(g,\omega)^{-1}=(g\,\sigma_{\omega}^{*}(s)\,g^{-1},1).

Since connected Lusztig series depend only on the

G^{\circ*F^{*}}
–conjugacy class of the semisimple parameter, this gives

(12.5)

\mathcal{E}(G^{\circ F},s^{\prime})=\mathcal{E}(G^{\circ F},g\,\sigma_{\omega}^{*}(s)\,g^{-1})=\mathcal{E}(G^{\circ F},\sigma_{\omega}^{*}(s)).

By functoriality of Lusztig series under pinned automorphisms, pullback along

\sigma_{\omega}^{-1}
induces a bijection

\omega\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\operatorname{Irr}(G^{\circ F})\to\operatorname{Irr}(G^{\circ F}),\qquad\chi^{\circ}\longmapsto\omega\chi^{\circ}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\chi^{\circ}\circ\sigma_{\omega}^{-1},

satisfying

(12.6)

\omega\bigl(\mathcal{E}(G^{\circ F},s)\bigr)=\mathcal{E}(G^{\circ F},\sigma_{\omega}^{*}(s)).

On the other hand, by Hypothesis
12.1

we may choose a lift
\dot{\omega}\in G^{F}
of
\omega
whose conjugation action
on
G^{\circ F}
is the pinned action corresponding to pullback by

\sigma_{\omega}^{-1}
. Conjugation by this lift permutes the irreducible
constituents of
\operatorname{Res}^{G^{F}}_{G^{\circ F}}\chi
. An arbitrary lift differs
from it by an element of
G^{\circ F}
, hence gives the same action on
characters. Therefore

\operatorname{Res}^{G^{F}}_{G^{\circ F}}\chi\text{ has a constituent in }\mathcal{E}(G^{\circ F},s)

if and only if

\operatorname{Res}^{G^{F}}_{G^{\circ F}}\chi\text{ has a constituent in }\mathcal{E}(G^{\circ F},\sigma_{\omega}^{*}(s)).

Together with (
12.5
), this shows that

\chi\in\mathcal{E}(G^{F},s)\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\chi\in\mathcal{E}(G^{F},s^{\prime}),

and hence
\mathcal{E}(G^{F},s)
depends only on the
G^{*F^{*}}
–conjugacy class of

(s,1)
.

We next prove that the resulting subsets exhaust
\operatorname{Irr}(G^{F})
. Let

\chi\in\operatorname{Irr}(G^{F})
, and choose an irreducible constituent
\chi^{\circ}
of

\operatorname{Res}^{G^{F}}_{G^{\circ F}}\chi
. For the connected group
G^{\circ}
, the
series
\mathcal{E}(G^{\circ F},t)
, with
t
running over semisimple elements of

G^{\circ*F^{*}}
up to
G^{\circ*F^{*}}
–conjugacy, form a partition of

\operatorname{Irr}(G^{\circ F})
. Thus
\chi^{\circ}\in\mathcal{E}(G^{\circ F},s)
for some
semisimple
s\in G^{\circ*F^{*}}=(G^{*})^{\circ F^{*}}
, and
Definition
12.6
gives
\chi\in\mathcal{E}(G^{F},s)
.

It remains to prove disjointness after indexing by
G^{*F^{*}}
–conjugacy
classes. Suppose

\chi\in\mathcal{E}(G^{F},s_{1})\cap\mathcal{E}(G^{F},s_{2})

for semisimple
s_{1},s_{2}\in(G^{*})^{\circ F^{*}}
. Choose constituents

\chi_{1}^{\circ},\chi_{2}^{\circ}
of
\operatorname{Res}^{G^{F}}_{G^{\circ F}}\chi
such that

\chi_{i}^{\circ}\in\mathcal{E}(G^{\circ F},s_{i})
. By Clifford theory, the constituents
of
\operatorname{Res}^{G^{F}}_{G^{\circ F}}\chi
form a single
G^{F}
–orbit. Hence there is
an element
g\in G^{F}
such that

\chi_{2}^{\circ}\simeq{}^{g}\chi_{1}^{\circ}
. If
\omega
denotes the image of
g

in
\Omega
, then
{}^{g}\chi_{1}^{\circ}
is the same constituent as

\omega\chi_{1}^{\circ}
in the notation above. By
(
12.6
),

\omega\chi_{1}^{\circ}\in\mathcal{E}(G^{\circ F},\sigma_{\omega}^{*}(s_{1})).

Thus

\chi_{2}^{\circ}\in\mathcal{E}(G^{\circ F},s_{2})\cap\mathcal{E}(G^{\circ F},\sigma_{\omega}^{*}(s_{1})).

Since the connected Lusztig series form a partition of

\operatorname{Irr}(G^{\circ F})
, the elements
s_{2}
and
\sigma_{\omega}^{*}(s_{1})
are

G^{\circ*F^{*}}
–conjugate. Equivalently,
(s_{2},1)
and
(s_{1},1)
are

G^{*F^{*}}
–conjugate. Therefore two distinct
G^{*F^{*}}
–conjugacy classes
of semisimple elements in
(G^{*})^{\circ F^{*}}
give disjoint subsets. Combining
exhaustion and disjointness proves the asserted partition.
∎

Theorem 12.8

(Cuspidal enriched Jordan decomposition for disconnected
\mathbf{G}
)
.

Let
\mathbf{G}
be a possibly disconnected reductive
\mathbb{F}_{q}
-group with abelian
component group, equipped with an
F
-stable pinning
\mathcal{P}
of

\mathbf{G}^{\circ}
. Assume that
(\mathbf{G},\mathcal{P})
satisfies
Hypothesis
12.1
. For

s\in(\mathbf{G}^{*})^{\circ F^{*}}
semisimple, put

\mathbf{H}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=C_{\mathbf{G}^{*}}(s),\qquad H\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathbf{H}^{F^{*}}.

There is a canonical bijection

J^{\mathrm{enh},\operatorname{cusp}}_{G,s}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathcal{E}(G,s)_{\operatorname{cusp}}\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }\operatorname{Uch}^{\mathrm{enh}}_{G,s}(H)_{\operatorname{cusp}},

depending only on the fixed pinning. If
\rho\in\mathcal{E}(G,s)_{\operatorname{cusp}}
and

\rho^{\circ}\prec\operatorname{Res}^{G}_{G^{\circ}}\rho
, then

J^{\mathrm{enh},\operatorname{cusp}}_{G,s}(\rho)=\left[\mathbb{J}_{s}^{G^{\circ}}(\rho^{\circ}),[\alpha_{\rho^{\circ}}],E_{\rho}\right],

where
E_{\rho}\in\operatorname{Irr}_{[\alpha_{\rho^{\circ}}]}(\Omega_{\rho^{\circ}})
is the
Clifford label of
\rho
for the normal inclusion

G^{\circ}\lhd I_{G}(\rho^{\circ}).

Proof.

Let
\rho\in\mathcal{E}(G,s)_{\operatorname{cusp}}
, and choose an irreducible constituent

\rho^{\circ}\prec\operatorname{Res}^{G}_{G^{\circ}}\rho.

Then
\rho^{\circ}\in\mathcal{E}(G^{\circ},s)_{\operatorname{cusp}}
. Put

u_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathbb{J}_{s}^{G^{\circ}}(\rho^{\circ})\in\operatorname{Uch}\bigl(C_{\mathbf{G}^{\circ*}}(s)^{F^{*}}\bigr)_{\operatorname{cusp}}.

By Lemma
12.3
, the stabilizers of
\rho^{\circ}
and

u_{0}
have the same image
\Omega_{\rho^{\circ}}=\Omega_{u_{0}}
in the pinned
component group. Clifford theory for

G^{\circ}\lhd I_{G}(\rho^{\circ})

attaches to
\rho^{\circ}
a canonical class

[\alpha_{\rho^{\circ}}]\in H^{2}(\Omega_{\rho^{\circ}},\mathbb{C}^{\times})
, and, after
choosing any cocycle representative, parametrizes the irreducible characters of

G
lying above
\rho^{\circ}
by

\operatorname{Irr}_{[\alpha_{\rho^{\circ}}]}(\Omega_{\rho^{\circ}}).

Let
E_{\rho}
be the resulting Clifford label of
\rho
. We define

J^{\mathrm{enh},\operatorname{cusp}}_{G,s}(\rho)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=[u_{0},[\alpha_{\rho^{\circ}}],E_{\rho}]\in\operatorname{Uch}^{\mathrm{enh}}_{G,s}(H)_{\operatorname{cusp}}.

This is independent of the choice of
\rho^{\circ}
. Indeed, any other
irreducible constituent of
\operatorname{Res}^{G}_{G^{\circ}}\rho
is a
G
-conjugate of

\rho^{\circ}
. Conjugation transports the inertia group, the quotient

\Omega_{\rho^{\circ}}
, the Clifford class, and the projective label, and the
quotient in Definition
12.5
identifies the resulting data.

The inverse map is obtained by reversing this construction. Given an enhanced
datum represented by
(u_{0},[\alpha],E)
, take

\rho^{\circ}=(\mathbb{J}_{s}^{G^{\circ}})^{-1}(u_{0}).

By the definition of the target,
[\alpha]=[\alpha_{\rho^{\circ}}]
, and Clifford
theory reconstructs a unique irreducible character

\rho_{E}\in\operatorname{Irr}(G\mid\rho^{\circ})

with Clifford label
E
. This construction is again invariant under replacing
the representative of the enhanced datum by a conjugate one. Hence

J^{\mathrm{enh},\operatorname{cusp}}_{G,s}
is a canonical bijection.
∎

13.
An enriched Jordan decomposition for disconnected
G
with rationally pinned abelian component group

Throughout this section we keep the standing notation of the preceding disconnected sections:

G
is a possibly disconnected reductive
\mathbb{F}_{q}
-group with Frobenius
F
,

G^{\circ}\lhd G
is the identity component, we write
G\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=G^{F}
,

G^{\circ}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=(G^{\circ})^{F}
, and we assume the component group

\Omega\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=G/G^{\circ}

is abelian. Fix an
F
-stable pinning
\mathbf{P}
of
G^{\circ}
, and assume
that the rational pinned-component condition of
Hypothesis
12.1
holds for
G
, and for
each regular
F
-stable Levi subgroup to which the construction is applied.
We use the resulting semidirect-product model

G^{*}=G^{\circ*}\rtimes\Omega
. For

s\in(G^{*})^{\circ F^{*}}=G^{\circ*F^{*}}
semisimple, put

H\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=C_{G^{*}}(s)^{F^{*}}.

We recall the disconnected Lusztig series in the identity-component case:

\mathcal{E}(G,s)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\left\{\chi\in\operatorname{Irr}(G)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\operatorname{Res}^{G}_{G^{\circ}}\chi\text{ has some constituent in }\mathcal{E}(G^{\circ},s)\right\}.

We shall use the following standard consequence of the Mackey formula.

Proposition 13.1

(Mackey criterion for disjoint Harish–Chandra series)
.

Let
G
be a possibly disconnected reductive
\mathbb{F}_{q}
-group. Let

(L,\tau)
and
(M,\lambda)
be cuspidal pairs of
G
, with
L,M

regular
F
-stable Levi subgroups, and let
P,Q
be
F
-stable
parabolic subgroups with Levi factors
L,M
. Assume that the Mackey
formula holds for the Lusztig functors attached to
(L,P)
and

(M,Q)
. Then

\left\langle R^{G}_{L\subset P}(\tau),R^{G}_{M\subset Q}(\lambda)\right\rangle_{G^{F}}=0

unless
(L,\tau)
and
(M,\lambda)
are
G^{F}
-conjugate. Consequently,
the sets

\operatorname{Irr}(G,(L,\tau))

are pairwise disjoint as
(L,\tau)
ranges over
G^{F}
-conjugacy classes
of cuspidal pairs for which the relevant Mackey formula holds.

13.1.
Disconnected Lusztig series are unions of Harish–Chandra series

Proposition 13.2
.

Let
s\in(G^{*})^{\circ F^{*}}
be semisimple.
For a regular
F
-stable Levi subgroup
L\leq G
, choose an
F
-stable
parabolic
P
with Levi decomposition
P=L\ltimes U
. For a cuspidal pair

(L,\tau)
set

\operatorname{Irr}(G,(L,\tau))\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\{\text{irreducible constituents of }R^{G}_{L\subset P}(\tau)\}.

Let
\Sigma_{G}(s)
be the set of
G
-conjugacy classes of cuspidal pairs

(L,\tau)
such that
\tau\in\mathcal{E}(L,s)
. Then

\mathcal{E}(G,s)=\bigcup_{[(L,\tau)]\in\Sigma_{G}(s)}\operatorname{Irr}(G,(L,\tau)).

Moreover, this union is disjoint once the indexing is taken modulo

G
-conjugacy of cuspidal pairs.

Proof.

The proof is the same as in the ordinary disconnected setting, and uses only the
definition of the disconnected Lusztig series by restriction to
G^{\circ}
. If

(L,\tau)
is a cuspidal pair with
\tau\in\mathcal{E}(L,s)
, choose

\tau^{\circ}\prec\operatorname{Res}^{L}_{L^{\circ}}\tau
with

\tau^{\circ}\in\mathcal{E}(L^{\circ},s)
. For any irreducible constituent

\chi
of
R^{G}_{L\subset P}(\tau)
, compatibility of Harish–Chandra
restriction with restriction to identity components gives a constituent

\chi^{\circ}\prec\operatorname{Res}^{G}_{G^{\circ}}\chi
which occurs in

R^{G^{\circ}}_{L^{\circ}\subset P^{\circ}}(\tau^{\circ})
. The connected
Harish–Chandra decomposition of Lusztig series gives

\chi^{\circ}\in\mathcal{E}(G^{\circ},s)
, hence
\chi\in\mathcal{E}(G,s)
.

Conversely, if
\chi\in\mathcal{E}(G,s)
, choose

\chi^{\circ}\prec\operatorname{Res}^{G}_{G^{\circ}}\chi
with

\chi^{\circ}\in\mathcal{E}(G^{\circ},s)
. In the connected group
G^{\circ}
, the
character
\chi^{\circ}
belongs to a Harish–Chandra series attached to some
cuspidal pair
(L^{\circ},\tau^{\circ})
with
\tau^{\circ}\in\mathcal{E}(L^{\circ},s)
.
Extending
L^{\circ}
to the corresponding regular Levi subgroup
L
of

G
, adjointness and the compatibility of Harish–Chandra restriction with
restriction to identity components produce an irreducible constituent

\tau
of
{}^{*}R^{G}_{L\subset P}(\chi)
lying above
\tau^{\circ}
. The same
restriction argument shows that
\tau
is cuspidal; hence

\tau\in\mathcal{E}(L,s)
, and
\chi\in\operatorname{Irr}(G,(L,\tau))
.

For Lusztig functors attached to
F
-stable parabolics in the present
disconnected setting, the Mackey formula holds by

[
8
, Theorem 3.2]
. Proposition
13.1

therefore gives disjointness modulo
G
-conjugacy of cuspidal pairs.
∎

13.2.
Enriched Harish–Chandra labels

Let
L\leq G
be a regular
F
-stable Levi subgroup, and let

\tau\in\mathcal{E}(L,s)_{\operatorname{cusp}}.

Choose an irreducible constituent

\tau^{\circ}\prec\operatorname{Res}^{L}_{L^{\circ}}\tau.

Put

H_{L}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=C_{L^{*}}(s)^{F^{*}},\qquad K_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=C_{(L^{*})^{\circ}}(s)^{F^{*}},

and

u_{L,0}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathbb{J}_{s}^{L^{\circ}}(\tau^{\circ})\in\operatorname{Uch}(K_{0})_{\operatorname{cusp}}.

By Lemma
12.3
, applied to the disconnected group

L
, the source inertia quotient

\Omega_{\tau^{\circ}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=I_{L}(\tau^{\circ})/L^{\circ}

is identified with the corresponding stabilizer quotient of
u_{L,0}
on the
centralizer side. Clifford theory on the source extension

L^{\circ}\lhd I_{L}(\tau^{\circ})

gives a canonical class

[\alpha_{\tau^{\circ}}]\in H^{2}(\Omega_{\tau^{\circ}},\mathbb{C}^{\times}).

Let

E_{\tau}\in\operatorname{Irr}_{[\alpha_{\tau^{\circ}}]}(\Omega_{\tau^{\circ}})

be the Clifford label of
\tau
above
\tau^{\circ}
. We write

(13.1)

\mathfrak{u}^{\mathrm{enh}}_{\tau}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=[u_{L,0},[\alpha_{\tau^{\circ}}],E_{\tau}]\in\operatorname{Uch}^{\mathrm{enh}}_{L,s}(H_{L})_{\operatorname{cusp}}.

Let
W_{\tau^{\circ}}
be the connected relative Weyl group attached to the
cuspidal pair
(L^{\circ},\tau^{\circ})
in
G^{\circ}
. The corner lemma

10.2
, applied with
X=G
,
M=L
, and

\theta^{\circ}=\tau^{\circ}
, shows that
W_{\tau^{\circ}}
acts on the set of
Clifford labels
\operatorname{Irr}_{[\alpha_{\tau^{\circ}}]}(\Omega_{\tau^{\circ}})
. Let

W_{\tau}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\operatorname{Stab}_{W_{\tau^{\circ}}}(E_{\tau}).

The same corner lemma supplies a canonically defined cohomology class

[\beta_{\tau}]\in H^{2}(W_{\tau},\mathbb{C}^{\times})

which measures the possible projectivity of the action of
W_{\tau}
on the
Clifford multiplicity space. After choosing a representative

\beta_{\tau}\in Z^{2}(W_{\tau},\mathbb{C}^{\times})
, the irreducible constituents of
Harish–Chandra induction from
(L,\tau)
are parametrized by

\mathfrak{R}_{\tau}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\operatorname{Irr}\bigl(\mathbb{C}_{\beta_{\tau}}[W_{\tau}]\bigr),

with the usual coboundary indeterminacy in
\beta_{\tau}
. Thus each

\rho\in\operatorname{Irr}(G,(L,\tau))

has a canonical relative Harish–Chandra label

\varphi_{\rho}\in\mathfrak{R}_{\tau}.

When the corner cocycle
\beta_{\tau}
is trivial, this label set is simply

\operatorname{Irr}(W_{\tau})
, which is the untwisted situation used in the ordinary
Howlett–Lehrer formulation.

Definition 13.3

(Full enriched unipotent target)
.

Define

\operatorname{Uch}^{\mathrm{enh}}_{G,s}(H)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\bigsqcup_{[(L,\tau)]\in\Sigma_{G}(s)}\left\{[H_{L},\mathfrak{u}^{\mathrm{enh}}_{\tau},\varphi]\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\varphi\in\mathfrak{R}_{\tau}\right\}.

Here the brackets mean that replacing
(L,\tau,\tau^{\circ})
by a

G
-conjugate replaces
H_{L}
, the enriched cuspidal datum, and the relative
label by the conjugate data.

13.3.
Jordan decomposition for disconnected
G
with rationally pinned abelian component group: enriched bijection part

Theorem 13.4

(Enriched pinned disconnected Jordan decomposition)
.

Let
G
be as above; in particular, assume that the finite component group is
abelian and that the rational pinned-component condition holds. Let

s\in(G^{*})^{\circ F^{*}}
be semisimple and put
H=C_{G^{*}}(s)^{F^{*}}
. There
is a canonical bijection

J^{\mathrm{enh}}_{G,s}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathcal{E}(G,s)\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }\operatorname{Uch}^{\mathrm{enh}}_{G,s}(H),

depending only on the fixed pinning. On the cuspidal part it is the bijection
of Theorem
12.8
. More generally, if

\rho\in\operatorname{Irr}(G,(L,\tau))

for a cuspidal pair
(L,\tau)\in\Sigma_{G}(s)
, then

J^{\mathrm{enh}}_{G,s}(\rho)=[H_{L},\mathfrak{u}^{\mathrm{enh}}_{\tau},\varphi_{\rho}],

where
\mathfrak{u}^{\mathrm{enh}}_{\tau}
is defined by
(
13.1
) and
\varphi_{\rho}
is the relative
Harish–Chandra label of
\rho
.

Proof.

By Proposition
13.2
,

\mathcal{E}(G,s)=\bigsqcup_{[(L,\tau)]\in\Sigma_{G}(s)}\operatorname{Irr}(G,(L,\tau)).

For each summand, the source-side disconnected Howlett–Lehrer analysis recalled
above gives a canonical parametrization

\operatorname{Irr}(G,(L,\tau))\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }\mathfrak{R}_{\tau},\qquad\rho\longmapsto\varphi_{\rho}.

Combining this label with the enriched cuspidal datum

\mathfrak{u}^{\mathrm{enh}}_{\tau}
gives a map

\rho\longmapsto[H_{L},\mathfrak{u}^{\mathrm{enh}}_{\tau},\varphi_{\rho}]

from the Harish–Chandra series
\operatorname{Irr}(G,(L,\tau))
onto the corresponding
summand of
\operatorname{Uch}^{\mathrm{enh}}_{G,s}(H)
. These maps are bijective on each
summand, and the summands are disjoint on both sides. Taking their disjoint
union gives the asserted bijection.

If
\rho
is cuspidal, then the relevant cuspidal pair is
(G,\rho)
, the
relative Weyl group is trivial, and the construction reduces exactly to the
cuspidal enriched bijection of Theorem
12.8
. Conjugating the
choice of
\tau^{\circ}
conjugates the inertia quotient, Clifford class,
Clifford label, and relative Harish–Chandra label compatibly, so the resulting
element of the quotient target is independent of all auxiliary choices.
∎

Remark 13.5

(Recovery of an ordinary disconnected target)
.

The theorem above does not assert a bijection

\mathcal{E}(G,s)\longrightarrow\operatorname{Uch}(C_{G^{*}}(s)^{F^{*}})

under Hypothesis
12.1
alone. Such an
ordinary target is recovered only after adding the extra assertion that, for
every relevant cuspidal constituent
\rho^{\circ}
, the transported source
Clifford class
[\alpha_{\rho^{\circ}}]
agrees with the ordinary Clifford class
of the unipotent centralizer side, and that the corresponding relative
Howlett–Lehrer corner labels are identified. Under that additional condition,
the enriched datum
[u_{0},[\alpha_{\rho^{\circ}}],E]
is realized by the usual
irreducible unipotent character of
C_{G^{*}}(s)^{F^{*}}
with the same Clifford
label, and
J^{\mathrm{enh}}_{G,s}
descends to the ordinary disconnected
pinned Jordan decomposition.

13.4.
Conditional ordinary packet sums

The preceding construction deliberately keeps the source Clifford class as part
of the target. Therefore the regular packet-sum statements that would be phrased
in terms of ordinary irreducible unipotent characters of

C_{G^{*}}(s)^{F^{*}}
require the additional ordinary-recovery hypothesis of
Remark
13.5
. Under that extra hypothesis, the
standard Clifford regular-sum argument applies: summing over all projective
labels in a fixed Clifford packet gives the induced character from the identity
component, and hence the sum depends only on the underlying orbit-valued Jordan
decomposition. Without that extra hypothesis, the correct invariant object is
the enriched packet

\{[u_{0},[\alpha_{\rho^{\circ}}],E]\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}E\in\operatorname{Irr}_{[\alpha_{\rho^{\circ}}]}(\Omega_{\rho^{\circ}})\},

not an ordinary packet inside
\operatorname{Uch}(C_{G^{*}}(s)^{F^{*}})
.

13.5.
Stable finite regular sums

The enriched disconnected Jordan decomposition records more information than the
orbit-valued Jordan decomposition: it remembers the source Clifford class and
the projective Clifford label. Stability, however, is insensitive to the
individual projective label. The stable finite objects used in the local
application are obtained by summing regularly over the Clifford packet. We
record this compatibility in a form which does not require an ordinary
unipotent-character target for the disconnected centralizer.

Lemma 13.6

(Independence of the Clifford-regular sum)
.

Let
N\lhd M
be finite groups, and let
\theta\in\operatorname{Irr}(N)
. Put

I_{M}(\theta)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\{m\in M\mid{}^{m}\theta\simeq\theta\},\qquad\Omega_{\theta}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=I_{M}(\theta)/N.

Choose a projective extension
\widetilde{\theta}
of
\theta
to

I_{M}(\theta)
, and let

\alpha_{\theta}\in Z^{2}(\Omega_{\theta},\mathbb{C}^{\times})

be the Clifford-label cocycle in the convention of
§
10.1
. For

E\in\operatorname{Irr}(\mathbb{C}_{\alpha_{\theta}}[\Omega_{\theta}]),

let

\chi_{\theta,E}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\operatorname{Ind}_{I_{M}(\theta)}^{M}\bigl(\widetilde{\theta}\otimes\operatorname{Inf}_{\Omega_{\theta}}^{I_{M}(\theta)}E\bigr)\in\operatorname{Irr}(M\mid\theta).

Then

\sum_{E\in\operatorname{Irr}(\mathbb{C}_{\alpha_{\theta}}[\Omega_{\theta}])}\operatorname{dim}(E)\,\chi_{\theta,E}=\operatorname{Ind}_{N}^{M}\theta

as characters of
M
. In particular, this regular sum is independent of the
chosen projective extension, of the cocycle representative, and of the Clifford
parametrization. It depends only on the
M
-orbit of
\theta
.

Proof.

Let
\Reg_{\alpha_{\theta}}
be the left regular module of the twisted group
algebra
\mathbb{C}_{\alpha_{\theta}}[\Omega_{\theta}]
. Since this algebra is semisimple,
its regular character decomposes as

\Reg_{\alpha_{\theta}}=\sum_{E\in\operatorname{Irr}(\mathbb{C}_{\alpha_{\theta}}[\Omega_{\theta}])}\operatorname{dim}(E)\,E.

Therefore

\sum_{E}\operatorname{dim}(E)\bigl(\widetilde{\theta}\otimes\operatorname{Inf}_{\Omega_{\theta}}^{I_{M}(\theta)}E\bigr)

is the character of

\widetilde{\theta}\otimes\operatorname{Inf}_{\Omega_{\theta}}^{I_{M}(\theta)}\Reg_{\alpha_{\theta}}.

If
i\in I_{M}(\theta)
maps to a nontrivial element of
\Omega_{\theta}
, then
left multiplication by its image on the regular module has trace zero. If

i=n\in N
, then the image of
i
in
\Omega_{\theta}
is trivial and the
trace on the regular module is
|\Omega_{\theta}|
. Since

\widetilde{\theta}|_{N}=\theta
, the above character has value zero on

I_{M}(\theta)\setminus N
and value

|\Omega_{\theta}|\,\theta(n)

on
n\in N
. These are precisely the values of

\operatorname{Ind}_{N}^{I_{M}(\theta)}\theta
, because
\theta
is

I_{M}(\theta)
-stable. Hence

\sum_{E}\operatorname{dim}(E)\bigl(\widetilde{\theta}\otimes\operatorname{Inf}_{\Omega_{\theta}}^{I_{M}(\theta)}E\bigr)=\operatorname{Ind}_{N}^{I_{M}(\theta)}\theta.

Inducing from
I_{M}(\theta)
to
M
and using transitivity of induction gives

\sum_{E}\operatorname{dim}(E)\,\chi_{\theta,E}=\operatorname{Ind}_{N}^{M}\theta.

The right-hand side is intrinsic. Replacing
\theta
by an
M
-conjugate
also leaves
\operatorname{Ind}_{N}^{M}\theta
unchanged. This proves the asserted
independence.
∎

Theorem 13.7

(Stable finite regular sums and enriched pinned Jordan decomposition)
.

Keep the notation of Theorem
13.4
. Thus
G
is a
possibly disconnected finite reductive group satisfying the rational
pinned-component hypothesis,
G^{\circ}\lhd G
, and

s\in(G^{*})^{\circ F^{*}}.

Put

H=C_{G^{*}}(s)^{F^{*}},\qquad H_{0}=C_{(G^{*})^{\circ}}(s)^{F^{*}}.

Let

J^{\mathrm{enh}}_{G,s}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathcal{E}(G,s)\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }\operatorname{Uch}^{\mathrm{enh}}_{G,s}(H)

be the enriched pinned Jordan decomposition, and let

J^{\mathrm{enh},\operatorname{cusp}}_{G,s}
denote its cuspidal restriction from
Theorem
12.8
. Let

O\subset\operatorname{Uch}(H_{0})_{\operatorname{cusp}}

be an
H/H_{0}
-orbit representing a stable connected finite unipotent datum,
and choose
u_{0}\in O
. Let

\rho^{\circ}=(\mathbb{J}_{s}^{G^{\circ}})^{-1}(u_{0})\in\mathcal{E}(G^{\circ},s)_{\operatorname{cusp}}.

For

E\in\operatorname{Irr}_{[\alpha_{\rho^{\circ}}]}(\Omega_{\rho^{\circ}}),

let
\rho_{E}\in\mathcal{E}(G,s)_{\operatorname{cusp}}
be the unique character satisfying

J^{\mathrm{enh},\operatorname{cusp}}_{G,s}(\rho_{E})=[u_{0},[\alpha_{\rho^{\circ}}],E].

Then the Clifford-regularized finite sum

\Theta^{\mathrm{enh}}_{s,O}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\sum_{E\in\operatorname{Irr}_{[\alpha_{\rho^{\circ}}]}(\Omega_{\rho^{\circ}})}\operatorname{dim}(E)\,\rho_{E}

satisfies

\Theta^{\mathrm{enh}}_{s,O}=\operatorname{Ind}_{G^{\circ}}^{G}\rho^{\circ}.

Consequently
\Theta^{\mathrm{enh}}_{s,O}
is independent of the representative

u_{0}\in O
, of the projective extension used to define the Clifford class, of
the cocycle representative, and of the pinned refinement. It depends only on
the orbit-valued connected Jordan datum
O
.

More generally, if a connected finite stable packet coefficient is a finite
linear combination of such orbit data, then its enriched Clifford-regularization
is obtained by applying the preceding construction orbit by orbit. Hence the
enriched regularized coefficient has the same finite stable-transport invariance
as the connected finite stable coefficient.

Proof.

By the construction of the cuspidal enriched bijection in
Theorem
12.8
, the characters of
G
lying above

\rho^{\circ}
are precisely the characters
\rho_{E}
obtained by Clifford
theory for the normal inclusion

G^{\circ}\lhd G

with projective label

E\in\operatorname{Irr}_{[\alpha_{\rho^{\circ}}]}(\Omega_{\rho^{\circ}}).

Applying Lemma
13.6
with

N=G^{\circ},\qquad M=G,\qquad\theta=\rho^{\circ}

gives

\sum_{E}\operatorname{dim}(E)\,\rho_{E}=\operatorname{Ind}_{G^{\circ}}^{G}\rho^{\circ}.

This proves the displayed identity and shows at once that the sum is independent
of all choices entering the Clifford parametrization.

If
u_{1}={}^{h}u_{0}
is another representative of the
H/H_{0}
-orbit
O
, then
Lemma
12.3
and the equivariance of the connected
Jordan decomposition identify

(\mathbb{J}_{s}^{G^{\circ}})^{-1}(u_{1})

with a
G
-conjugate of
\rho^{\circ}
. The induced character

\operatorname{Ind}_{G^{\circ}}^{G}\rho^{\circ}
is unchanged by replacing
\rho^{\circ}
by a

G
-conjugate. Therefore
\Theta^{\mathrm{enh}}_{s,O}
depends only on the
orbit
O
, not on its representative. Since the right-hand side only sees the
orbit-valued connected Jordan datum, the regularized sum is also independent of
the additional pinned refinement which selects the individual enriched labels.

Finally, Lusztig’s finite character-sheaf theory identifies the connected
orbit-valued packet coefficients attached to stable finite unipotent data with
stable almost characters; see
[
17
, Ch. 13]
,
[
19
]
, and, for
the disconnected-centre form of the Jordan decomposition,
[
20
,
7
]
.
The preceding paragraphs show that the enriched disconnected coefficient is the
Clifford regularization of this connected stable coefficient. Clifford
